# KB CORE — Hunter Allen, Andrew Coggan, PhD, Stephen McGregor, PhD — Training and Racing with a Power Meter (2019)

Companion file: `allen-coggan-mcgregor-training-racing-power-meter_biblioteca.md` (full plans, workouts, exercises, recipes, templates). IDs like `TRPM-L2-004` point there.

## C01 Overview & Scope

### [TRPM-C01-001] Overview & Scope

**What the book is.** *Training and Racing with a Power Meter* (3rd ed., 2019, VeloPress; Hunter Allen, Andrew Coggan PhD, Stephen McGregor PhD) is a practical, power-based method for training, testing, pacing and racing in cycling and triathlon. It is explicitly "not a training manual" and not an exercise-physiology text: it teaches how to use watts to individualize training and analyze performance.

**For whom.** Cyclists at every level (beginner to elite, masters included), coaches, and triathletes. Also track, cyclocross and ultra-endurance MTB riders. Run and swim workouts are included for triathletes.

**Problem solved.** Heart rate, perceived exertion and volume (hours/km) are noisy proxies. The power meter measures the true rate of work, allowing: objective self-assessment, quantified fitness tracking, individualized intensity levels, weakness diagnosis, correct pacing, load management (TSS, CTL/ATL/TSB) and timing of peak form.

**Central thesis.** Anchor everything to Functional Threshold Power (FTP), then: (1) set training levels from it, (2) find strengths and weaknesses with the Power Profile and Power Duration Curve, (3) build workouts and plans targeting limiters, (4) interpret data with NP, IF, TSS, Quadrant Analysis, (5) manage load and peak with the Performance Manager, (6) pace races by watts. The tool only works if the athlete is willing to change and do the work.

**Organization.** Ch1 why power; Ch2 hardware and data practices; Ch3 FTP and levels; Ch4 Power Profile; Ch5 workouts and interval stop rule; Ch6 data interpretation; Ch7 NP/IF/TSS/Quadrant/bilateral; Ch8 Power Duration model; Ch9 Performance Manager; Ch10 four case-study plans; Ch11 tracking fitness; Ch12 triathlon; Ch13 racing/pacing/aero; Ch14 cyclocross, track, ultra MTB; Epilogue 8 steps; Appendix workout library (cycling, swim, run).

**Not covered.** Detailed physiology and periodization theory; product reviews (stale quickly; see DC Rainmaker); software walkthroughs; nutrition beyond kJ≈kcal and fueling hints; strength training (judged of limited value for cyclists); a one-size-fits-all plan.

### [TRPM-C01-002] Scope and purpose of Training and Racing with a Power Meter

- The book teaches cyclists (and triathletes) at every ability level to train and race with a power meter. Goal: demystify the power meter so riders can use it for peak performance in training and racing.
- Audience: any cyclist interested in improving performance, whether they already own a power meter or are considering buying one. No PhD in exercise physiology is needed to understand the data; one does not have to be an elite racer to benefit.
- Self-declared limits: it is NOT a training manual. It does not explain the nuances of peaking or go into the details of exercise physiology (other books cover these).
- Rationale for using a power meter: it can uncover hidden areas of weakness that a heart rate monitor or simple cyclometer would never reveal. It records a second-by-second diary of the ride, which can be downloaded and analyzed.
- Uses named: track improvements over any period (compare this week's hill repeats to last week's; best 20-minute effort this year vs. two years ago; change in average cadence over three years). Authors claim what is learned can be the difference between a mediocre and a successful season.
- Any athlete benefits from being challenged to think critically about training and from understanding the essential components of peak performance.

### [TRPM-C01-003] Book structure: chapter roadmap

- Ch 1: how using a power meter impacts training.
- Ch 2: how the equipment works and how different software interprets data.
- Ch 3: finding functional threshold power (FTP) and individualizing training levels.
- Ch 4: identifying strengths and weaknesses.
- Ch 5: sample workouts based on wattage (time trials, hill climbs, intervals, etc.) targeting the power-based training levels.
- Ch 6: interpreting data from rides, with sample graphs.
- Ch 7: Normalized Power, Intensity Factor, Training Stress Score.
- Ch 8: advances since the previous edition, e.g., functional reserve capacity and stamina.
- Ch 9: using data to create and time peak fitness.
- Ch 10: four case studies, each with a full training plan usable or adaptable; shows use of the Appendix workout menu, developing a Power Profile and building fatigue resistance where most needed.
- Ch 11: longer-term meaning of data (track long-term changes, compare races year to year).
- Ch 12: triathletes: pacing, training for long- and short-distance events, racing advice.
- Ch 13: using the power meter to reach peak racing performance.
- Ch 14: other disciplines: cyclocross, track, ultra-endurance.
- Epilogue: summary of steps to put it all together.
- Appendix: more than 100 sample workouts sorted by training level; a starting point, after which athletes will build their own workouts from their Power Profile and Power Duration Curve.
- Reference aids: Abbreviations (pp. xv-xvi) and Glossary (p. 351).

### [TRPM-C01-004] Why train with power: central thesis of Chapter 1

The power meter lets a cyclist quantitatively track fitness changes, define weaknesses and refocus training on them. The data are the impetus for change.
- Benefits accrue only when the athlete knows how to interpret the data, implement wattage-based workouts, and adjust training. Otherwise the meter is just a pricey upgrade.
- Four overlapping areas of advantage:
  - Self-Assessment: identify strengths and weaknesses.
  - Collaboration: share detailed information with coach and teammates.
  - Focused Training: better training goals and methods.
  - Peak Performance: accurate information, collaboration and smarter training put the athlete in position to do their best.
- Without the data, analysis, communication and planning involve guesswork. Once the data are the baseline, all these areas move to a new level.
- Even experienced riders benefit. Example: a masters 60+ rider with 30+ years of training used the meter to focus interval training, breakaway pacing and time-trial pacing, and saw his progress in quantitative form for the first time.
- Warning to old-timers: if you are unwilling to change the way you train, a power meter might not be for you. It takes time and effort.
- Closing: the meter will not do the work. Speed eventually requires pushing harder on the pedals. It is only worth doing if you are willing to work at it, and you must be willing to change your thinking first.
- Top performances in recent years (Tour de France stages, hour records, track, HPV, mountain-bike and BMX) have been aided by power meters.

### [TRPM-C01-005] Chapter 4 purpose: objective Power Profile of strengths and weaknesses

Chapter 4 teaches how to build an objective Power Profile from power-meter data (races, training rides, tests) instead of subjective guesses ("I am a bad climber", "I have no sprint"). Such guesses are often not on target. The authors began collecting data in 2002 to see what power different rider types (elite pros, masters, beginners) could produce; the Power Profile chart (Table 4.1) came from these datasets. Originally meant to check whether athletes were on track, it turned out to be one of the most effective ways to quantify each rider's relative strengths and weaknesses and physiological systems.

### [TRPM-C01-006] How the Power Profile standards were built (method behind the data)

- Large self-reported databases are unreliable; deriving power from time-trial performance requires tenuous assumptions (body mass, aerodynamic drag) and is complex for short non-steady-state events (e.g., 1 km track).
- Instead the authors anchored upper and lower ends of each range using known abilities of world champions and novices. Example: a world-class match sprinter produces over 23 W/kg in 5 s; a novice only 10 to 12.5 W/kg.
- Six intermediate divisions: exceptional, excellent, very good, good, moderate, fair. Their own athlete data confirmed the figures.
- Advantage: validity of comparison across durations; a "world-class" output should be equivalent whether measured over 5 seconds or 1 hour.
- Tables were revised as more data from the world's best riders arrived.

### [TRPM-C01-007] Ch 9 Overview: Using Power to Manage Performance

Chapter 9 presents the Performance Manager (PMC), the authors' practical way of using power data to create "form" on a chosen day. Form = Fitness + Freshness. The challenge is not just being faster but being fitter and faster at the exact time wanted. The chapter covers: Banister's impulse-response model and its limitations; how the Performance Manager (CTL, ATL, TSB) simplifies it; peaking guidelines; two case studies; and practical application hints.

### [TRPM-C01-008] Ch 10 overview: building a power-based training plan

Chapter 10 shows how to integrate wattage into a training plan rather than teaching training theory. Start from the known items: weekly training-time constraints, strengths and weaknesses (from Power Profile, Power Duration Curve, Quadrant Analysis) and season goals. From these fill in the blanks: how hard to train, which roads, sprints vs endurance vs hill climbs. Four case studies: Bob Rider (42, masters, 16-week threshold plan for a two-day stage race), Jill Racer (32, 8-week peak plan for state championship criterium), Joe TriGuy (38, age-group triathlete, 12-week 70.3 plan), Bill Masters (58, 12-week gran fondo plan with a 45-min climb). Workout codes refer to the Appendix menu.

### [TRPM-C01-009] Ch 11 overview: tracking fitness changes with a power meter

Before the power meter, cycling fitness was hard to track: race placings and rides with training partners are subjective, and results depend on tactics, luck and conditions (even a time trial varies with wind). With a power meter, changes are quantitative (e.g. peak 5-minute or peak 60-minute power), motivating when rising and discouraging when falling. The authors still value knowing precisely how much fitness has declined, so training can be changed to get back on track. Key charts to understand and use daily: Mean Maximal Power (MMP) periodic chart, MMP curve, training-level distribution, cadence charts, overlays of the same ride/race, and PD history metrics. Examples use TrainingPeaks WKO software. Closing motto: "Ride, collect data, do nothing extra" (a minimalist approach).

### [TRPM-C01-010] Epilogue: purpose and the eight-step workflow overview

The epilogue closes the book by summarizing how to put power-meter use together. Goal: make the reader a savvy power-meter user, more competitive in training and racing, asking questions that lead to breakthroughs (example question: "my Power Profile says I am a sprinter and I can do 4.55 watts per kilogram at FTP, so how do I increase my VO2max power?").
The method is organized as eight steps (chapter pointers in the book):
1. Collect data and determine threshold power (Ch 3)
2. Determine training levels (Ch 3)
3. Determine strengths and weaknesses (Ch 4)
4. Create workouts and begin training (Ch 5)
5. Interpret your data (Ch 6)
6. Use the data analysis tools (Ch 7, 9, 10)
7. Race with your power meter (Ch 12, 13, 14)
8. Make changes to achieve your goals

## C02 Philosophy & Core Principles

### [TRPM-C02-001] Core principles (summary)

1. **Power is the true measure of work; heart rate is a response.** HR is the "intensity of your intention" and is affected by hydration, heat, sleep, stress, fatigue (e.g. 165 bpm at 280 W may become 158 bpm after 7 hard days). Wattage tells you when you truly need rest. Use both, but power decides dose.
2. **FTP is the anchor.** Lactate threshold expressed as power is the main determinant of endurance performance, because it integrates VO2max, fraction of VO2max sustained, and efficiency. Levels, IF and TSS all derive from it, so it must be tested and kept current (every 6–8 weeks, standardized conditions).
3. **Relative, not absolute, comparison.** The Power Profile compares you with yourself across durations (neuromuscular, anaerobic, VO2max, threshold) to find limiters; train weaknesses unless a weakness is irrelevant to your event (Joe TriGuy skips sprinting).
4. **Specificity.** Train the demands of the event: use Quadrant Analysis, VI, matches, race files. Heavy weights and big-gear strength-endurance are judged of little value because strength rarely limits cycling power.
5. **Least necessary work, optimally dosed.** "Do the least amount of the most specific training that brings continual improvement" (Friel). The interval stop rule (Table 5.1) quantifies it. Track overload and rest: ATL drives CTL, and rest is when adaptation shows.
6. **Average power misleads.** Use NP, VI, IF, TSS; stochastic power means use ranges, not exact targets.
7. **Form = fitness + freshness.** Short anaerobic events need more freshness; long aerobic events need more fitness; a rising TSB matters more than a positive one.
8. **Pacing is the most important race skill.** RPE is unreliable early in races; the meter is the ceiling. Don't start too hard.
9. **Levels are continuous; don't be a slave to numbers.** Art of coaching and specificity remain; models are snapshots.
10. **Testing is training; collect every file; hard work is non-negotiable.** "Training is testing; testing is training." The meter does not do the work.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:principles
src: Ch 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 13; Epilogue

### [TRPM-C02-002] Why power meters have become essential (context)

- First power meters (ergometers) appeared in the late 1800s: essentially stationary bikes that let researchers determine power; not practical for everyday use or portable.
- Only in recent decades have technological advances made power meters portable, cheaper and accessible to cyclists at all levels. Once a secret of top coaches and a few elite athletes, they are now considered essential equipment.
- Power data is now a focus of race coverage and social media sharing (e.g., power-to-weight ratio), which brought power to the masses.
- Authors' view: with power meters and software more accessible in price and practice, there is never a better time to incorporate power into training.
- Authors' attitude (McGregor): "When it's all said and done, the science doesn't matter if it doesn't help you go faster!"
tags: type:philosophy sport:cycling level:all subtopic:history subtopic:practicality
src: Introduction
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C02-003] Power versus heart rate: what each measures

- Power (watts) is the true rate of work: how hard you push on the pedals. Watts drive the bike forward. The meter shows the exact training dose.
- Heart rate is the body's response to a stimulus, not a measure of improvement. Hunter calls it the "intensity of your intention", a readout of how hard you are trying. The analogy is a car tachometer (rpm) versus horsepower.
- Heart rate is affected by hydration, environment, body temperature, sleep, stress and more, most of which have little to do with performance. Relying on it alone can lead to false conclusions about fitness and undermine confidence.
- Sometimes it is better to ride on perceived exertion without knowing heart rate.
- The heart is a muscle and gets tired. After about 7 days of hard training, heart rate may be lower than normal at a given wattage.
  - Example: 165 bpm at 280 W when fresh becomes 158 bpm at 280 W when fatigued.
  - Heart rate alone might suggest backing off, yet the rider can probably still produce the same or nearly the same watts.
- Comparing heart rate with power shows days when heart rate says slow down but power says the muscles are not working hard enough for a training stimulus.
- Principle: "Wattage is the key to knowing when you truly need a rest day."
tags: type:principle sport:cycling metric:power,hr subtopic:power-vs-heart-rate level:all
src: Ch 1 "Add Meaning to Heart Rate Monitoring"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C02-004] Why train with a power meter: knowing yourself as a cyclist

- The power meter gives a second-by-second record (diary) of each ride in graph format. It is more than a gadget: it is one of the best ways to get to know yourself as a cyclist.
- The authors say that installing the meter is easy, but using the hardware and especially the software as intended takes work. Learning all its features enhances the experience, like taking lessons for a Ferrari.
- Before analyzing data, get a feel for what wattage means in the real world. Observe power in crosswinds, on long climbs, on short hard hills, and before and after a stop.
- Look at how a given power (e.g., 300 W) affects heart rate, cadence and speed.
- Note how hard or easy a given power feels, both briefly and when sustained.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:getting-started sport:cycling metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 intro | p. [not marked]

### [TRPM-C02-005] Rationale for seven levels and their arbitrary nature

- Defining levels is arbitrary because physiological responses form a continuum, with one intensity domain blending into the next.
- Compromise: more levels reflect the continuum better; fewer are simpler. Seven was the minimum needed to represent the full range of physiological responses and the types of training needed for competitive cycling.
- Levels were developed by Andrew Coggan from exercise physiology principles and his laboratory and field experience with power.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:coggan-levels sport:cycling metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Coggan's Classic Training Levels

### [TRPM-C02-006] Relative, not category-based, comparison: why Power Profiling

- Earlier benchmarks were based on racer category (Cat. I, II...). The authors consider them of limited practical use: the best measure of competitive ability relative to others is actual race performance, not power output.
- Power Profiling instead uses standards across durations representing different physiological abilities. The aim is to see how you do in one area compared with your other areas, not versus other people. This is the true value of Power Profiling.
- Knowing relative strengths and weaknesses lets you (1) build a program to improve weak areas ("the start of real progress"), and (2) identify events where you can expect greatest success and build on strengths.
- Examples of what the profile shows: strong anaerobic capacity relative to lactate threshold; neuromuscular talent but cardiovascular challenge.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:power-profile subtopic:relative-strengths
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "The Power Profile" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C02-007] Stochastic nature of cycling and ranges instead of exact targets

- Stochastic: a process that appears random but has patterns analyzable statistically. Road power varies (500 W, then 0 W, then 220 W) because of terrain, wind and riders around.
- Holding a narrow power range is possible only on very flat roads with little wind, or on an indoor trainer.
- Do not be discouraged if you cannot hold a perfect wattage (e.g., 300 W in a Lactate Threshold interval). Aim for a range, avoiding dipping too low or overdoing the maximum. Example: at least 300 W but not over 320 W at 90 rpm.
- Pacing is what allows the interval to be completed correctly.
- Do not become a slave to the numbers; keep the big picture and the philosophy of each workout.
tags: type:philosophy type:principle sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:pacing subtopic:stochastic-power
src: Ch 5 "The Stochastic Nature of Cycling"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C02-008] Why interpret every file: data as the key to fitness and training adaptation

- Interpretation of the data is the key to understanding current fitness, how it has changed over time, and how to adapt training. Every chart and graph has meaning, as every ride (and rest day) has meaning in the program.
- Power meter summary: max and average watts, heart rate, cadence, kilojoules; significant fluctuations (matches); cadence especially over high power; relationship between total work and time at varying intensity.
- Race analysis: identify significant race segments and their exact power demands, then compare with training data. Determine the power needed to stay with the front group at the winning split. When dropped, check the power, and whether cadence was too low or pedaling became sloppy with fatigue (torque too high relative to power). Compare with other races or similar rides.
- Not every file needs a full markup, but review each ride and make notes on how it fits the big picture of fitness and racing goals.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:data-interpretation sport:cycling metric:power
src: Ch 6 intro and closing
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C02-009] Why average power and total work are inadequate

- Power outdoors is variable (elevation changes, wind gusts), so training with power is not directly comparable to heart rate training. Keeping power constantly within a zone during a session is very difficult and often counterproductive.
- Average power for a ride is a poor indicator of intensity. Example: two 20-min intervals at FTP averaging 300 W, with easy riding before and after, can give a ride average of only 180 W. That equals the average of a 2-hour recovery ride at 180 W, yet the physiological systems used and the training stress are entirely different.
- In racing the problem is larger: attacks and conserving energy skew the averages. A hardest-race-of-the-year of 4 h may show only 200 W average.
- Total work (kJ) was rejected as a load measure because it mixes volume and intensity and ignores the nonlinear relation between physiological response and intensity. Example: 2,000 kJ in 3.5 h = 159 W average (a garden-variety Level 2 Endurance ride, little next-day fatigue). 2,000 kJ in 2 h = 278 W average (Level 3 or 4 for most; tired for one or more days).
- Motivation (2002): replace arbitrary hours-by-category planning with a per-ride score accounting for duration and intensity, to correlate past data with success and predict peak fitness (the "holy grail of periodization"). Inspired by studies modelling performance with Banister's heart-rate-based TRIMP.
- Tools developed: Normalized Power (NP), Intensity Factor (IF), Training Stress Score (TSS), plus Quadrant Analysis.
tags: type:philosophy sport:cycling metric:power metric:load level:all subtopic:average-power-limitations subtopic:total-work
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Beyond Average Power" | opening

### [TRPM-C02-010] Why move from discrete Power Profile time frames to the Power Duration model

- FTP is the basis for training levels and for IF and TSS. Power Profiling (Ch 4) characterizes the rider's phenotype and relative strengths and weaknesses more completely than FTP alone. Two athletes can have identical FTPs and excel at different things (all-rounder, time trialist, sprinter).
- The Profile time frames are the functional expression of underlying physiology: neuromuscular power, anaerobic capacity, VO2max, functional threshold.
- Power meters allow data collection in ecologically valid conditions (training, races, group rides). The best data usually come from races, because competitive pressure elicits truly maximal efforts that are hard to get in training. Hence the book's emphasis on both training and racing with a power meter.
- Limitation 1: field efforts do not fall in exact discrete durations. Example: a criterium prime effort of 50 s is maximal but misses the 60-s Anaerobic Capacity criterion, so 1-min mean maximal power under-represents anaerobic capacity.
- Limitation 2: the discrete-system assumption is wrong. Even a 5-s effort uses some glycogen and a very small aerobic contribution, and a maximal 1-min effort gets about 50 percent of its energy from aerobic metabolism. No effort relies on a single physiological system. In-between durations (e.g. 3 min, important for pursuiters) are also informative.
- Limitation 3: planning and pacing. The power-duration relationship is non-linear, so simple interpolation between available durations is nearly impossible (example: Sue Hillclimber needs a 13-min power but has only efforts under 5 min or over 20 min).
- Coach use case: with two years of files, existing data beat formal testing, because the athlete may be out of shape at the time of testing.
- Solution: a model that captures the Profile information without requiring exact discrete efforts.
tags: type:philosophy type:principle sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:power-profile-limitations subtopic:ecological-validity
src: Ch 8 "Shifting to the Power Duration Model" | intro
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C02-011] Managing fitness and fatigue: budget analogy and balance of ATL and CTL

- Adaptation time to training can range from 3–6 weeks to 6 months or 2 years; years of training (kilometers in legs) allow more load and speed. Current fitness depends on training a week, a month, 6 months and years ago; CTL reflects mainly the past three months.
- If you rest too much, you lose fitness (CTL falls); if you train before properly rested, you do not get the greatest benefit.
- Budget analogy: income = capacity, fixed expenses = baseline obligations, variable purchase = extra hard training. Training too hard in a block = overspending and may need months to recover; too much rest = not spending where needed; fitness deteriorates, you may come on form too soon or lose watts at FTP.
- Accumulated ATL forces rest after hard weeks and builds CTL; rest periods let the body adapt to ATL. Managing the two is among the toughest things to master but has more impact on fitness than any other training aspect.
- Friel: "An athlete should do the least amount of the most specific training that brings continual improvement." (e.g., if you can win with 10 VO2max intervals, why do 12 or 15.)
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:ctl-atl-balance
src: Ch 9 "Managing Fitness and Fatigue"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C02-012] Pacing is the crux of triathlon

- Triathlon is about metering energy over the whole event so the athlete finishes strong on the run. Riding too fast on the bike hurts the run, but holding back too much does not always give a faster run.
- A power meter suits triathlon even better than road racing. Most triathlons have no complicated tactics, so pacing matters more, and most are held on the same course every year, so pacing can be planned and tested in advance.
- All earlier power concepts still apply: 20-minute FTP test, Power Profile, Power Duration Curve, power training zones, file analysis.
- Pacing depends on race length: a sprint is trained and raced very differently from an Ironman.
- Pacing is a skill learned by trial and error. The power meter gives an objective real-time view and speeds up learning.
tags: type:philosophy sport:triathlon metric:power level:all subtopic:pacing
src: Ch 12 "A Powerful Triathlete" | intro
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C02-013] Racing with a power meter: purpose and ride story

- Races are won by whoever finishes first, not by whoever produces the most watts per kilogram.
- Three uses of a power meter in racing: (1) pacing during demanding parts of the race (breakaway: pull or sit on, and how hard to pull); (2) defining the physiological demands of the event (riding in the peloton, escaping it, winning); (3) post-race review to prepare for the next race and next season.
- Race power data are some of the most valuable data collected. They also let the athlete communicate better with a coach.
- Power data are incomplete without the story behind each ride. After each race, spend a couple of minutes writing the story in the ride diary: key points, areas to review, and important moments of the race.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:race-analysis subtopic:ride-diary
src: Ch 13 intro | p. [n/a]
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C02-014] Pacing: the overlooked skill

- Pacing is one of the most overlooked areas for improvement. It is a learned skill; only a minority do it naturally. Without a dialed-in pacing strategy and smooth power metering, equipment such as an aero helmet does no good.
- Many races, not just time trials, are lost through poor pacing. Examples: the group ride where the strong rider hammers 40 miles and fades in the last 20; the century newbie who goes hard for 40-50 miles, then crawls at 10 mph with rest stops; the Tour de France, where early-stage attackers rarely finish in the top 10 and the winner never goes off the front early.
- Pacing matters for short events too. The author argues it may be even more critical in short events: in a track pursuit, starting too hard and spending the energy by lap 2 makes power drop off and the time suffer. Track racers should spend the most time of all learning pacing.
- Triathlon: overdoing the bike leads to walking the run. Even 10 watts (240 instead of 250 W over an Ironman distance) can be the difference between a steady run and a walk.
tags: type:principle sport:general level:all subtopic:pacing
src: Ch 13 "Pacing: The Skill We Never Talk About"
evidence: author-experience

## C03 Integral Model

### [TRPM-C03-001] Integral Model

**1. Chain of reasoning (beliefs → mechanisms → method → decisions)**
- Belief: training is dose → adaptation; the dose must be quantified objectively and individualized. Heart rate and volume are poor dose measures.
- Mechanism: lactate threshold (as power, FTP) governs the fraction of VO2max usable and fuel mix/fatigue; higher intensities recruit more fast-twitch fibers and use more glycogen (see "Fiber recruitment and threshold as recruitment point"); physiology responds nonlinearly and with lags, hence NP (30-s rolling average, 4th power).
- Method: FTP → Levels → Power Profile/PDC → workouts → data interpretation → load (TSS) → Performance Manager → race pacing.
- Decisions: which level, how many intervals, when to rest, how to pace, when to retest, how to taper.

**2. Key variables and priority order**
1. Accurate, current FTP (all %s depend on it; a 4% FTP error gives ~8% TSS error).
2. Intensity level relative to FTP (Table 3.1: L1 <55, L2 56–75, L3 76–90, L4 91–105, L5 106–120, L6 121–150, L7 neuromuscular; Sweet Spot 88–94/88–93).
3. Interval dose: duration, number (Table 5.1 stop rule), rest.
4. Weekly structure and load: TSS, CTL ramp 3–7 TSS/day/week; rest weeks.
5. How watts are made: cadence/force (Quadrant Analysis), VI.
6. Freshness at the event (TSB), by event duration.

**3. How the tools fit**
- FTP test (20-min, minus 5%; see "TEST Functional Threshold Power") gives FTP; triangulate with distribution charts, NP from ~1 h races, IF>1.05, mFTP.
- Classic levels first; iLevels (nine, from PD model) when 5-min/short power does not fit percentages.
- Power Profile test (Table 4.1) → phenotype; PDC → dips and fatigue-resistance gaps; retest/revise every 4–6 weeks.
- Workouts (L2 library) chosen by limiter; base = L2 + Sweet Spot (6–8 sessions) before threshold; L5–L7 sharpen; race-winning, crisscross, micro-burst workouts replicate race demands.
- Files interpreted via distribution charts, MMP curve, interval shape, matches, scatterplots.
- NP/IF/TSS quantify load (Tables 7.1–7.4); Performance Manager (CTL 42 d, ATL 7 d, TSB=CTL−ATL; Tables 9.1, 9.2) plans ramp and taper.
- Race: pacing by watts (Table 12.1 for triathlon; TSS budget; FTP isopower TT; hill percentages), post-race review.

**4. Decision flow for a new athlete**
1. Check hardware/data practices: zero offset, 1-s recording, 3–5 s display, remove spikes ("Power meter setup and data cleaning").
2. Ride and look at data; estimate FTP (six ways) then do the FTP test ("TEST Functional Threshold Power").
3. Build levels (Table 3.4 method) and decide Classic vs iLevels.
4. Do the Power Profile test ("TEST Power Profile"); read phenotype and PDC limiters.
5. Match limiters to event demands (VI, Quadrant, matches) and time available; decide which weaknesses to train and which to ignore.
6. Build plan in blocks: "three weeks on, one week off" (or a variant as in Bill's plan), weekly template (Mon rest, Tue–Thu key, Fri recovery, weekends long/race); see plan tables in L1.
7. Execute with the interval stop rule; use ranges; use trainer for exact control.
8. Download every ride; review shapes, TSS, CTL ramp; adjust FTP when IF>1.05 in ~1-h efforts (raise cautiously, confirm by test).
9. Retest ≥ every 8 weeks, after a rest week.
10. Peak: reduce ATL so TSB is rising/neutral to modestly positive (more positive for short anaerobic events); pace by watts + RPE + HR.
11. Post-race review; write the ride story.

**5. Tensions and acknowledged trade-offs**
- FTP "≈60 min" is a convention; true range ~30–75 min; mFTP and TTE capture individual difference.
- Power Profile (discrete, needs maximal efforts) vs PD model (curve fit, depends on data quality; can be "wildly inaccurate"); use both and formal tests as triangulation.
- Levels are artificial divisions of a continuum; time-in-level misleads for power surges.
- Hard training vs overreaching: push through ordinary tiredness, but avoid ramp >7 TSS/day/week for >4 weeks.
- TSB need not be positive for bests; endurance events can peak with negative-but-rising TSB.
- Interval stop rule is guidance "with some hesitation"; adjust to athlete.
- Early hard pacing is wrong in TTs but can be right in ultra MTB (Allen effect).
- Bilateral/pedaling-style changes can reduce efficiency; change only under stated thresholds.
- Impulse-response model is rigorous but impractical; Performance Manager trades precision for usability ("as much art as science").
tags: type:methodology level:all subtopic:integral-model
src: Whole book
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C03-002] Performance Manager concept and relation to impulse-response model

- Rationale: performance is typically greatest when training is progressively increased to a very high level, then tapered to eliminate residual fatigue ("form equals fitness plus freshness").
- Eliminating gain factors k_a and k_f (a) removes uncertainty in estimating them (price: interpretation becomes as much art as science) and (b) allows exponentially weighted moving averages to replace the integral terms (qualitatively same behavior).
- CTL (42 d) ~ fitness term; ATL (7 d) ~ fatigue term; TSB = CTL – ATL.
- CTL (and the composition of training behind it) determines performance potential (within limits); TSB influences ability to express that potential. Performance depends on both; how much emphasis each gets is trial and error/experience, not science.
- Square-wave example (Fig 9.3: 0 to 100 TSS/day on Jan 1): CTL and ATL rise exponentially, same time courses as the fitness/fatigue components. TSB dips (trough ~ -60 late Jan) then rises mono-exponentially to eventually equal CTL/ATL (~0); it never exceeds its initial level, unlike model-predicted performance. TSB minimum occurs later than the model's performance minimum with the same time constants (42 and 7 days).
- ATL drives CTL: hard short-term work builds long-term fitness; stopping training short-term hurts long-term fitness.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:performance-manager metric:load
src: Ch 9 | Figs 9.3, 9.4
evidence: author-experience

## C04 Mechanisms

### [TRPM-C04-001] Five methods of power-meter hardware

Power-meter hardware differs by how the manufacturer measures effort. There are five distinct methods: (1) crank-based integrated systems (spider, crank arm, chainring), (2) hub-based integrated systems, (3) bottom-bracket sensors, (4) systems that measure "opposing forces", (5) pedal-based force sensors.
Crank-based is the most common place to measure power because it is close to where power is produced; it measures across the full 360-degree circle. Variations exist for road, mountain bike and track.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:all subtopic:power-meter-hardware metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Power Hardware"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-002] Spider-based power meters (SRM, Quarq, Power2Max, FSA, Team Zwatt)

- SRM (Schoberer Rad Messtechnik; Ulrich Schoberer, medical engineer) made the first commercial power meter. Prototype in the 1980s: old crank with the spider cut off and replaced by a saucer-sized plate of embedded strain gages. Early units cost upward of $10,000. The SRM Training System set the standard against which others are measured. Greg LeMond was one of the first Americans to use it.
- SRAM Quarq: next spider-based meter, with a battery outside the spider for easy replacement. Power2Max, FSA and Team Zwatt also made spider-based meters.
- Principle: force travels foot → pedal axle → crank arm → spider (right side, between right crank arm and chainrings) → chainrings, twisting (torsion) the spider slightly. Strain gages on spider legs measure the torsion and calculate wattage. Multiple gages in different locations are averaged into one second of data. Data include force from both legs.
- Calibrated at the factory; check periodically for accuracy.
- Advantage: single unit within the crank, easy integration; many existing cranks are compatible, so you may only need the chassis. Disadvantage: if your crank is incompatible you must find one that fits your frame; a new frame may be incompatible.
- Limitation: it cannot measure legs independently, so true bilateral left/right power is not possible. Left/right balance can be extrapolated using two reed switches that mark dead center top and bottom. Example: total 300 W, right side 170 W from 0–180 degrees, so left 130 W → balance reported as 57% left and 43% right [Likely typo in source — 170 W on right out of 300 W is 57% right / 43% left, but printed as 57 percent left and 43 percent right]. This ignores positive or negative force from 181–359 degrees (upstroke), so this pseudo-balance can be completely incorrect. For true bilateral, use a crank-arm or pedal-based unit.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:all subtopic:spider-power-meter metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Spider Power Meters"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-003] Crank-arm power meters (single and dual-sided)

- Strain gages are affixed inside the crank arms (scalloped-out cranks with gages embedded measure the whole arm deflection, very accurate) or glued on the outside with a small electronics pod (popular, economical). At publication at least six companies used this method, from first-to-market Stages to Shimano.
- Adaptable to many crank arms (fit and aesthetics).
- Single-sided (left arm) measures only the left leg; Andrew jokes these are "only correct half of the time". Typical leg-strength discrepancy is roughly 5 percent for most adults: at FTP 300 W a 15-watt discrepancy could be very misleading. With Ergomo's first left-leg meter (2002) athletes looked ready for the pro peloton but just had very large, strong left legs.
- Gages on both cranks: independent left and right power measured, reported and recorded. Some record at a higher rate, showing tangential and radial forces, useful for correcting pedaling form.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:all subtopic:crank-arm-power-meter metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Crank Arm"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-004] Chainring-based power meters (PowerTap, 2014)

PowerTap introduced this in 2014 so it had products measuring in pedals, crank and hub. Not actually the chainrings holding the gages: an additional spider is bolted to the existing crank spider at the chainring bolts, with extra arms between the crank spider arms containing strain gages measuring torsion. The chainrings are customized to the meter, so worn rings are replaced as normal. Compatible with many existing cranks (bolt on, no crank replacement). Measures left and right power, but not independently. Close to where power transfers from the body. Disadvantage: not compatible with all cranks.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:all subtopic:chainring-power-meter metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Crank-Based: Chainring"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-005] Hub-based power meters (PowerTap hub)

- Developed 1997 by Etune (later bought by CycleOps), evolved into the PowerTap hub. Strain gages sit in a "torque tube" in a complete rear hub; they measure torsion in the hub as chain load twists it, converted to wattage.
- Measures power actually reaching the road after the drivetrain, so reads about 5–10 watts lower than a crank or pedal meter.
- Advantages: easy to move between bikes (one wheel for multiple bikes); a coach can loan the wheel.
- Disadvantage: locked into the wheel the hub is laced into; to use race wheels you need an additional hub.
- PowerTap has been the long-time leader and the only company with this type; lighter, more weatherproof, simpler battery replacement; ANT+ or Bluetooth compatible.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:all subtopic:hub-power-meter metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Hub-Based Integrated System"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-006] Bottom-bracket power meters

- Introduced 2002 by Ergomo (optical light sensors measuring bottom-bracket axle twist). The right side of the axle is attached to the drivetrain and does not twist significantly; only the left side twists, so only left-leg power is measured (problematic, see left-only discrepancy). Ergomo could not adapt to two-piece cranks and external-bearing bottom brackets.
- Other companies make a self-contained sensor (electronics, strain gages, battery) that slides into the hollow axle and locks in; once calibrated it measures left-side axle torsion and transmits to the head unit. Internal battery is charged via micro-USB at the axle end cap. Compatible with various cranks and axles.
- Combined with a right-side-only meter, it makes a bilateral power meter.
- Delicate: requires extra care in installation and removal.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:all subtopic:bottom-bracket-power-meter metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Bottom-Bracket Power Meter"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-007] Opposing-forces method (Velocomp iBike, PowerPod, AeroPod)

- Velocomp introduced the iBike computer in 2006 (calibrated altitude sensor, accelerometer, Pitot tube for opposing air pressure from wind and rider movement).
- PowerPod (formerly iBike PowerPod) measures opposing forces rather than applied forces (all other meters measure applied). Sensors are in the pod, not on wheel, crank or chain: small, light, relatively inexpensive, easy ANT+ connection.
- Computes wattage from force and speed: Power = Force × Speed. Cyclists apply force to overcome opposing forces (hills, wind resistance, rolling resistance, acceleration). By Newton's Third Law opposing forces equal applied forces. User inputs: weight, an aerodynamic coefficient, a rolling resistance number.
- Wobble: rider and bike move at the same speed on average, but within a crank revolution the bike wobbles side-to-side and front-to-back relative to the center of mass of bike-and-rider. The accelerometer defines wobble as the average distance the bike deviates from the rider's center of mass over the user-selected period; reported in two graphs in iBike software: side-to-side vs front-to-back, and front-to-back vs crank angle.
- AeroPod (2018): digital sensors give power for both legs; with a direct-force power meter (DFPM) it continuously measures aerodynamic drag (Continuous CdA). On a bike with an ANT+ Sport DFPM the computer shows DFPM power plus CdA and Time Advantage; AeroPod records in internal memory DFPM and AeroPod power, wind speed and slope. Isaac software is included.
- Not a conventional meter; firmware improvements addressed first-generation critiques (road surfaces, temperature changes, complicated setup). Reports impressive numbers against a DFPM.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:all subtopic:powerpod-opposing-forces metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Opposing Forces Method"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-008] Pedal-based power meters

- First on-bike pedal meter: MetriGear, 2008; Garmin acquired it two years later. Multiple companies now offer them. Measure power where it transfers from foot to bike.
- Advantages: light; easily move between bikes or to an indoor cycling class with a pedal wrench; truly bilateral (left and right power); unique metrics to head units/phones: tangential and radial forces, and pronating (pushing with the inside of the foot) vs supinating (outside). Authors say these could help improve power output. Bilateral data are covered in Chapter 7.
- Disadvantages: some cyclists strongly prefer a particular pedal; for criterium racers, cornering angle may be less acute because pedals are bulkier; bearings can be an issue (manufacturers typically refurbish/replace).
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:all subtopic:pedal-power-meter metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Pedal-Based Power Meters"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-009] Why threshold power is the basis for training design

- Lactate threshold (LT), the intensity at which lactate begins to accumulate in blood, has been known for ~40 years to be a powerful predictor of endurance performance.
- VO2max (cardiovascular fitness) sets the upper limit of aerobic energy production; LT (metabolic fitness) determines the fraction of VO2max that can be used for a given period.
- Blood lactate is an indirect marker of biochemical events in muscle. LT reflects the ability of muscles to match energy supply to demand, which determines the fuel mix (carbohydrate vs fat) and the development of fatigue.
- LT is the single most important physiological determinant of performance in events from a 3 km pursuit to a three-week stage race, especially when expressed as power output, which also accounts for cycling efficiency.
- Perceived effort at any intensity depends on power relative to power at LT, so threshold power is a sound basis for any power-based program.
- Power at lactate threshold integrates VO2max, the percentage of VO2max sustainable for a duration, and cycling efficiency. So levels are defined relative to threshold power rather than power at VO2max (just as HR levels are better referenced to threshold HR than to maximal HR).
- Lab lactate testing is rarely available, depends on protocol design and interpretation, and is not more accurate than simple field tests, "since the best predictor of performance is performance itself."
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:lactate-threshold sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Step 1; Coggan's Classic Training Levels

### [TRPM-C04-010] Target durations of the Power Profile and the physiology they represent

The Power Profile indexes 5 s, 1 min, 5 min and functional threshold power (FTP) to reflect, respectively: neuromuscular power, anaerobic capacity, maximal oxygen uptake (VO2max), and lactate threshold (LT).
Caveats given by the authors:
- A 1-minute all-out effort is not completely anaerobic: roughly 40–45 percent of energy is derived aerobically.
- It does not fully use anaerobic capacity, which generally requires 1.5–2.5 minutes to deplete.
- A 5-minute all-out effort is not exactly 100 percent of VO2max: most athletes can sustain a power eliciting 105–110 percent of VO2max for this duration.
- Power over these durations is still expected to correlate with more direct measurements of these abilities. Durations were also chosen for reproducibility and convenience of data collection.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling system:neuromuscular system:anaerobic system:vo2max system:threshold level:all subtopic:power-profile
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "Target Durations" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C04-011] Inverse and positive relationships between anaerobic and aerobic power

Physiologically an inverse relationship might be expected between anaerobic and aerobic efforts (a great Tour de France rider may be weaker in match sprint), but a positive association is also expected between pairs within a category. The scientific literature is split on whether short-term and long-term power are truly inversely related, but there is clearly a positive association within each category. Expected: inverse relation between neuromuscular power and lactate threshold; positive relation between VO2max and lactate threshold.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:all subtopic:power-profile system:aerobic system:anaerobic
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "Defining Your Phenotype" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C04-012] Training levels are continuous

All training levels are continuous with no definitive start or stop. You do not go from training aerobic capacity at Level 3 (76–90% FTP) to threshold at 91% in Level 4. The physiological systems meld into one another: at a given intensity one system is used proportionally more but the others are still affected. Keep the big picture and do not become a slave to numbers.
tags: type:mechanism type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:training-levels
src: Ch 5
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-013] The Sweet Spot Model: Balancing Training Effect, Volume, and Physiological Strain

Figure 5.2 illustrates the conceptual mechanism underlying Sweet Spot training (88–94% of FTP):
- Training effect (increase in threshold power): Follows an inverted U-shaped bell curve starting near zero at ~45% of FTP, peaking in the upper 80s to low 90s % of FTP (upper Zone 3 / lower Zone 4), and declining back toward zero above ~135% of FTP.
- Maximum duration (volume): Starts high (~97 arbitrary units at 40% of FTP) and declines progressively as intensity rises, dropping steeply through Zones 4, 5, and 6 down to zero near ~138% of FTP.
- Physiological strain: Remains low and relatively flat through Zones 1 and 2, then curves exponentially upward through Zones 3, 4, 5, and 6, reaching maximum near ~134% of FTP.
- Synthesis: The "Sweet Spot" balances high training stimulus with tolerable physiological strain and sufficient repeatable duration. While it does not specifically maximize sprint power, VO2max, or anaerobic capacity, it provides the greatest stimulus-to-fatigue efficiency for building FTP.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power level:intermediate subtopic:sweet-spot
src: Ch 5 | Figure 5.2
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-014] Xert Maximal Power Available (MPA) and Fitness Signature

- Xert (predictive analysis software) analyzes past data and predicts future physiological improvements; adaptive workouts and plans.
- MPA: real-time estimate of power a rider can generate at any point in a ride, combining fatigue modeling with the rider's three-parameter Fitness Signature: Peak Power (PP) = peak 1-second power; High Intensity Energy (HIE) = close to anaerobic capacity; Threshold Power (TP) = FTP.
- At ride start MPA = Peak Power (peak 1 s). It is recalculated each second from current power, work above TP and work below TP. MPA declines as you work above TP and fatigue, and is restored when effort eases (like a battery gauge: can I climb this hill, pace this TT, start the next interval?). E.g., 400 W is easy for 10 s but much harder after 60 s.
- Uses: shown on bike computer in real time or post-ride; predicts fatigue to target energy systems; helps tactics and race decisions.
tags: type:mechanism level:advanced subtopic:xert subtopic:mpa subtopic:fitness-signature sport:cycling metric:power
src: Ch 6 "Predictive Power Analysis" | Fig 6.18
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-015] TSS rationale and interpretation

- Intensity, frequency and duration all matter; as intensity rises, volume must fall (and vice versa) or overtraining results. TSS was developed to quantify total load and help prevent this.
- TSS is based on a 1-hour threshold time trial, familiar to riders; 40 km TT performance correlates highly with power at lactate threshold. Because TSS is relative to FTP, a 300 TSS ride for a beginner differs in distance/duration from one for Peter Sagan but puts the same stress on the physiological system. Tolerable load differs by individual.
- TSS is computed per workout, shown in software and most bike computers; lets you decide how to proceed (e.g., rest days after very high TSS), and, accumulated over a year, shows effect of load after the ride, 2 days, 1 week and a month later.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling metric:load subtopic:tss level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Training Stress Score"; closing

### [TRPM-C04-016] Neuromuscular analysis: cadence distribution, AEPF, CPV

- NP/IF/TSS recognize neuromuscular effects only insofar as they influence metabolism (e.g., fiber recruitment). Neuromuscular responses are essentially instantaneous, so histograms (which ignore continuity of efforts) are acceptable.
- Cadence frequency histogram: e.g., large time share at 80-90 rpm likely indicates a Level 2 Endurance ride (Figure 7.3).
- Force-velocity diagrams (used by muscle physiologists since the early 1920s) are plotted as AEPF vs CPV. CPV is a good predictor of muscle-shortening velocity and joint angular velocity; with constant crank length, cadence could be used instead.
- Plain scatterplots are relative with no anchor values, making rides hard to compare; Quadrant Analysis solves this by using threshold power as reference.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling system:neuromuscular metric:cadence subtopic:aepf subtopic:cpv level:all
src: Ch 7 "Measuring Neuromuscular Power"

### [TRPM-C04-017] Fiber recruitment and threshold as recruitment point

- Curvilinear metabolic responses (glycogen use, lactate) are partly due to Type II (fast-twitch) recruitment. Well below lactate threshold there is little Type II use; e.g., Level 2 might be about 90% Type I and 10% Type II; at Level 4 significant Type II fibers are recruited too.
- Figure 7.5 (Vollestad et al. 1985): Type I recruited maximally even at about 40% VO2max; Type IIa and especially IIx only at much higher intensities (IIx starts around 75% VO2max).
- Studies (EMG spectral analysis, biopsies) suggest FTP is also a threshold for fast-twitch recruitment: at a typical self-selected cadence FTP occurs at the force where significant fast-twitch recruitment begins. The division into quadrants is somewhat arbitrary; duration matters but is not shown; the true threshold is a curve falling left to right, not a horizontal line.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling system:threshold system:neuromuscular subtopic:fiber-recruitment level:all
evidence: study (N.K. Vollestad et al., Acta Physiologica Scandinavica 125, 1985, p. 395)
src: Ch 7 | Fig 7.5

### [TRPM-C04-018] Strength versus power in cycling

- Strength = maximal force-generating capacity, technically measured at zero velocity (isometric). Power = rate of doing work, depends on force, distance and especially speed. Many confuse them and wrongly conclude strength limits cycling performance.
- Strength rarely limits power. Example (Figure 7.13): maximal force about 1,100 N (just over 110 kgf, about 166% of body mass); highest force in racing about 600 N (about 55% of max); most of the time less than 400 N (about 35%); time trialing at FTP only about 25% of max force. Maximal AEPF 1,110 N, maximal CPV 2.87 m/s. Exceptions: standing starts (BMX, some track).
- Key issue is how much power can be produced on average and at key moments (attacks, sprint). Riders rarely reach 100% of maximal power in endurance events; the gap is due to fatigue, so train the aerobic and anaerobic ATP-producing pathways; even sprinters can train fatigue resistance.
- Weight training aimed only at strength (heavy, slow) has limited effect on maximal power of trained cyclists (specificity principle: slow-speed resistance training increases force mainly at slow speeds). Taken to an extreme it may reduce maximal shortening velocity (steeper AEPF-CPV line: higher Y-intercept, x-intercept moved left), impairing power at high velocities. Explosive work such as plyometrics may mitigate. Quadrant Analysis lets riders test whether off-bike training helps.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling system:neuromuscular subtopic:strength-vs-power subtopic:resistance-training level:all
evidence: clinical-trial (studies referenced generally, not cited here)
src: Ch 7 "Strength Versus Power"

### [TRPM-C04-019] Interpreting bilateral power data

- Normal asymmetry: the typical left-right leg-strength discrepancy is 5%.
- ANT+ power balance uses average power per leg (e.g., left 150 W, right 120 W = 55% / 45%) and ignores power absorbed by the opposite leg on its upstroke.
- Net leg contribution = released minus opposite-leg absorbed. Example: left releases 150 W, right absorbs 20 W, net left 130 W; right releases 170 W, left absorbs 30 W, net right 140 W; total still 270 W but true balance is 48% left / 52% right (Figure 7.15).
- The ANT+ metrics (Pedaling Smoothness, Torque Effectiveness, Balance) are less accurate; authors created GPR, GPA and KI instead.
- Plot GPR and GPA on a Mean Maximal Power Curve (left vs right) to see durations where one leg releases or absorbs more. Figure 7.17 example: left releases more up to 30 s, right more from 30 s to 20 min; left absorbs more at all durations, so the rider should try to reduce GPA. Also check if metrics change with fatigue during the ride.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:bilateral-power subtopic:gpr-gpa level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Bilateral Power Analysis" | Figs 7.15-7.17

### [TRPM-C04-020] Inconsistent V-shaped Power Profile (physiological reasoning)

- A V-shaped Power Profile (high 5-s neuromuscular power and high FTP, with low anaerobic capacity) is hard to reconcile with physiology. High neuromuscular power is associated with a predominance of fast-twitch fibers, and high FTP with a predominance of slow-twitch fibers. Both cannot exceed 50 percent in one individual.
- More likely this rider is an all-rounder with exceptionally high neuromuscular power and FTP relative to others. The low 1-min power is an artifact of testing, or of never performing a true maximal 60-s effort.
tags: type:mechanism type:decision-rule sport:cycling system:anaerobic level:all subtopic:profile-artifact subtopic:muscle-fiber-type
src: Ch 8 | intro
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-021] FTP is not exactly a 60-minute power (duration artifact)

- FTP is an intensity that can be sustained for different durations by different individuals. Per the scientific literature, roughly 30 to 70 minutes, with more trained subjects toward the upper end.
- Andrew originally suggested the 40 km TT power as the best way of estimating FTP. A 40 km TT lasts about 60 min but almost never exactly 60 min, and the faster the rider the greater the discrepancy.
- Example (Jimmy Fast): practice 40 km TT on 32-spoke wheels with no aero gear gives FTP 300 W for 60 min. A week later, in full aero gear, he finishes the 40 km in 55 min at 310 W and shuts down at the line. His 60-min mean maximal power is 284 W. His FTP did not drop; the lower 60-min value is an artifact of the fixed time period.
- Formal testing would be needed to fix this, which would give up the advantage of ecologically valid data.
tags: type:principle type:mechanism sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power level:all subtopic:ftp-definition
src: Ch 8 | intro
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-022] Power Duration model: what it is and its advantages

- A mathematical approach (non-linear curve fitting) applied to maximal efforts in ecologically valid situations. It extracts the same information as the Power Profile (NP, AC, VO2max, FTP), bridges the time frames between discrete values, and extends beyond FTP to effort levels the Profile did not consider.
- It smooths the lumps and bumps in a mean maximal power dataset (day-to-day variation, power-meter error), which matters when basing further analyses on mean maximal power.
- It rigorously predicts what can be done at all durations from consistent rider data accumulated over a sustained period.
- Power falls non-linearly (exponentially) with duration. The decline is steep between short durations (e.g. 10 s and 30 s), and power roughly plateaus beyond several minutes toward an hour. The plateau roughly corresponds to functional threshold.
- Simple interpolation error (Figure 8.1): interpolating between about 20 s and 20 min gives about 500 W for 8 min, while the PD model gives 380 W.
- Figure 8.1 example values: Pmax 1,287 W, FRC 16.8 kJ.
- The model needs a more flexible and nuanced interpretation by athlete or coach, but gives more accurate and valuable insight.
- Result: formalized testing becomes less important and data from typical riding more relevant.
tags: type:methodology type:mechanism sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:power-duration-curve subtopic:interpolation
src: Ch 8 "Utilizing the Power Duration Model" | Fig 8.1
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-023] Phenotyping and the Phenotypic Map

- Phenotype: physical expression of a genotype. Complex phenotypes involve many genes and varying environmental influence. VO2max is thought to be 40 to 60 percent genetically determined (so 40 to 60 percent environmental), with at least 97 genes contributing to its trainability. Sprint performance depends on myosin ATPase, calcium ATPase, motoneuron innervation, energy-system characteristics, etc.
- The classic Power Profile types (sprinter, pursuiter, time trialist, all-rounder) are phenotypes. Auto-phenotyping is a statistical calculation giving an objective rather than subjective determination, presented as a label or as a Phenotypic Map (Figure 8.3).
- Map axes: horizontal Pmax/FTP (0 to 10), vertical FRC/Pmax (0 to 45). Regions: Pursuiter (top left), Merckxian (top right), Time Trialist (bottom left), All-rounder (bottom center), Sprinter (bottom right). Grid: horizontal line at FRC/Pmax = 25; all-rounder central column between Pmax/FTP 4.0 and 5.0.
- Low Pmax/FTP corresponds to the upward-sloping time trialist Power Profile; high Pmax/FTP to the downward-sloping sprinter profile.
- Time trialist vs pursuiter: both have high FTP relative to Pmax (slow-twitch predominance). Among these, FRC/Pmax (an anaerobic/anaerobic ratio) distinguishes the successful pursuiters (higher FRC/Pmax). Adage: in a breakaway with a time trialist you win the sprint; among time trialists the likely sprint winner is a pursuiter. Bradley Wiggins and Geraint Thomas (track pursuit champions who became Grand Tour winners) show plasticity between pursuiter and time trialist.
- The 2D map shows the spectrum within and between phenotypes and the movement of a rider through training or de-training (e.g. all-rounder to pursuiter to time trialist and back).
tags: type:mechanism type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:phenotype subtopic:phenotypic-map metric:power
src: Ch 8 "Phenotyping" | Fig 8.3
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-024] Banister's impulse-response model (concept)

- First proposed by Dr. Eric Banister in 1975; most studies of training dose vs. performance use it.
- Model: a transfer function. Inputs = daily training doses (volume and intensity combined); output = predicted performance.
- Training has two opposing effects: (1) positive adaptations (improved performance), longer-lasting, "chronic"; (2) negative consequences of recent bouts (residual fatigue), shorter, "acute". Acute negative effects can obscure the chronic positive effects, but only for a time.
- Applied successfully to weight lifting, hammer throwing, running, swimming, cycling, triathlon. Factors account for more than 70%, often more than 90%, of day-to-day variation in performance. Also predicts markers of load/adaptation (serum testosterone, creatine kinase, anxiety, perceived fatigue).
- Used to optimize training and tapering and to evaluate cross-sport effects (e.g., running training on cycling). Usually load was quantified by Banister's heart-rate-based TRIMP, but it works about equally well however training is quantified.
- Single session (Fig 9.1, TSS 100): performance first degraded by fatigue, then positive adaptation dominates and performance is eventually improved. Repeated bouts = summation of individual impulses; effect depends on magnitude and timing of each dose (Fig 9.2: sustained 100 TSS/day; fatigue plateaus near 1,500 arbitrary units, fitness slightly above 4,000, performance plateaus 2,500–3,000).
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:all subtopic:impulse-response metric:load
src: Ch 9 "Banister's Impulse-Response Model" | pp. n/a
evidence: study (Banister 1975)

### [TRPM-C04-025] Somatotypes and muscle fiber characteristics (ectomorph, mesomorph, endomorph)

The author categorizes cyclists into three primary body types with distinct muscle-fiber profiles:
- Ectomorph: thin build with relatively small muscles and a higher percentage of slow-twitch (Type I) muscle fibers. Suited to endurance, climbing, and steady-state time trialing, but naturally limited in short-duration explosive snap and neuromuscular force (e.g., Bob Rider).
- Mesomorph: medium build with a relatively even distribution of fast-twitch (Type II) and slow-twitch (Type I) muscle fibers.
- Endomorph: heavier build, higher percentage of body fat, and predominantly fast-twitch muscle fibers.
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:all subtopic:somatotypes
src: Ch 10 | "Bob's 16-Week Threshold Improvement Plan"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-026] Wattage creation: not all watts are equal

- The same watts can be produced by high force and slow cadence (e.g. 1,000 W in 53:12 gear) or low force and fast cadence (1,000 W in 39:21 gear). They recruit different fibers.
- More fast-twitch (Type II) fibers are recruited in Quadrant II (high force, low cadence); more slow-twitch (Type I) in Quadrant IV (low force, high cadence).
- Fast-twitch recruitment uses more muscle glycogen. Fluctuating between low and high forces costs the most glycogen, like constantly flooring a car.
- High Normalized Power relative to average power (high Variability Index) means too much fluctuation. Smooth effort on hills and avoid bursts to keep VI low and reduce glycogen use.
- Even efforts not fully in Quadrant I or II cost more than wanted. The authors do not advocate high cadence per se, but mindfulness: stay light on the pedals, use gearing for consistent cadence, and if you are a gear masher spend training time developing a smoother stroke. Choose gearing in your pacing plan to minimize glycogen use.
tags: type:mechanism sport:triathlon metric:cadence,power level:all subtopic:quadrant-analysis
src: Ch 12 "Wattage Creation: Are All Watts Created Equally?"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C04-027] Aerobic vs anaerobic contribution in the pursuit (MAOD)

- Individual pursuit: 4 km men, 3 km women on a banked velodrome; duration 3.5-5 min. Resistance split: about 85% wind, 8% inertia, 5% rolling, 2% drivetrain and bearing friction. This makes aero position crucial.
- Method: compare theoretical maximal aerobic power (from lab VO2max and efficiency, smooth line) with total measured power (jagged line). Area under the aerobic line is the share done aerobically; the rest is anaerobic (PCr/ATP and lactate). Expressed as the extra O2 needed, this is the estimated MAOD (maximal accumulated oxygen deficit).
- Alternative without a lab: the quasi-plateau in power after about 1.5-2.5 min of a well-paced pursuit is essentially power at VO2max, since anaerobic capacity is used up by then; after that exercise is 100% aerobic ("pay as you go").
- Rider A (male masters, road/long TT, poor sprint): VO2max 4.47 L/min, est. MAOD 3.36 L, avg power 397 W, CdA 0.214 m2, 3 km 3:47.3; 80% aerobic / 20% anaerobic.
- Rider B (elite female pursuiter): VO2max 4.20 L/min, est. MAOD 5.27 L, avg power 411 W, CdA 0.236 m2, 3 km 3:49.7; 72% aerobic / 28% anaerobic. Despite 4% lower VO2max she makes 4% more power thanks to anaerobic capacity. With Rider A's aerodynamics she would take about 3:43, about 4 s faster. Anaerobic capacity is generally lower in women than men, even relative to muscle mass.
- Training implication: Rider A (weak anaerobic) should focus on Level 6 intervals, especially in the run-up to the goal event. Rider B should emphasize VO2max and FTP via Levels 3, 4 and 5 (especially off-season and preseason). After doing this she improved her PB by more than 4 s and won the national championship pursuit.
- Data also show whether training works (power over 3.5-5 min) and aid pacing feedback beyond lap times.
tags: type:mechanism type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:elite subtopic:maod subtopic:pursuit subtopic:aerobic-anaerobic system:vo2max system:anaerobic metric:power
src: Ch 13 "Gauging Aerobic and Anaerobic Energy Production", Fig 13.11
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C04-028] Quadrant Analysis: points race vs criterium (power expansion path)

Same rider (national-caliber female), 20 km track points race vs ~30 min road criterium with nearly identical average power and cadence (Fig 14.3; reference CPV 1.6 m/s and AEPF about 165 N at 260 W and 90 rpm). The points race (fixed gear) showed far more variation in force and pedal speed. Connecting points sequentially gives a consistent clockwise pattern: lulls (QIII) → high force at lower cadence at attack (QII) → high force high cadence sprint/breakaway (QI) → easing but pedaling rapidly while slowing (QIV). Criterium gives a tangled knot with no pattern. Illustrates the need for track-specific training to gain "track legs".
tags: type:mechanism sport:cycling level:all subtopic:quadrant-analysis subtopic:track
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Determining Race Demands" | Fig 14.3

## C05 Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-001] 40 kilometer TT

A 40 km (24.8-mile) solo race against the clock; time trials are often called "the race of truth."
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:time-trial sport:cycling
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-002] Abbreviations (list from the book, A–M)

The book's own abbreviation list, part 1 (AEPF to W/kg are split across two entries). Expansions are exactly as printed:
- AEPF: average effective pedal force
- AT: anaerobic threshold
- ATL: Acute Training Load
- ATP: adenosine triphosphate
- bpm: beats per minute
- C: cadence
- Cat. I, Cat. II, etc.: Category I, Category II, etc.
- CdA: cyclist's aerodynamic drag
- CP: critical power
- CPV: circumferential pedal velocity
- Crr: cyclist's rolling resistance
- CTL: Chronic Training Load
- CX: cyclocross
- FRC: functional reserve capacity
- FTHR: functional threshold heart rate
- FTp: functional threshold pace
- FTP: functional threshold power
- IF: Intensity Factor
- J: joule
- kJ: kilojoule
- LT: lactate threshold
- m/s: meters per second
- MAOD: maximal accumulated O2 deficit
- mFTP: modeled FTP
- MLSS: maximal lactate steady state
- MMP: Mean Maximal Power
- MTB: mountain bike
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling subtopic:abbreviations
src: Abbreviations

### [TRPM-C05-003] Abbreviations (list from the book, N–W/kg)

The book's own abbreviation list, part 2:
- NP: Normalized Power
- OBLA: onset of blood lactate accumulation
- PCr: phosphate creatine
- PDC: Power Duration Curve
- Pmax: max power
- PMC: Performance Manager Chart
- Ppeak: peak power
- RM: repetition maximum
- RPE: rate of perceived exertion
- rTSS: running Training Stress Score
- SX: Super Cross
- TRIMP: training impulse
- TSB: Training Stress Balance
- TSS: Training Stress Score
- TTE: time to exhaustion
- TT: time trial
- VI: Variability Index
- W/kg: watts per kilogram
tags: type:definition level:all sport:cycling subtopic:abbreviations
src: Abbreviations

### [TRPM-C05-004] Acute Training Load (ATL)

Measure of how much an athlete has trained recently, considering volume and intensity. Exponentially weighted moving average of daily TSS with default time constant 7 days (primarily the past ~two weeks). Analogous to the negative (fatigue) term of the impulse-response model, a relative indicator of changes in performance ability due to fatigue, not an absolute predictor (k_f eliminated). Fatigue is more closely related to ATL.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:atl metric:load
src: Ch 9 "The Performance Manager Concept"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-005] Allen effect

Occurs when a competitor accelerates through a slow section of a course knowing a much faster section follows; on the fast section he extends the gap while those behind are still in the slow section (e.g. attacking a steep hill, then averaging over 30 mph on the descent while others climb at about 4 mph). Starts as a distance gap, which can remain while the time gap stays the same; it accumulates through the race and is exaggerated in ultra-endurance MTB races.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:allen-effect subtopic:mountain-bike
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Ultra-Endurance Mountain Biking" | n/a

### [TRPM-C05-006] Anaerobic capacity / anaerobic work capacity

The overall quantity of work (not the rate, which is power) that you can perform relying on anaerobic metabolism. Usually trained with short (e.g., 30-second to 3-minute), very high-intensity intervals.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:anaerobic-capacity sport:cycling system:anaerobic
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-007] Anaerobic threshold (AT)

More correctly termed "ventilatory threshold": the exercise intensity at which there is a nonlinear increase in ventilation relative to metabolic rate (rate of oxygen uptake, VO2). Not mechanistically related (not cause and effect) to lactate threshold, but often used to estimate it.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:anaerobic-threshold sport:cycling system:threshold
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-008] Athlete Home Page and Periodic Chart (WKO)

- Athlete Home Page: main screen showing long-term changes in fitness in TrainingPeaks WKO Software.
- Periodic Chart: chart in WKO allowing data to be viewed over a certain period of time.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:wko-software sport:cycling
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-009] Average Effective Pedal Force (AEPF)

The average (over 360 degrees) effective (tangential to the crank) pedal force produced by both legs combined, expressed in newtons (N). Derived from power and cadence.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:aepf level:all
src: Ch 7 "Measuring Neuromuscular Power"

### [TRPM-C05-010] Burn a match

A very hard effort. A rider has only so many "matches" in the matchbook, so timing of such efforts is critical. The power meter shows when a match was burned.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:matches level:all
src: Ch 1 "Analyze Your Race"

### [TRPM-C05-011] Cadence; Self-selected cadence

- Cadence: revolutions per minute (rpm) of the cranks.
- Self-selected cadence: the cadence range in which you naturally pedal without consciously thinking about it.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:cadence sport:cycling metric:cadence
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-012] Calibration (Power meter)

The formal verification or adjustment of a power meter's force measurement response, which entails hanging a known weight (e.g., 50 kg) off the crank arm or pedal to confirm or set the torque measurement slope. Typically performed at the factory, though some manufacturers publish instructions. Frequently confused with zeroing because Garmin head units label the zero-offset command as "calibration."
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:calibration metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Zero Your Power Meter"

### [TRPM-C05-013] Calibration vs Zero offset; Strain gauges; Aliasing

- Calibration: in power-meter head units, refers to "zeroing" (see Zero offset). Misnamed: true calibration requires calibrating against a known static or dynamic load.
- Zero offset: also called "calibration" in head units; "zeroes" the power meter while no force is applied so it correctly interprets applied force.
- Strain gauges: small foil leaflets bonded to a surface in an electronic circuit to measure strain (deformation), which relates to the applied force; used to measure force (torque).
- Aliasing: distortion or artifact in data (e.g., power meter) when an analog signal is sampled at too low a frequency.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:power-meter-hardware sport:cycling
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-014] Cardiovascular fitness vs Metabolic fitness

- Cardiovascular fitness: capability of the cardiovascular system to transport O2 to tissues (e.g., contracting muscles) and aid thermoregulation by increasing skin blood flow; traditionally quantified by VO2max.
- Metabolic fitness: ability of muscles to balance aerobic energy production with energy demand, which determines the rate of muscle glycogen utilization, blood lactate levels, etc.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:fitness-types sport:cycling system:aerobic
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-015] CdA (Cyclist's aerodynamic drag)

A measure of an object's aerodynamic drag; here the object is the cyclist and bike. CdA is the product of the coefficient of drag (Cd) and frontal area (A). Best measured in a wind tunnel, but can be estimated with field tests using a power meter.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:cda sport:cycling
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-016] CdA (Effective Frontal Area)

The product of the dimensionless aerodynamic drag coefficient ($C_\text{d}$) and the frontal area ($A$, in $\text{m}^2$). Represents total aerodynamic drag characteristics of rider and equipment.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:glossary subtopic:aerodynamics
src: Ch 13 "Aerodynamic Testing with a Power Meter"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-017] Chronic Training Load (CTL)

Measure of how much an athlete has trained historically, considering volume and intensity. Calculated as an exponentially weighted moving average of daily TSS (or TRIMP) with default time constant 42 days (primarily training of the past ~three months). Analogous to the positive (fitness) term of the impulse-response model, but a relative indicator of changes in performance ability due to fitness, not an absolute predictor (gain k_a eliminated). The PMC is not very sensitive to the CTL time constant, so there is little point changing it from the default.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:ctl metric:load
src: Ch 9 "The Performance Manager Concept"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-018] Circumferential Pedal Velocity (CPV)

How fast the pedal moves around the circle it makes while pedaling, expressed in meters per second (m/s). Derived from cadence and crank length, and serves as an excellent predictor of muscle-shortening velocity and joint angular velocity.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:cadence subtopic:cpv level:all
src: Ch 7 "Measuring Neuromuscular Power"

### [TRPM-C05-019] Continuous CdA

The continuous on-road measurement of aerodynamic drag (coefficient of aerodynamic drag multiplied by frontal area) obtained by pairing an opposing-force digital sensor device (such as the AeroPod) with an on-bike direct-force power meter (DFPM).
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:aerodynamics metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Opposing Forces Method"

### [TRPM-C05-020] Critical Power

Defined in the scientific literature as the slope of the work-time relationship.
