An inherent characteristic of the aerobic energy supply system, representing a power that can be sustained for a very long time without fatigue. When measured with bouts of 3 to perhaps 30 minutes, it is essentially the same as functional threshold power. See also Mean Maximal Power.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:critical-power sport:cycling metric:power system:threshold
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-021] Direct-force power meter (DFPM)

A power meter that measures the forces applied by the cyclist (strain-gage based: crank, spider, pedal, hub), as opposed to the PowerPod/AeroPod, which measure opposing (resistive) forces.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:dfpm
src: Ch 2

### [TRPM-C05-022] Form (Form = Fitness + Freshness)

Form is the proper balance of fitness and freshness. Fitness is the result of training stress; freshness is the result of rest; so form comes from the combination of training stress and rest. A rider fit at the end of the Tour de France is tired, not on form; one who has not ridden for two months is fresh but unfit, not on form; one who trains very hard but does not rest/taper is fit but fatigued. Only the correct balance creates form.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:form
src: Ch 9 "Peaking with Power"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-023] Functional Reserve Capacity (FRC)

The amount of continuous work that can be performed above FTP until fatigue occurs. Roughly analogous to Anaerobic Capacity (AC) power, but a capacity measure (joules), not a rate measure.
- Below FTP, power comes primarily from sustainable aerobic metabolism (as long as fuel is available). Above FTP, progressively more energy comes from non-oxidative sources (anaerobic glycolysis, ATP-PCr), which deplete quickly. The higher the power above FTP, the faster FRC depletes.
- Energy above FTP is a reserve: not sustainable, but readily and quickly available.
- Analogy: a rechargeable battery that recharges slowly, like the electric motor of a gas-electric hybrid. With rest or effort below FTP, FRC recharges to some extent. Recharge is faster the easier the recovery: recovering just below FTP recharges more slowly than resting entirely.
- The traditional notion of isolated anaerobic versus aerobic systems is not accurate.
tags: type:definition type:mechanism sport:cycling system:anaerobic metric:power level:all subtopic:frc
src: Ch 8 "Functional Reserve Capacity (FRC)"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-024] Functional Threshold Pace (FTp)

The running counterpart to cycling Functional Threshold Power (FTP), designated with a lowercase "p" to denote pace instead of power. It represents the maximal running pace sustainable aerobically without rapid fatigue, where ~45 minutes of running at FTp represents equivalent training stress to 1 hour of cycling at FTP.
tags: type:definition sport:running,triathlon metric:pace level:all subtopic:ftp-running
src: Ch 12 "Timing Your Fitness Peak"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-025] Functional Threshold Power (FTP)

"FTP is the highest power that a rider can maintain in a quasi–steady state without fatiguing. When power exceeds FTP, fatigue will occur much sooner (generally after approximately one hour in well-trained cyclists), whereas power just below FTP can be maintained considerably longer."
Note: "threshold" has many synonyms and definitions (AT anaerobic threshold, LT lactate threshold, MLSS maximal lactate steady state, OBLA onset of blood lactate, plain "threshold"), based on HR, lactate, wattage, etc.; FTP is the authors' power-based version.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:ftp sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power
src: Ch 3 | Step 1

### [TRPM-C05-026] Gross Power Absorbed (GPA)

The gross (i.e., muscular + inertial + gravitational) power absorbed by one leg (left or right), primarily during the upstroke.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:bilateral-power subtopic:gpa level:all
src: Ch 7 "Bilateral Power Analysis"

### [TRPM-C05-027] Gross Power Absorbed (GPA) and Gross Power Released (GPR)

- GPA: gross (muscular + inertial + gravitational) power absorbed by one leg (left or right), primarily during the upstroke.
- GPR: gross (muscular + inertial + gravitational) power released by one leg, primarily during the downstroke.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:pedal-force sport:cycling metric:power
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-028] Gross Power Released (GPR)

The gross (i.e., muscular + inertial + gravitational) power released by one leg (left or right), primarily on the downstroke.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:bilateral-power subtopic:gpr level:all
src: Ch 7 "Bilateral Power Analysis"

### [TRPM-C05-029] Heart rate as the intensity of your intention

Hunter's description of heart rate: a readout of how hard you are trying. It is a response to a stimulus, like the rpm dial (tachometer) of a car.
tags: type:definition metric:hr subtopic:heart-rate level:all
src: Ch 1 "Add Meaning to Heart Rate Monitoring"

### [TRPM-C05-030] iLevels

Individualized training levels built from the Power Duration Curve Model (TrainingPeaks WKO4), used above Coggan Classic Level 4 for advanced users or riders who do not fit Coggan Classic levels. Created because some outliers (e.g., holding 150% FTP for 5 min, previously thought impossible) did not fit; Andrew Coggan reconciled this. Percent-change markers on the curve (e.g., big drops at 28 s and 1:33) define where the metabolic system operates at the AC level. Only above Level 4 because at and below Level 4 individual variation relative to FTP is small. Exception: Level 4a Sweet Spot, included for its popularity and effectiveness. New names reflect the energy-system continuum.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:power level:advanced subtopic:ilevels
src: Ch 5 "iLevel Workouts"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-031] Intensity Factor (IF)

The ratio of Normalized Power to functional threshold power (IF = NP/FTP): the fraction of FTP maintained for a workout or part of it. Designed for comparing relative intensity over time within an individual or between riders.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:intensity-factor level:all
src: Ch 7

### [TRPM-C05-032] Isopower

A pacing strategy of maintaining a smooth, constant power output at Functional Threshold Power (FTP) for the duration of an event until the final 3 to 5 minutes, when intensity is increased toward the finish line. Default pacing strategy for flat, windless time trials.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:glossary subtopic:pacing subtopic:time-trial metric:power
src: Ch 13 "Flat Time Trials"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-033] Kilocalorie and Kilojoule

- Kilocalorie (k/cal): the energy required to raise 1 kilogram of water by 1 degree Celsius; commonly called one Calorie (capital C).
- Kilojoule (kJ): a measure of energy. One joule = 1 watt-second (work of 1 watt for 1 second); 1 kJ = 1,000 joules.
tags: type:definition type:formula level:all subtopic:energy-units sport:cycling
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-034] Kitchen sink workout

Author's term (Ch 10): a workout in which everything is thrown in (Tempo, Sweet Spot, FTP, VO2max, etc.), done as a long ride to exhaust energy systems and muscular endurance.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:kitchen-sink
src: Ch 10 | Joe Weeks 5-8

### [TRPM-C05-035] Kurtotic Index (KI)

A measure of the "peakedness" of the pattern of force, torque, and power application during the power-producing phase. A higher Kurtotic Index indicates a "stomper" who applies force sharply; a lower index indicates smoother application across the pedal stroke.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:bilateral-power subtopic:kurtotic-index level:all
src: Ch 7 "Bilateral Power Analysis"

### [TRPM-C05-036] Lactate Threshold (LT)

The exercise intensity at which release of lactate into the blood first begins to exceed its rate of removal, so blood lactate begins to rise. For most athletes and coaches, LT is a relatively low intensity, approximately the transition between Levels 2 and 3.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:lactate-threshold sport:cycling system:lactate
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-037] MAOD (Maximal Accumulated Oxygen Deficit)

The difference between theoretical total energy demand and actual aerobic energy production during maximal supra-threshold exercise, quantified as the equivalent volume of oxygen (in liters of $\text{O}_2$) that would have been required to generate that energy aerobically. Represents the contribution of anaerobic energy sources (phosphocreatine/ATP and anaerobic glycolysis/lactate production).
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:glossary subtopic:maod system:anaerobic
src: Ch 13 "Gauging Aerobic and Anaerobic Energy Production"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-038] Match (burning a match)

- "Burning a match" = a hard effort in which you had to dig deep. You start the day with a full matchbook; each attack or hard effort burns one. Matchbook size differs by cyclist; nobody has infinite matches. Burn them at the right time: if burned prematurely you will not win.
- Four goals: (1) figure out exactly what a match is for you, (2) figure out the size of your matchbook, (3) increase the number of matches, (4) burn them at the right time in the race.
- Working definition: an effort going over threshold power by at least 20 percent and held at least 1 minute. Longer efforts need a lower percentage above threshold. No exact definition; individual.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:matches sport:cycling metric:power
src: Ch 6 "What Is a Match?"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-039] Maximal Accumulated O2 Deficit (MOAD/MAOD)

The difference between the rate of oxygen uptake (VO2) and the rate of O2 demand at the onset of supra-maximal (more than 100 percent of VO2max) exercise continued to fatigue. Currently considered the "gold standard" for measuring anaerobic capacity. (The source spells the acronym both MOAD and MAOD.)
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:maod sport:cycling system:anaerobic
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-040] Maximal heart rate; Maximal neuromuscular power

- Maximal heart rate: the maximal rate at which your heart can beat per minute.
- Maximal neuromuscular power: the maximal power you can generate under optimal conditions (e.g., at the right cadence).
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:maximal-values sport:cycling metric:hr,power
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-041] Maximal Lactate Steady State (MLSS)

The highest exercise intensity at which blood lactate levels remain essentially constant over time. Comparable to FTP, and closer to what most coaches and athletes mistakenly call "LT."
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:mlss sport:cycling system:lactate
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-042] Maximal Power Available (MPA) and Fitness Signature (Xert)

- Maximal Power Available (MPA): a dynamic metric developed by Xert that estimates in real time the maximum power a cyclist can produce at any given moment during a ride. Calculated second by second using current power, work performed above Threshold Power, and work performed below Threshold Power. At the start of a ride, MPA equals Peak Power; it drops as fatigue accumulates during efforts above Threshold Power and is restored when effort drops below Threshold Power.
- Fitness Signature: the three-parameter model defining the rider's physiological profile in Xert:
  1. Peak Power (PP): peak 1-second power.
  2. High Intensity Energy (HIE): capacity for work above threshold (analogous to anaerobic capacity).
  3. Threshold Power (TP): functional threshold power (FTP).
tags: type:definition level:advanced subtopic:xert subtopic:mpa subtopic:fitness-signature sport:cycling metric:power
src: Ch 6 "Predictive Power Analysis" | Fig 6.18
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-043] Mean Maximal Power (MMP)

A plot of an athlete's best average power for each second (or duration) of a ride, or aggregated across multiple rides over time. Over larger datasets (six months to an entire year), the MMP curve models the Power Duration Curve, reflects personal abilities, and reveals the athlete's phenotype, strengths, and weaknesses across different physiological systems.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:mean-maximal-power sport:cycling metric:power
src: Ch 6 "Mean Maximal Power"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-044] Mean Maximal Power (MMP) Curve

A plot of an athlete's peak power outputs achieved across every time duration (from 1 second to multi-hour efforts) over a selected time span. It displays the rate of wattage decay as duration increases and identifies the exact durations where decay accelerates. The shape of the curve and its slope changes distinguish training levels, relative strengths and weaknesses, and rider phenotypes (sprinter, time trialist, climber). Best viewed on a semi-logarithmic time axis.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:mean-maximal-power subtopic:mmp-curve
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 "Changes in Mean Maximal Power"

### [TRPM-C05-045] Mean Maximal Power (MMP) Periodic Chart

A chart compiling data from every ride over an extended calendar timeframe, tracking best power outputs for specific selected durations (such as peak 5 seconds, peak 1 minute, peak 5 minutes, and peak 20 minutes). When smoothed over seven-day intervals (week-by-week view), each point represents the peak power achieved for that duration during that week, illustrating seasonal fitness progression, peaks, and declines.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:mean-maximal-power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 "Changes in Mean Maximal Power"

### [TRPM-C05-046] Mean maximal power; MMP Curve; MMP Periodic Chart

- Mean maximal power: your highest average power for a particular duration.
- MMP Curve: the curve of all your average best watts over each second of time, from zero seconds to the longest ride completed.
- MMP Periodic Chart: a chart of specific average best power for a certain time period, e.g., a line graph of your best 5 seconds for each ride over the entire year.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:mean-maximal-power sport:cycling metric:power
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-047] Micro-bursts; Match; Prime; Big-ring and Small-ring sprint

- Micro-bursts (micro-intervals): intervals with very short work and rest periods (e.g., 15 s on, 15 s off).
- Match: a tremendous amount of energy expended in a short time, e.g., attacking in a race; "burning a match" is actually expending it.
- Prime: a special prize given to the winner of a designated lap in a race.
- Big-ring sprint: maximal effort in the largest chainring, usually 53 teeth.
- Small-ring sprint: maximal effort in the smallest inner chainring, usually 39 to 42 teeth.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:racing-terms sport:cycling
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-048] Modeled FTP (mFTP)

The power level on the Power Duration Curve at which the curve flattens horizontally.
- First-edition FTP definition: "the highest power a rider can maintain in a quasi-steady-state without fatiguing."
- Before FTP, threshold was poorly defined (lactate, anaerobic, ventilatory thresholds; a review from the early 2000s presented over 30 validated definitions). FTP removed the need for lab assessment, standardized the definition, and made threshold a practical, functional measure.
- The one-hour criterion fit common US events (one-hour criteriums, 40 km TTs) and underlies IF 1.0 and 100 TSS points (Ch 7). It overweighted the idea that FTP must be an exact one-hour all-out effort.
- Critical Power (CP; Monod and Scherrer) uses multiple shorter tests to find an effort sustainable for a very long time without fatigue. It is often very close to FTP and sustainable for anywhere from 40 to 75 minutes.
- mFTP may correspond to a maximal 45-, 60- or 75-min effort depending on the individual and fitness; on average about one hour. Deviation from 60 min is another distinguishing characteristic of a rider.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power level:all subtopic:mftp subtopic:critical-power
src: Ch 8 "Modeled Functional Threshold Power (mFTP)"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-049] Modeled Functional Threshold Power (mFTP)

Modeled FTP (mFTP) is an estimate of Functional Threshold Power calculated by computer modeling (specifically the Power Duration Model in TrainingPeaks WKO4 software) from an athlete's historical power data without requiring formal testing. By default, it is calculated from the trailing 90 days of data. Like all models, it is a snapshot of reality dependent on the quality, quantity, and recency of data; it serves as a convenient point of triangulation alongside formal field testing.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:mftp sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Determining Functional Threshold Power

### [TRPM-C05-050] Non-Functional Over-Reaching (NFOR)

A state reached when Acute Training Load (ATL) remains greater than Chronic Training Load (CTL) for too long, in which an athlete can still train but can no longer improve and becomes progressively more fatigued. If allowed to continue, non-functional over-reaching can lead to overtraining syndrome.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:overtraining metric:load
src: Ch 9 "Guidelines for Optimal CTL and ATL"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-051] Normalized Power (NP)

An adjusted power for a ride or segment (longer than 30 s). It is the wattage you would have averaged had you pedaled smoothly for the entire effort, the power your body thought it was doing. It estimates the constant power that could be maintained for the same physiological cost as the variable power actually produced. Integral to IF and TSS.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:normalized-power level:all
src: Ch 7 | p. n/a

### [TRPM-C05-052] Onset of Blood Lactate (OBLA)

The exercise intensity corresponding to a blood lactate concentration of 4 millimoles per liter. Generally close to, but may be significantly higher or lower than, an individual's MLSS or FTP.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:obla sport:cycling system:lactate
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-053] Overreached vs Overtrained

- Overreached: an acute state of fatigue and diminished performance from a brief period of excessive training relative to what you normally do. Riders often call themselves "overtrained" but have usually just overreached; performance recovers after just a few days of rest or reduced training.
- Overtrained: a chronic state of overreaching from which recovery takes a long time, usually over 30 days. Also called Overtraining Syndrome (OTS).
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:overreaching sport:cycling metric:load
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-054] Pedal force and torque terms: AEPF, CPV, Maximal effective pedal force, Kurtotic Index

- Average Effective Pedal Force (AEPF): average force applied to the pedal that causes the crank to turn.
- Circumferential Pedal Velocity (CPV): speed at which the pedal travels around the circle; determines the speed at which muscles must contract to produce force and power.
- Maximal effective pedal force: the maximal force that isolates the height (peak) of each torque curve for each leg.
- Kurtotic Index (KI): indicator of the "peakedness" of force application during the downstroke. Higher value (closer to 5) = rider "punches" down harder for a briefer time; lower value (closer to 3) = same power by "pushing" for longer.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:pedal-force sport:cycling metric:power
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-055] Pedaling smoothness, Ppeak, Torque efficiency

- Pedaling smoothness (bilateral power meters): mean power over one crank cycle divided by peak power in that cycle, Pavg/Ppeak. Lower = larger Ppeak relative to Pavg. Example: Pavg=200W, Ppeak=1500, PS=13 percent (big peaks, small average); smoother: Pavg=350W, Ppeak=700W, PS=50 percent.
- Ppeak: peak power applied during a single pedal cycle. Misnamed "Pmax" in ANT+ pedaling metrics and seen as Pmax in Garmin head units; see Pmax for the correct definition.
- Torque efficiency (Garmin ANT+ bilateral metric): sum of positive and negative power over a single stroke divided by positive power. Example: P+=150W, P-=30W; TE=100x(150+-30)/150=100(120/150)=80%. Also used in Pioneer power meter with a slightly different formula.
tags: type:definition type:formula level:all subtopic:pedaling-metrics sport:cycling metric:power
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-056] Performance Manager

Analysis available in WKO and other software that quantifies changes in acute and chronic training load and hence training stress balance over time, helping build fitness and peak at the appropriate time while minimizing the risk of overtraining, illness, or injury.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:performance-manager sport:cycling metric:load
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-057] Phenotype (cycling)

The unique physiological and performance profile of a cyclist, determined by the pattern of relative strengths and weaknesses across the target durations of the Power Profile (5 s, 1 min, 5 min, FTP) and the Power Duration Curve. The authors describe four classic phenotypes: All-Rounder (horizontal profile across categories), Sprinter (downward-sloping profile), Time Trialist / Climber / Steady-State Rider (upward-sloping profile), and Pursuiter (inverted-V profile).
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:phenotype subtopic:power-profile
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "Defining Your Phenotype" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C05-058] Phenotyping or Rider Phenotyping

The objective classification of a cyclist as a sprinter, pursuiter, all-rounder, or time trialer based on quantitative analysis of the shape of their individual power duration relationship.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:phenotyping sport:cycling
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-059] Pmax

Greatest amount of power that can be generated for a very short period of time, with a minimum of one full pedal revolution with both legs; essentially the true maximal power an individual can produce on the bicycle. Analogous to the Power Profile neuromuscular power, but more rigorously defined.
- Neuromuscular power was stipulated at 5 s because older power meters had anomalous spikes and aliasing; 5 s minimized these.
- With improved meters and WKO4 analysis, a single-pedal-revolution maximum is feasible. It minimizes fatigue and overlapping energy-system contributions and is captured more often in daily data.
- The 5-s value can still be obtained from actual efforts or interpolated from the Power Duration Curve.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:neuromuscular metric:power level:all subtopic:pmax
src: Ch 8 "Pmax"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-060] Power

The rate of doing work, where work is equal to force times distance.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:power sport:cycling metric:power
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-061] Power Balance (ANT+)

A metric defined under the ANT+ standard .fit file format that measures average power from each individual leg and calculates the percentage that each contributes to total power. It does not account for opposing negative power (power absorbed on the upstroke) and can therefore misstate true bilateral dominance.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:bilateral-power level:all
src: Ch 7 "Interpreting Bilateral Power Data"

### [TRPM-C05-062] Power Duration Curve

A curve based on the power duration (PD) model, a mathematical model that uses all of an athlete's data to give insight into their unique physiology, allowing more comprehensive insight into the correct training needed.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:power-duration sport:cycling metric:power
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-063] Power Duration Curve (PDC)

A mathematically calculated curve of best fit along the Mean Maximal Power Curve, using the relationship between time to exhaustion and work rate in anaerobic and aerobic exercise plus personal power data, to chart power over all time periods. Derived from Mean Maximal Power (actual best outputs for every duration; Chapter 6). Basis for metrics such as functional reserve capacity, stamina and time to exhaustion; shows theoretical best power for a given time so you can see where it hasn't actually been achieved. Replaced the earlier-edition Fatigue Profile (which tested durations beyond the four Power Profile ones, e.g., distinguishing a snap sprinter from a diesel sprinter who starts 350 m out and holds power).
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:power-duration-curve subtopic:mean-maximal-power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "The Power Duration Curve" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C05-064] Power Profile

A profiling method and standardized classification chart that indexes a cyclist's maximal power output across four target durations—5 seconds (neuromuscular power), 1 minute (anaerobic capacity), 5 minutes (maximal oxygen uptake / VO2max), and functional threshold power (lactate threshold)—expressed in watts per kilogram (W/kg). Its primary function is to quantify an individual rider's relative strengths and weaknesses across physiological systems to guide training and event selection, rather than ranking them solely by racing category.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:power-profile metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "The Power Profile", "Target Durations" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C05-065] Power Profile and Power Profile table

- Power Profile: the specific measurements of power (watts) per kilogram generated at 5 seconds, 1 minute, 5 minutes, and FTP, revealing relative strengths and weaknesses of a cyclist.
- Power Profile table: a table categorizing the watts per kilogram needed to be successful in each category of racing.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:power-profile sport:cycling metric:power
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-066] Prime

An intermediate sprint with a prize contested during a criterium or road race.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:glossary subtopic:criterium
src: Ch 13 "Pacing in a Criterium"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-067] Quadrant Analysis

Analysis of a force-velocity scatterplot (AEPF vs CPV) divided into four quadrants by the AEPF and CPV at threshold power (and associated cadence). It focuses on the outliers/peripheral points of a power file, showing the neuromuscular demands of rides and races and the specificity of training.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:quadrant-analysis level:all
src: Ch 7

### [TRPM-C05-068] Rate of perceived exertion (RPE)

An individual's subjective evaluation of how intense or strenuous an exercise intensity feels. Typically rated on a linear 20-point or a nonlinear 10-point scale, both developed by Dr. Gunnar Borg.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:rpe sport:cycling metric:rpe
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-069] Recording rate vs display rate

Recording rate: rate at which the head unit or phone records data (faster is better). Display rate: rate at which data are averaged and updated on the display (rolling averages of 3, 5, 10, 20 s).
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:recording-display-rate
src: Ch 2

### [TRPM-C05-070] Repeatability; Specificity

- Repeatability: the ability to repeat a certain effort many times without loss in power.
- Specificity: exercise-physiology concept that adaptations to training tend to be specific, or unique, to the particular demands imposed.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:specificity sport:cycling
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-071] Running Training Stress Score (rTSS)

A metric developed by Dr. Stephen McGregor to quantify training stress from running, designed to create a level playing field with cycling TSS so that 100 points on the bike represents the same amount of training stress as 100 points on the run. Because running incurs additional musculoskeletal-structural stress from gravitational foot-strike impact, roughly 45 minutes of running at functional threshold pace (FTp), or a flat-out 15 km race, produces 100 rTSS (equivalent to 1 hour at FTP on the bicycle).
tags: type:definition sport:running,triathlon metric:load,pace level:all subtopic:rtss
src: Ch 12 "Timing Your Fitness Peak"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-072] Sit on

To draft directly behind the rider or riders in front without taking a turn pulling or helping in the workload. Recommended when riding in a breakaway at an intensity above functional threshold power to avoid blowing up and getting dropped.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:glossary subtopic:tactics subtopic:breakaway
src: Ch 13 intro; Ch 13 "Pacing in a Criterium"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-073] Smart recording

A default recording setting on Garmin head units that varies recording frequency—capturing fewer data points when traveling in a straight line and more points when turning. Designed to conserve memory for GPS tracking, but unsuitable for power-meter data collection because it drops critical power data points.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:recording-rate metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Set a High Recording Rate"

### [TRPM-C05-074] Stacking

Term coined by Coach Gale Bernhardt: adding workouts over the weekend to make up for those lost earlier in the week; similar to weekend-warrior mentality.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:stacking
src: Ch 10

### [TRPM-C05-075] Stamina

A measure of resistance to fatigue during prolonged, moderate-intensity exercise. In the Power Duration model it refers to the tail of the curve beyond mFTP, where the curve flattens slightly a second time. Expressed as a percentage: the time an athlete can ride at a sub-FTP level before fatiguing.
- 100 percent: the rider would never fatigue. Below 50 percent: fatigues quickly. The higher the score, the flatter the tail.
- Most riders: 75 to 85 percent.
- Track over time to see increasing or decreasing ability to resist fatigue at sub-FTP power.
tags: type:definition type:threshold sport:cycling system:aerobic metric:power level:all subtopic:stamina
src: Ch 8 "Stamina"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-076] Stochastic

Technically "varying randomly." Used for the marked power fluctuations when riding outdoors. Such variations are generally not truly random but arise from ever-changing resistances (hills, wind).
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:stochastic sport:cycling
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-077] Sweet Spot

A small area of intensity characterized by 88–93 percent of one's FTP.
tags: type:definition type:zones level:all subtopic:sweet-spot sport:cycling metric:power
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-078] Sweet Spot (Level 4a)

A training intensity level introduced in the individualized power levels (iLevels) framework, situated between upper Tempo and lower Threshold at 88–95% of FTP. It is designed to target physiological adaptations at the intersection of aerobic endurance and threshold development.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:sweet-spot sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power
src: Ch 3 | Individualized Power Levels or iLevels

### [TRPM-C05-079] Sweet Spot (Sub-Threshold)

An exercise intensity located at approximately 88–94% of Functional Threshold Power (FTP)—on the cusp of Level 3 (Tempo) and Level 4 (Lactate Threshold). In the iLevels system, it is formalized as Level 4a (88–95% of FTP). It represents the optimal balance point between maximal physiological training effect (increase in threshold power) and manageable physiological strain, allowing sufficient training volume (duration) to push FTP higher without premature exhaustion.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power level:intermediate subtopic:sweet-spot subtopic:ilevels
src: Ch 5 "Lower Level 4: Sub-Threshold, or the Sweet Spot", "iLevel Workouts" | Figure 5.2, Table 5.2
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-080] Tempo (or Fartlek)

From Swedish, "speed play"; workouts performed at an intensity "up tempo" from what a rider normally trains at when riding at a comfortable level.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:tempo sport:cycling
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-081] Thermodynamic efficiency (cycling)

As used in this book: the ability to process food and convert it into energy when cycling. For most trained cyclists it is on the order of 20–25 percent. The rest becomes waste heat.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:efficiency level:all
src: Ch 1 sidebar "What Is a Kilojoule?"

### [TRPM-C05-082] Threshold heart rate

The heart rate corresponding to functional threshold power.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:threshold-heart-rate sport:cycling metric:hr system:threshold
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-083] Time to Exhaustion (TTE)

The duration for which the mFTP power level can be maintained. It varies between and within individuals and describes a rider beyond the Power Profile and FTP.
- Figure 8.2 example: two riders with nearly the same Pmax (~1,300 W) and mFTP (~320 W). Rider 1 has TTE 34:23, Pmax 1,305 W, FRC 16.9 kJ. Rider 2 has TTE 52:40, Pmax 1,371 W, FRC 15.1 kJ.
- Rider 1's curve falls away sooner and steeper; Rider 2 has more sustainable power.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power level:all subtopic:tte
src: Ch 8 "Time to Exhaustion (TTE)" | Fig 8.2
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-084] Training Stress Balance (TSB)

Term coined by cycling coach Dave Harris. TSB = CTL – ATL. Compares recent (acute) training with historical (chronic) training. Because gain factors are eliminated, TSB is not a predictor of actual performance but an indicator of how fully adapted the individual is to recent training load, i.e., how fresh. Positive TSB = more freshness; negative TSB = more fatigue (tired from high load, CTL and ATL may be too high). TSB can be viewed as a predictor of form.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:tsb metric:load
src: Ch 9
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-085] Training Stress Score (TSS)

A composite number that takes into account the duration and intensity of a workout to arrive at a single estimate of the overall training load and physiological stress created by that session. Conceptually modeled after the heart rate–based training impulse (TRIMP).
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:tss sport:cycling metric:load
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-086] Variability Index (VI)

Term coined by Charles Howe. VI = Normalized Power / average power. The more variable the ride (after 30-s smoothing), the higher the VI. A larger gap between NP and average means a more variable, less continuously aerobic effort.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:variability-index level:all
src: Ch 7

### [TRPM-C05-087] Virtual Elevation

A field-testing method developed by Dr. Robert Chung that uses point-by-point speed and power data, along with total mass and cycling physics equations, to mathematically back-calculate the apparent topographical elevation profile of a course. By iteratively adjusting assumed values of $C_\text{d}A$ and $C_\text{rr}$ until repeated passes over the same route produce an identical ("level") elevation profile, aerodynamic drag and rolling resistance can be derived without wind tunnels or velodromes.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:advanced subtopic:glossary subtopic:virtual-elevation subtopic:aero-testing
src: Ch 13 "Virtual Elevation Method"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C05-088] VO2max

The maximal rate of whole-body oxygen uptake achievable during exercise. Primarily limited by the cardiovascular system's ability to deliver oxygen-carrying blood to exercising muscle; considered the best measure of cardiovascular fitness and sets the upper limit to aerobic power production.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:vo2max sport:cycling system:vo2max
src: Glossary

### [TRPM-C05-089] WAC score

Williams, Allen, Coggan score: assigns a rider a score for all time durations against the world's best and adjusts for age; a variation on the Power Profile chart. A score of 74 means performing at 74% of world champion's best wattage for that duration. Bill: age-adjusted peak 74 at 22:48 (year-to-date unadjusted peak 57); at 5 h drops 43%.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling subtopic:wac-score
src: Ch 10 | Fig 10.12

### [TRPM-C05-090] Wobbling

The average distance the bicycle deviates from the rider's center of mass, both side-to-side and front-to-back, during a period of time selected by the user. Measured outside the laboratory by an accelerometer (in the PowerPod/iBike) and plotted as (1) side-to-side vs front-to-back, and (2) front-to-back vs crank angle.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:wobbling metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Opposing Forces Method"

### [TRPM-C05-091] Zeroing (Zero offset)

The process of establishing a zero-load baseline on the power meter so that the head unit recognizes no-load as zero watts, attributing any subsequent load to the rider. Performed before every ride or after mechanical trauma by waking the meter without load and selecting zero (labeled "calibration" on Garmin devices). Distinct from true factory calibration.
tags: type:definition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:zero-offset metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Zero Your Power Meter"

## C06 Quantitative Reference

### [TRPM-C06-001] Table 3.1 Power-Based Training Levels (Coggan Classic)

| Level | Description | % of FTP* | % of FTHR* | RPE** | Typical Duration of Continuous Ride | Typical Duration of Interval Effort |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Active Recovery | <55 | <68 | <2 | 30–90 min. | N/A |
| 2 | Endurance | 56–75 | 69–83 | 2–3 | 60–300 min. | N/A |
| 3 | Tempo | 76–90 | 84–94 | 3–4 | 60–180 min. | N/A |
| 4 | Lactate Threshold | 91–105 | 95–105 | 4–5 | N/A | 8–30 min. |
| 5 | VO₂max | 106–120 | >106 | 6–7 | N/A | 3–8 min. |
| 6 | Anaerobic Capacity | 121–150 | N/A | >7 | N/A | 30 sec.–3 min. |
| 7 | Neuromuscular Power | N/A | N/A | Maximal | N/A | <30 sec. |
*Percentage of average power and average HR are at functional threshold.
**RPE uses 10-point Borg Scale (see Table 3.3).
tags: type:zones level:all subtopic:coggan-levels sport:cycling metric:power,hr,rpe
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Table 3.1

### [TRPM-C06-002] Table 3.2 Expected Physiological and Performance Adaptations for Levels 1–7

Plus signs = magnitude of adaptation for a given dose of training (more + = greater). Columns: L1 Active Recovery, L2 Endurance, L3 Tempo, L4 Lactate Threshold, L5 VO₂max, L6 Anaerobic Capacity, L7 Neuromuscular Power. Blank = none listed. Values listed in order L1 | L2 | L3 | L4 | L5 | L6 | L7.

| Adaptation | L1 | L2 | L3 | L4 | L5 | L6 | L7 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Increased plasma volume | | + | ++ | +++ | ++++ | + | |
| Increased muscle mitochondrial enzymes | | ++ | +++ | ++++ | ++ | + | |
| Increased lactate threshold | | ++ | +++ | ++++ | ++ | + | |
| Increased muscle glycogen storage | | ++ | ++++ | +++ | ++ | + | |
| Hypertrophy of slow-twitch muscle fibers | | + | ++ | ++ | +++ | + | |
| Increased muscle capillarization | | + | ++ | ++ | +++ | + | |
| Interconversion of fast-twitch fibers (Type IIx → Type IIa) | | ++ | +++ | +++ | ++ | + | |
| Increased stroke volume/maximal cardiac output | | + | ++ | +++ | ++++ | + | |
| Increased VO₂max | | + | ++ | +++ | ++++ | + | |
| Increased muscle high-energy phosphate (ATP/PCr) stores | | | | | | + | ++ |
| Increased anaerobic capacity ("lactate tolerance") | | | | | + | +++ | + |
| Hypertrophy of fast-twitch fibers | | | | | | + | ++ |
| Increased neuromuscular power | | | | | | + | +++ |
Adaptations are influenced by initial fitness, workout duration, time between interval efforts, and other factors.
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:adaptations-by-level sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Table 3.2

### [TRPM-C06-003] Table 3.3 Perceived Exertion Scale (Borg 10-point category ratio)

| Rating | Description |
|---|---|
| 0 | Nothing at all |
| 0.5 | Extremely weak |
| 1 | Very weak |
| 2 | Weak (light) |
| 3 | Moderate |
| 4 | Somewhat strong |
| 5 | Strong (heavy) |
| 6–7 | Very strong |
| 8–10 | Extremely strong |
| • | Maximal |
Use: Table 3.1 uses Borg's 10-point category ratio scale, not the 20-point scale, because it recognizes the nonlinear response of many physiological variables (e.g., blood and muscle lactate) and better indicates overall effort. Perceived exertion rises over time even at constant power, so the RPE values refer to effort early in a session or interval series.

### [TRPM-C06-004] Table 3.4 Calculating Power Levels Based on FTP of 290 Watts (Joe Athlete)

| Level | Description | % of FTP | Power (W) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Active Recovery | <55 | 1–160 |
| 2 | Endurance | 56–75 | 161–218 |
| 3 | Tempo | 76–90 | 219–261 |
| 4 | Lactate Threshold | 91–105 | 262–305 |
| 5 | VO₂max | 106–120 | 306–348 |
| 6 | Anaerobic Capacity | 121–150 | 349–435 |
| 7 | Neuromuscular Power | N/A | N/A |
Method: multiply FTP by each percentage (e.g., 290 × 0.55 = 159.5, so Level 1 is 1–160 W). Insert your own FTP to build your table.
tags: type:zones type:formula level:all subtopic:power-level-calculation sport:cycling metric:power
src: Ch 3 | Table 3.4

### [TRPM-C06-005] Individualized Power Levels (iLevels): rationale

- Origin: after the WKO software release, data from outlier riders showed power levels not fitting the Classic model. Example: Level 5 (106–120% FTP) typically allows ~115–118% of FTP for 5 minutes, but some riders held 150% of FTP for 5 min (high Level 6 range per Table 3.1) and needed new levels beyond Level 4. Others struggled to hold 105% for 5 min and needed levels scaled down. Riders with similar FTPs can have radically different short-term power.
- Goal of WKO4: individualized levels via a rule-based system rooted in exercise physiology applied to past data.
- iLevels diverge from Classic above FTP. Levels 1–4 remain wide enough for most riders and are expressed as a percentage of software-modeled FTP.
- Level 4a (88–95% of FTP), the Sweet Spot, was added because it is popular for training (see Chapter 5).
- There are nine iLevels and eight cut-off points, all determined by the Power Duration model (Chapter 8). Cut-offs are inflection/transition points in the fitted Power Duration Curve where significant changes occur in the primary performance parameter stressed (e.g., Pmax versus FRC); exact method is "secret sauce."
tags: type:methodology level:advanced subtopic:ilevels sport:cycling metric:power
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C06-009] Table 5.1 When to Stop Intervals

| Interval | Average Drop in Power |
| :--- | :--- |
| 20 min. | 3–5% |
| 10 min. | 4–6% |
| 5 min. | 5–7% |
| 3 min. | 8–9% |
| 2 min. | 10–12% |
| 1 min. | 10–12% |
| 30 sec. | 12–15% |
| 15 sec. | 10–15% (15–20% peak power) |

Note: The drop in power is based on the number of watts achieved in the third effort. E.g., with 5-minute intervals, a rider is ready for a rest when the average watts for an interval are 5–7 percent lower than for the third effort.
Basis: review of more than 3,000 power-meter files and work with more than 1,000 athletes; validated since with hundreds more.

Fig 5.6 example PD curve: Pmax 1,207 W, mFTP 259 W. Fig 5.7 caption (different example): Level 7 Pmax 0:00–0:08, 7a 0:08–0:24, 6 FRC 0:24–1:36, 5 FRC/FTP 1:36–18:15. FRC (shaded area under PD curve above mFTP) can be raised by (1) raising Pmax, (2) focusing on a specific period such as 0:30–1:30, or (3) shifting the whole PD curve right.

### [TRPM-C06-014] Table 7.3 Impact of Training Stress on Fatigue

| TSS | Intensity | Recovery Status |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| <150 | Low | Recovery is generally complete by the following day. |
| 150–300 | Moderate | Some residual fatigue may be present the next day, but recovery is generally complete by the second day. |
| 300–450 | High | Some residual fatigue may be present even after 2 days. |
| >450 | Very high | Residual fatigue lasting for several days is likely. |
Approximate guide only. Tolerance differs: Peter Sagan might do 300–400 TSS at IF 0.85 for 21 days in a row and keep improving, whereas a beginner may be severely overreached by 2 days at that level. A very high TSS from one race/session means one or more rest days should be scheduled.

### [TRPM-C06-018] Guidelines for optimal CTL and ATL

- "Optimal" training load lies at CTL between 100 and 150 TSS per day (experience across elite amateur track cyclists, masters marathon MTB racers, pro road racers). CTL <100 usually feels like undertraining (could tolerate more if time/life stress allowed; not necessarily better performance). Few if any athletes sustain a long-term average >150 TSS/day.
- Hardest stages of hors catégorie stage races typically generate TSS of 200–300; average daily TSS of the Tour is lowered by rest days and short stages. Tour riders maintain CTL 150–160 most of the year, 170–180 after the Tour; a hypothetical genetic CTL limit may be 180–200.
- CTL pattern: a 4–6 week plateau in CTL while training focus and performance are constant = stagnation (just maintaining, overload principle not applied).
- Raising CTL too rapidly often leads to illness or overreaching/overtraining. Any sudden increase (training camp, stage race) must be followed by reduced training/recovery. Letting TSB rise toward neutrality periodically is needed. If ATL > CTL for too long: non-functional overreaching (can train but not improve, increasingly fatigued); too long there may lead to overtraining syndrome requiring months or a year off the bike.
- Most cyclists can increase CTL at 3–7 TSS/day per week. Ramp >7 TSS/day per week for more than 4 weeks in a row may cause an overreaching downward spiral. >7 for 1 week, possibly 2 weeks, is fine; longer risks chronic overreaching.
- More mature (training age) riders can drive CTL higher and ramp steeper; newer cyclists must be more careful (injury, non-functional overreaching). Approaching CTL 100, back off ramp rate. Example: Tour riders from CTL 150 to 170 = ~7 TSS/day ramp.
- Short ramp increases are advisable only in periods <2 weeks (e.g., a weeklong camp) and need a rest week afterward (Table 9.2). A 22-year-old pro handles faster ramps than a 55-year-old masters rider. Hover over the ATL line across a 7-day period in the PMC to check whether short-term load rises too fast.
tags: type:threshold sport:cycling level:all subtopic:ctl-ramp-rate metric:load
src: Ch 9 "Guidelines for Optimal CTL and ATL"

### [TRPM-C06-019] Table 9.2 CTL Ramp Rate as a Function of Training Load

| Duration | Training Age | CTL <100 Ramp Rate | CTL >100 Ramp Rate |
| Long-term (14–28 days) | 5+ years | 7–10 TSS/day | 5–7 TSS/day |
| Long-term (14–28 days) | 3–5 years | 5–8 TSS/day | 3–6 TSS/day |
| Long-term (14–28 days) | 1–3 years | 4–7 TSS/day | 3–5 TSS/day |
| Long-term (14–28 days) | <1 year | 3–5 TSS/day | 3–4 TSS/day |
| Short-term (<14 days) | 5+ years | 14–20 TSS/day | 10–14 TSS/day |
| Short-term (<14 days) | 3–5 years | 10–16 TSS/day | 6–12 TSS/day |
| Short-term (<14 days) | 1–3 years | 8–14 TSS/day | 6–12 TSS/day |
| Short-term (<14 days) | <1 year | 6–10 TSS/day | 6–8 TSS/day |
Increasing ramp at these rates for one week, especially two, requires a rest week afterward.

### [TRPM-C06-020] Guidelines for optimal TSB for racing (survey of ~200 athletes)

- TSB 0 = neither fresh nor fatigued. A negative but rising TSB means recovering from a deep hole; good performance is still possible. Precise values depend on the individual and time constants (defaults CTL 42, ATL 7); do not apply literally.
- Survey of ~200 athletes: TSB when they set a Personal Best for Power at any duration. Bands: Negative (< –30 to –10), Neutral (–10 to +10), Positive (>+10).
- All durations (Fig 9.6): PBs occurred from –30 to +30, many in neutral; majority between –5 and +15; highest frequency bin 10 to 15 (~19%).
- <5 minutes (Fig 9.7): PBs at more positive TSB; highest bin 10 to 15 (>20%), moderately elevated through higher positives; low at TSB under –10. Reason: short efforts need more neuromuscular power and anaerobic capacity, highest when well rested. Short, heavily anaerobic events (track, BMX, short hill climbs): be very well rested.
- ≥5 minutes (Fig 9.8): bell curve from –10 to +25, peak 5 to 10 (~15%) followed by 10 to 15; athletes as likely to PB at –10 as at +25. A TSB of –10 likely means recovering from lower, not getting more fatigued; a +10 PB was probably reached by creeping up to it. Longer events (road race, MTB race, stage race): do not taper/rest too much or the peak window may be missed.
- Conclusion: the more anaerobic the event, the more important freshness; the more aerobic, the more important fitness. Plan for TSB to reach the optimal point at the right time; use the PMC retrospectively to replicate or modify past peaks.
- Fig 9.5 example: four athlete bests occurred at TSB –62 to –130, after nearly 3 weeks of positive TSB; positive TSB not always necessary for a best ride.
tags: type:threshold sport:cycling level:all subtopic:tsb-race-day phase:peak phase:taper
src: Ch 9 "Guidelines for Optimal TSB for Racing" | Figs 9.5–9.8
evidence: author-experience (survey of ~200 athletes)

### [TRPM-C06-023] Sources of mechanical resistance in track pursuit

Distribution of power output required to overcome resistive forces in an individual pursuit on a banked velodrome (3–4 km, 3.5–5 minutes duration):

| Source of Resistance | Share of Total Power Output |
|---|---|
| Aerodynamic drag (wind resistance) | ~85% |
| Inertia (acceleration) | ~8% |
| Rolling resistance ($C_\text{rr}$) | ~5% |
| Drivetrain and bearing friction | ~2% |

Implication: Aerodynamic positioning and frontal area reduction ($C_\text{d}A$) represent the dominant determinants of speed at pursuit velocities.
tags: type:zones type:threshold sport:cycling level:elite subtopic:pursuit subtopic:aerodynamics metric:power

### [TRPM-C06-024] Appendix Workout Guide: level headings and workout-code conventions

The Appendix lists every workout by power-based training level. For power zone detail it refers to Table 3.1 (p. 32). In the workout tables, RI = rest interval between efforts. Each workout has a code, a name and an approximate time. Columns are Time | Description | % of FTP | % of FTHR.

Level headings as printed:
- LEVEL 1: ACTIVE RECOVERY (<55 OF FTP), codes AR-W
- LEVEL 2: ENDURANCE (56–75% OF FTP), codes END-W
- LEVEL 3: TEMPO (76–90% OF FTP), codes TEMP-W
- LEVELS 3 AND 4: SWEET SPOT (88–93% OF FTP), codes SubLT-W
- LEVEL 4: LACTATE THRESHOLD (91–105% OF FTP), codes LT-W
- LEVEL 5: VO2MAX (106–120% OF FTP), codes VO2-W
- LEVEL 6: ANAEROBIC CAPACITY (121–150% OF FTP), codes AC-W
- LEVEL 7: NEUROMUSCULAR POWER, codes NP-W
- ALL LEVELS: KITCHEN SINK, codes WATTS-W. Many rides work several energy systems at once, usually with emphasis on one. The kitchen-sink rides throw in "everything but the kitchen sink". They are challenging and a good test of overall fatigue resistance.
- PYRAMID WORKOUTS (P-W), PERFORMANCE TESTING (TEST), RACE PREPARATION (RACE-W), SWIM WORKOUTS (SWIM-W), RUN WORKOUTS (RUN-W; stretch after finishing these workouts).
See library L2 for each workout.

## C07 Testing & Assessment

### [TRPM-C07-001] Mobile testing lab and indoor training with power

- A power meter allows periodic fitness testing (for example monthly) on the bike. It can replace some costly lab testing for serious racers.
- It measures force put into the pedals, not just cardiovascular strain. Athletes differ: some improve faster with shorter efforts, others with longer ones.
- Indoor trainer: outdoor wattage fluctuates moment to moment with wind, hills and dogs. Indoors, intervals can be held in exact wattage zones.
- Indoor power files are "cleaner", without terrain or group-riding fluctuations, so effort periods are easier to analyze.
- Intensity can be compared with on-road efforts.
- Computerized trainers can re-create a ridden racecourse from downloaded data.
tags: type:test sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:indoor-training level:all
src: Ch 1 "Create a Mobile Testing Lab"; "Enhance Indoor Training"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C07-002] Retest frequency and conditions for FTP testing

- Repeat the FTP test every six to eight weeks to assess fitness, track changes and decide whether to change the program.
- Always test on the same stretch of road or on an indoor trainer, at close to the same time of day, in similar weather.
- Minimize external influences (stress, lack of sleep) so results are comparable.
- Overall frequency: assessing FTP six to eight times a year is probably sufficient. Suggested timing: mid-winter training; near the start of serious outdoor training (baseline); partway through the pre-competition period; a couple of times in season for peak fitness; after peak fitness to see how far you have fallen.
- How often FTP changes depends on training history. A beginner or someone returning after a long break may see large, rapid changes. A long-time or year-round conditioned rider sees much less variation.
tags: type:test type:monitoring level:all subtopic:ftp-retest-frequency sport:cycling metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Step 1

### [TRPM-C07-003] Six ways to estimate FTP (in order of increasing complexity)

1. Power Frequency Distribution Chart: upload all training data and view the chart (Fig 3.1). Because exercising above threshold is strenuous, there is often a noticeable drop-off in time spent above FTP. Works best if the period includes high-intensity training/racing. The drop-off is often clearer with smaller power bins (5 or 10 W). The same approach works even better for identifying spontaneously achieved maximal HR. Example in Fig 3.1: drop-off between the 300–315 W bin (18:09, 5.2% of ride) and the 316–330 W bin (3.2%).
2. Routine Steady Power: evaluate steady power produced in long hard efforts (LT intervals, long climbs). In software, add a horizontal gridline to a stacked graph and look for places where power is quasi-constant for minutes; adjust the line to home in. Fig 3.2 example: shaded 2:37:00–2:41:45, gridline ~325 W, FTP estimated 325 W.
3. Normalized Power: examine NP during hard mass-start races of approximately one hour (NP is explained in Chapter 7). Many programs compute NP even without a threshold entered, so analyzing several race files may be the quickest route to an estimate.
4. One-Hour Time Trial: the most direct estimate. Inspect the graph (perhaps smoothed) to see if pacing was even. If started too hard and faded, average power somewhat underestimates true threshold power.
5. Mathematical Modeling (Critical Power / Power Duration model): plot total work (joules) against duration (seconds) for a series of all-out efforts lasting between 3 and 30 minutes and fit a straight line; the slope is critical power (CP). If the guidelines are followed, CP is essentially equal to FTP. If CP is calculated from tests that are too short, CP is higher than FTP and overestimates the true maximal metabolic steady state. The CP paradigm shows whether changes in anaerobic or aerobic function (or both) drive performance improvements, to plan future training.
6. Computer Modeling: TrainingPeaks WKO4 (developed in part by the authors) has a Power Duration Model producing "modeled FTP" (mFTP). Like all models it depends on the quality, quantity and accuracy of data; it can be quite accurate or wildly inaccurate, so use it as another point of triangulation. Default: built on the last 90 days of data; with no hard effort in 90 days it most likely underestimates; if a best effort drops off on day 91, mFTP can take a surprisingly large single-day drop. Advantage for new users: quick estimate without formal testing.
- The authors maintain that the best way to determine FTP is through testing ("testing is training and training is testing"). Recommended start: a ride specifically designed to find threshold, the FTP test.
tags: type:test level:all subtopic:ftp-estimation sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Determining Functional Threshold Power; Figs 3.1, 3.2

### [TRPM-C07-004] TEST Functional Threshold Power (20-minute protocol)

Purpose: estimate FTP from the highest watts you can average over a substantial period (protocol developed by Hunter Allen). Use the same warm-up, at the same intensity, every time. Warm-up and recovery intervals are at about 65% of FTP (Endurance pace). After the three fast pedaling intervals, the true warm-up begins.

| | Time | Description | % of FTP | % of FTHR |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Warm-up | 20 min. | Endurance pace | 65 | 70 |
| Warm-up | 3 × 1 min. (1 min. RI) | Fast pedaling, 100 rpm | N/A | N/A |
| Warm-up | 5 min. | Easy riding | 65 | <70 |
| Main set | 5 min. | All-out effort | max | >106 |
| Main set | 10 min. | Easy riding | 65 | <70 |
| Main set | 20 min. | Time trial | max | 99–105 |
| Cooldown | 10–15 min. | Easy riding | 65 | <70 |
FTP = Functional Threshold Power. FTHR = Functional Threshold Heart Rate. N/A = Not Applicable. RI = recovery interval.

5-minute all-out effort: "punch it and hold it." Start high but not so high that you die at the end; keep a little in reserve to kick in the last minute. Goals: (1) open the legs; (2) capture VO2max power (Level 5); (3) use a majority of aerobic work capacity to reduce the influence of aerobic ability in the 20-minute test, so the next effort is more truly representative of FTP.

20-minute time trial: use a fairly flat road allowing a strong steady effort. Do not start too hard; get up to speed and hold it. If you have never done one, try a steady climb or slight headwind that forces maximum effort throughout. Goal: highest average wattage over the whole period. Running out of energy prevents true maximal steady-state power. Better to be a little under your believed FTP for the first 2 minutes, build speed, and ride at maximum in the last 3 minutes. Finish at an easy pace.
tags: type:test type:protocol level:all subtopic:ftp-test sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power,hr
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Testing Protocol: Functional Threshold Power

### [TRPM-C07-005] TEST Power Profile (5 s, 1 min, 5 min plus FTP)

Purpose: with the Chapter 3 FTP test result, establishes a complete profile of strengths and weaknesses.
Conditions: road section without stop signs/intersections, repeatable for retesting; same conditions each time (wind, weather, training phase); do it right after a rest week (fresh, relatively fit); same warm-up routine on the way.
Protocol (% of FTP / % of FTHR):
- Warm-up 45 min easy riding: 65 / <70
- Warm-up 3 × 1 min (1 min RI), fast pedaling 110 rpm: 80–90 / 80–90
- Warm-up 1 min Test, all-out: sprint out of the saddle 15 s, then sprint next 15 s in the saddle and hang on for the last 30 s: >150 / >106
- Warm-up 10 min easy: 70–80 / <75
- Main set 5 min Test: start hard but pace yourself; maintain power for the entire effort and push hard in the last 30 s: 115–120+ / >106
- Main set 10 min easy: 70–80 / <75
- Main set 2 × 15 sec (2 min RI): out of the saddle, sprint at maximum, starting from 15 mph in the big chainring: Max / N/A
- Cooldown 15 min easy: 60–70 / <68
Notes: majority of warm-up in Levels 2 and 3 (Endurance/Tempo, Table 3.1); at least 10 min easy pedaling before first effort. For test efforts ignore cadence, HR, etc.; only the clock matters, drive hard to the end ("just do the work, and what happens, happens"). Record efforts as intervals on the power meter if possible. Cooldown to reach at least another 300–500 kilojoules of Level 2 riding. Then download data and pick the best watts for each time period (5 s, 1 min, 5 min; FTP from Ch 3 test).
tags: type:test sport:cycling level:all subtopic:power-profile metric:power metric:hr
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "Testing Protocol: Power Profile" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C07-006] Measuring maximal pedal force (AEPF-CPV line) with a power meter

- Perform a series of brief maximal efforts (less than 10 s each so fatigue does not limit power), seated or standing depending on the question, recording power and cadence at least once per second. Example in book: 10 maximal standing-start efforts.
- Calculate AEPF and CPV for the efforts and plot them: an essentially straight line sloping down left to right. Y-intercept = estimated maximal AEPF at zero CPV (strength); x-intercept = theoretical maximal CPV at zero AEPF.
- Caution: due to sampling differences (time-based vs event-based), not all power meters give data accurate enough for this test.
tags: type:test sport:cycling system:neuromuscular subtopic:aepf subtopic:max-force level:all
src: Ch 7 | Fig 7.13

### [TRPM-C07-007] Pedaling Asymmetry Hill Climbing Test

Purpose: learn whether you truly have a leg-strength discrepancy or whether pedaling form or bike position needs altering.
- Four different days; each day three 5-minute intervals at Level 5 (VO2max) power (roughly 113–115 percent of FTP): 1st standing the entire time, 2nd seated the entire time, 3rd alternating standing and sitting at will.
- Outside, same course and same start locations every day, conditions as identical as possible (to capture power while the bike is moving).
- Day 1: no emphasis on either leg, climb naturally. Day 2: emphasize the leg that releases less power to see if GPR/GPA balance. Day 3: emphasize the left leg only in all efforts. Day 4: emphasize the right leg only.
- After each test download and analyze with the GPR-GPA Mean Maximal Power Curve; Day 1 analysis is critical to know which leg to emphasize on Day 2. Emphasize the weaker leg strongly (overemphasize downstroke force); the emphasized leg should feel on fire.
Case (Joe Athlete): standing, left released more (GPA about equal); seated, right released more and left absorbed slightly more. Day 2: standing with right emphasis nearly balanced GPR; seated with left emphasis just barely equaled the right, suggesting a muscular discrepancy in seated climbing muscles (gluteals, biceps femoris) rather than positional. Day 4 insight: he wobbled the bike right and shifted weight left on left downstrokes but not the mirror on right; shifting weight right during right downstroke gained 10–20 W from the right leg and the same in total average power standing.
tags: type:test sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:bilateral-power subtopic:asymmetry level:all
evidence: case-study
src: Ch 7 | Figs 7.18-7.21

### [TRPM-C07-008] Fatigue resistance: fresh vs after 2,000 kJ

- Fatigue resistance across all energy systems affects race outcomes (sprint wins, a 10 W higher average over a 7-hour MTB race, long gran fondo climbs). The more fatigue resistant, the stronger at the end.
- Method: compare the Power Duration Curve (best power fresh) against best power after a given amount of work (e.g., 300 W for 20 min fresh, but what after 2,000 kJ?). Sprint fatigue resistance can be assessed too, to decide between focusing on explosiveness or on fatigue resistance.
- Bill Masters (Fig 13.8): best 5-min power 261 W fresh vs 202 W after 2,000 kJ; best 20-min power 241 W vs 187 W. Remedy: longer rides plus more "kitchen sink" workouts in long rides, which improved fatigued-state 5- and 20-min power.
- Recommended for rides/races longer than 3 hours: review this chart to guide the training plan.
tags: type:test type:monitoring sport:cycling level:intermediate subtopic:fatigue-resistance subtopic:power-duration-curve metric:power metric:load
src: Ch 13 "Fatigue Resistance", Fig 13.8
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C07-009] Sprint fatigue resistance charts (Figs 13.9 and 13.10)

- The chart plots the first minute of the Power Duration Curve with percent power degradation from maximum.
- Sprinter (Fig 13.9): 1,274 W peak; at 15 s power is 1,085 W (down 20%); at 35 s down about 40-41% (the book also says 41 percent), about a 600 m sprint, still above 800 W. Labels: Fatigue Resistance 41.0%, Mean Max Power 803 W. Implication: start the sprint 300+ m out, or surprise from 600 m, and hold it to the line.
- Bill Masters (Fig 13.10): never exceeds 888 W; loses 20% by 6 s and 43% by 20 s. Labels: Fatigue Resistance 59.7%, Mean Max Power 381 W. Conclusion: he is not a sprinter and should stick with climbing.
tags: type:test type:evidence sport:cycling level:intermediate subtopic:sprint subtopic:fatigue-resistance metric:power
src: Ch 13, Figs 13.9-13.10
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C07-010] Aerodynamic field testing (CdA): methods

- Who: road time trialists, track racers (pursuit, kilometer, 500 m), triathletes. With a good venue and care, CdA (Cd x frontal area A, in m2) can be measured as precisely as in a wind tunnel, to choose position or equipment ("free speed"). Commercial tools: Aero Stick (Alphamantis, acquired by Garmin in 2017), AeroPod (iBike). Three approaches:
1. Constant speed (or constant power) method: one or more passes in both directions on windless road (better a running track or indoor velodrome) of known grade, at constant speed measuring power (or constant power measuring speed). Distance at least 500 m. Correct power for variations in start/end speed (kinetic energy): choose start/end points with equal speed; on a track use a straightaway. Correct for grade (potential energy) if not flat. SRM, Quarq, Polar: correct for assumed drivetrain efficiency (PowerTap measures downstream of the chain, so no correction). Corrected power is a rough aero indicator if speed, air density and rolling resistance are constant. Complete all measurements in a short time; otherwise record barometric pressure, temperature, humidity and wind to compute air density (calculators at www.analyticcycling.com). Must assume Crr and weigh rider plus equipment accurately; different tires/wheels, pressure or temperature complicate results.
2. Regression method: 6-9 passes at varied speeds (about 5 to 15 m/s, or the highest sustainable and reproducible), equal runs each direction if course is not level or has residual wind. Plot corrected steady-state power (Y) vs speed (X): Y = aX + bX3, where a = rolling resistance (N) and b is proportional to one-half x air density (g/L) x CdA. Linear form: divide power by speed and plot against speed squared (m2/s2); intercept = rolling resistance, slope proportional to one-half x air density x CdA. Advantages: separates rolling resistance from aero drag (e.g., whether wheels are faster because aero or because tires roll better), and gives a more precise CdA from multiple measurements.
3. Virtual elevation method (Dr. Robert Chung): no constraints on speed or power; ride the same stretch repeatedly as normal. Use speed, power, total mass and cycling physics to solve for the virtual elevation profile, assuming starting CdA and Crr, then adjust them to visually level the plot (or use formal statistical fits). Short out-and-back or loop courses work best. Avoid braking (or exclude it); a half-pipe course (short road with moderately steep hills at each end) minimizes braking and gives the speed variation needed to separate CdA and Crr. Advantages: slope need not be zero or constant, profile need not be known, point-by-point data reveal anomalies (wind gust, position change). Drawback: CdA and Crr can be hard to tell apart, complicating comparison of tires/wheels or locations; keep tires, pressure, surface, temperature and mass the same.
tags: type:test type:methodology sport:cycling sport:triathlon level:advanced subtopic:cda subtopic:aero-testing metric:power
src: Ch 13 "Aerodynamic Testing with a Power Meter"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C07-011] Track: evaluating physical performance across tracks (Table 14.1)

Power shows how well you functioned as an engine regardless of track speed; recording conditions allows comparing performances across tracks. Example: two 3 km pursuits by the same rider; the altitude race was nearly 6 s faster, but adjusted for reduced atmospheric pressure (aero drag and aerobic power) predicted power and time equal the sea-level result, so performances are equivalent.
Table 14.1 Performance Comparison for Track Cyclist, 3 km Pursuit
| | Sea Level | Altitude |
| Total time (min:sec) | 03:55.9 | 03:50.2 |
| Average power (W) | 386 | 360 |
| Time for 1st lap | 00:30.0 | 00:30.0 |
| Time laps 2–9 | 03:25.9 | 03:20.2 |
| Average power laps 2–9 (W) | 358 | 333 |
| Air density (g/mL) | 1.159 | 0.97 |
| CdA (m²) | 0.24 | 0.24 |
| Sea-level equivalent power (W) | 358 | 358 |
| Sea-level equivalent time | 03:55.9 | 03:55.9 |
Tracks: sea level 333.3 m concrete; altitude very similar. [Air density units printed as g/mL; likely g/L — Likely unit/typo error in source — recorded as printed]

### [TRPM-C07-012] TEST Monthly Testing Protocol (performance test ride)

Approx. time 2 hr. After warming up: three 1-min fast pedaling efforts, 1 min rest after each; 3 min easy. 5-min all-out effort; 10 min easy. The next set tests aerobic capacity: two 1-min efforts with 5 min between; 5 min easy. Third set tests neuromuscular power: three 20-sec "super jumps": jump hard out of the saddle and sprint the full 20 sec; 3 min rest after each; after the last pedal easy 10 min. Finally a 20-min time trial. Cool down.
Table:
- Warm-up | 15 min. | Easy riding | 56–75 | 69–83
- | 3 × 1 min. (1 min. RI) | Fast pedaling, 100+ rpm | <80 | <90
- | 3 min. | Easy riding | <68 | <75
- Main set | 5 min. | All-out | max | >105
- | 10 min. | Active Recovery | <68 | <75
- | 2 × 1 min. (5 min. RI) | Anaerobic Capacity | 125–150 | >105
- | 5 min. | Recovery | <68 | <75
- | 3 × 20 sec. (3 min. RI) | Super jumps | max | N/A
- | 10 min. | Easy riding | 56–75 | 69–83
- | 20 min. | TT Test | 100 | 99–103
- Cooldown | 15 min. | Easy riding | 56–75 | 69–83
(The text calls the 1-min set the aerobic capacity test; the table labels it Anaerobic Capacity.)
