## C08 Methodology & Progression

### [TRPM-C08-001] Coach-athlete collaboration with power data

- A coach's job is to determine the dose (training) that elicits the response (adaptation). Seeing the athlete's response lets the coach prescribe more accurately and react faster to fitness changes.
- The data show what the athlete did in races and training rides, including things the coach could not learn even by racing with the athlete.
- Accountability: the coach will see if only 5 of 10 prescribed efforts were done ("A power meter doesn't lie").
- Case (Sam Krieg):
  - Knowing the file would go to his coach, he completed a 50-minute threshold-power workout with cadence changes and power spikes, which he had thought impossible, and set a new 50-minute peak power.
  - He reframed a race after being dropped as a 30-minute threshold time trial, racing his power meter minute to minute. He caught the break and finished at the front of the remaining riders.
  - Lesson: if power and heart rate are stable at the prescribed wattage, keep going. The data can convince you that you are capable.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:coaching-accountability level:all
src: Ch 1 "Give Your Coach Better Information"
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C08-002] Three steps to begin power-based training

- Step 1: Estimate your Functional Threshold Power (FTP) with a first testing session. This gives a fitness baseline.
- Step 2: Establish your power-based training levels from FTP (Coggan Classic levels first; iLevels later if needed).
- Step 3: Go collect more data. Ride, learn the watts needed for local hills, find the best roads for specific intervals (e.g., a long gradual climb for steady Level 4 efforts), and learn your strengths and weaknesses.
- Recommended path: start with the Coggan Classic Levels; as you gain insight and data, decide whether to use iLevels.
tags: type:methodology level:beginner subtopic:getting-started sport:cycling metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Steps 1-3

### [TRPM-C08-003] Step 3: Go collect more data

- After testing and setting levels, ride and learn: how many watts local hills need, how hard to go in group rides to win a sprint.
- Identify roads suited to specific interval types holding a narrow wattage range (e.g., a long gradual climb for steady Level 4 work).
- Collecting data reveals strengths and weaknesses, preparing for the next step (Chapter 4).
tags: type:methodology level:beginner subtopic:data-collection sport:cycling metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Step 3

### [TRPM-C08-004] Power Profiling versus Power Duration Model

With Power Profiling, an athlete compares power across four durations (5 s, 1 min, 5 min, FTP) taken to represent Neuromuscular Power, Anaerobic Capacity, VO2max, and Lactate Threshold to determine actual peak power outputs and establish an initial phenotype.
With the Power Duration Curve Model, power is compared across all durations along the curve of best fit to reveal specific limiters in fatigue resistance.
Methodological relationship:
1. The Power Profile establishes the rider's initial phenotype and relative strengths/weaknesses across primary physiological systems.
2. The Power Duration Curve Model refines this analysis by identifying specific time frames where fatigue resistance is a limiter (e.g., where actual mean maximal power dips below the PDC, or where the PDC crosses category reference bands).
3. Both tools are used together to target training at physiological limiters and align racing goals with individual strengths.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling level:all subtopic:power-profile subtopic:power-duration-curve metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "Power Profiling versus Power Duration Model" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C08-005] Designing your own power workouts

The listed workouts are not exhaustive; hundreds of designs exist (over 100 in the Appendix). When designing, be aware of the levels addressed and their impact on the workout goal. Use Table 5.1 and the intervals-to-exhaustion rules. Goal of the chapter: help the athlete develop and revise an individual program rather than follow a one-size-fits-all plan.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling level:all subtopic:workout-design
src: Ch 5
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C08-006] Case study: timing TSB for multiple peaks (Matt)

Matt: 30-year-old Cat I, FTP 5.2 W/kg, 11 cyclocross races, low volume/high intensity, CTL not high in fall. Used progressive CTL rise: bring CTL up quickly, let it stabilize and grow slowly, then another big week of stress (ATL shock) to raise it again.
Lessons:
- A small decrease in CTL ramp rate can produce a positive TSB, a good predictor of good performance.
- Four best 20-min powers occurred end of January after a taper preceded by a large CTL increase; third-best 20-min came end of March right after a steep rise (12 TSS/day for 2 weeks) while TSB was still negative but climbing. TSB need not be positive for a peak; it needs to be climbing toward positive.
- Highest wattages came unplanned in January (placed 3rd overall in an early stage race); noticed only retrospectively.
- Illness after second Build in March: CTL rise of 12 TSS/day plus a very hard cold wet race (slight hypothermia) likely compromised immunity; CTL dropped from 122 to 90 during illness; TSB became very positive from lack of riding; underperformed at Tour of the Gila (early April goal). Third-best 20-min of season at masters nationals (2nd RR, 6th TT) came in the longest period of positive TSB (mid-July).
- Multiple-goal seasons are more complicated; knowing the story behind the chart is critical, so coaching art remains.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling level:advanced subtopic:multiple-peaks phase:peak
src: Ch 9 "Timing TSB for Multiple Peaks" | Figs 9.9, 9.10
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C08-007] Case study: timing TSB for a single peak (Dave)

Dave, 55, Cat II masters time trialist, FTP 5.2 W/kg, goal masters nationals (July). Coach Hunter built CTL gradually to ~100 at peak without digging a big TSB hole. CTL stabilized in May for a mid-May goal race and then climbed again for nationals. Short positive-TSB weekends gave personal bests in early-season key races. He won races at negative TSB (March–June) because fitness exceeded peers'. At nationals: best 20- and 60-min NP of the season (TSB ~+25; 2nd TT, 1st crit, 4th RR).
Worlds: CTL dropped during/after nationals (planned season end); nearly 6 weeks of positive TSB produced a 3-min PB of 462 W (6% above any previous 3-min best of the year). After deciding on worlds, a short 2-week large ATL dose rebuilt CTL to ~80 (near his sustainable load) without overdoing it, protecting the immune system. Tapered to TSB +35, top-10 in TT but only 4.9 W/kg, form not equal to mid-June. Hindsight: had worlds been known, CTL would not have been allowed to drop so much.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling level:masters subtopic:single-peak phase:taper phase:peak
src: Ch 9 "Timing TSB for a Single Peak" | Figs 9.11, 9.12
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C08-008] Rest week rules (Jill Week 4)

Rest week is essential: without it she will not reap the benefits of the previous 3 hard weeks nor recover for the next 2. Ride easy and really easy: watts under 62% of FTP, no speeding over 14 mph on a flat road. Many riders ride Endurance pace when they should ride even easier. Limit riding to less than 2 hours per day; weekday rides only 1 h 15 min; rest completely on some days (Jill: Monday, Wednesday, Sunday). Rest week before final peak week lets athlete be rested; good result the weekend before peak builds confidence (form plus confidence).
tags: type:principle sport:cycling phase:transition metric:power subtopic:rest-week
src: Ch 10 | Jill Week 4, Week 7
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C08-009] Bill's non-classic periodization (5 on, 1 off, 1 on, 1 off) and block commentary

Plan does NOT use "three weeks on, one week off": push 5 weeks, rest Week 6, hard Week 7, reduce Week 8; concentrated stress then rest mid-plan to adapt and reach next level in last four weeks; Weeks 9–11 challenge FTP/endurance; Week 12 taper. Rest in Week 8 raises Training Stress Balance given cumulative fatigue.
Weeks 1–4: jump into intensity (decent base): hill repeats Wed, FTP with bursts Thu, 4-h Endurance ride with FTP/Tempo/VO2max. Goal of Week 1 is to arrive at weekend fatigued and use it for long ride; if failing intervals he may back off intensity as long as he gets the work in (cumulative stress, not perfect intervals). Week 2: sprint workout Tue (six small-ring and six big-ring sprints; small ring = explosiveness, big ring = fatigue resistance in longer sprint); adjust for racing per Plans A/B. Week 3: with limited time cut the fluff (shorter warm-up/cool-down, shorter rest between intervals) provided prescribed wattages hit. Week 4: threshold: 2 × 20 min just below FTP with bursts Tue and Wed; crisscross on weekend.
Weeks 5–8: Week 5 Endurance focus, big 5-h Saturday with FTP, Tempo, some VO2max. Week 6 truly rest; power meter as governor; if Week 5 not hard enough, rest week not needed and time is lost. Week 7 VO2max work (right after rest week) incl. simulated short TTs Sunday; tough FTP/Sweet Spot Thursday.
Weeks 9–12: Week 9 every level: AC Monday, VO2max Tuesday, FTP Wednesday; intense workouts early in week when freshest. Week 10 Sweet Spot and FTP, two kitchen-sink weekend rides, intensity lowered after Week 9. Week 11 final workouts; weekend Endurance rides for confidence. Week 12: increase complex carbohydrate intake so muscle and liver glycogen full; at event pace by wattage and RPE (and heart rate): Sweet Spot on climbs, eat and drink, conserve on descents, stay with buddies on flats. If producing more power than ever with lower HR and RPE, maintain effort (do not retest in Week 12). All three metrics needed.
tags: type:periodization sport:cycling level:masters phase:taper subtopic:case-study-bill metric:power
src: Ch 10 | Bill Weeks 1-12
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C08-010] Steps to implement a power-based training plan

1. Find FTP (methods and test, pp. 26–31). 2. Use FTP to establish training levels. 3. Try sample workouts and collect data. 4. Understand data and charts. 5. Use Power Profile, Power Duration Curve and Quadrant Analysis to pinpoint strengths and weaknesses. 6. Assess time constraints. 7. Develop a plan targeting needed improvements for goal. 8. Review power data regularly. 9. Adjust FTP and levels as fitness progresses. 10. Refine plan for extra rest or adaptation.
Typical week template: Monday rest; Tue/Wed/Thu key workouts; Friday Active Recovery; Sat long ride/race; Sun medium ride/race; "three weeks on, one week off" default; retest FTP at least every eight weeks.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling level:all subtopic:implementation-steps
src: Ch 10 | "How to successfully implement..."
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C08-011] Specific triathlon training: two-pronged approach

- Triathlon is a highly aerobic sport: FTP is critical, but absolute stamina (endurance) is also paramount.
- An athlete may have a high FTP from training intensely only in one-hour sessions. That is fine for sprint triathlons only. Longer events require riding farther without major muscle fatigue.
- Workouts address FTP and endurance separately. A third advanced workout combines both. A fourth covers short, high-wattage efforts for unique courses. More workouts are in the Appendix.
See library: Workout Functional Threshold Power; Workout Endurance; Workout FTP and Endurance; Workout Anaerobic Capacity.
tags: type:methodology sport:triathlon system:threshold level:all subtopic:ftp-and-endurance
src: Ch 12 "Specific Training"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C08-012] Cyclocross weekly threshold work recommendation

Cyclocross demands a strong FTP, so traditional Level 4 FTP workouts are important. Recommended: in one workout each week, do four efforts at FTP of 10 minutes each, or three of 15 minutes each, or two of 20 minutes each. Together with the 30-30-30 workout. Improvement with power in CX hinges on mimicking the demands of upcoming races in training.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power level:all subtopic:cyclocross
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Cyclocross" | n/a

### [TRPM-C08-013] Ultra-endurance MTB: pacing differs from time trialing

Mountain biking is extreme in demand for high-force low-cadence efforts (Quadrant II); a power meter helps train in the correct quadrant with appropriate stress. Ultra-endurance MTB is like time trialing (little drafting; after about 15 minutes each rider is in own time trial), but the time-trial rule of not starting too hard does not apply. Holding back early is rarely going to help at the end because of the Allen effect.
Training needs: push muscular endurance progressively to handle the volume of contractions; solid force work in Quadrant II; finish with threshold work; combine with careful pacing and energy conservation. Physiological demands are similar to cross-country but greater in volume.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:ultra-endurance subtopic:pacing subtopic:mountain-bike
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Ultra-Endurance Mountain Biking" | n/a

### [TRPM-C08-014] Eight-step sequence for putting power-based training together

Step 1 – Collect data and determine threshold power: testing FTP when starting with a power meter is one of the most important steps; it defines the intensity of all training from then on. Gather ride data over local routes and races to learn what particular wattages mean in particular ride types. Repeat the FTP test every six to eight weeks, or whenever you think fitness has changed. (Ch 3)
Step 2 – Determine training levels: with threshold power known, training in the correct levels guides success. Understanding the physiology of each level lets you target weaknesses. (Ch 3)
Step 3 – Determine strengths and weaknesses: plot the Power Profile to see how you stack up against peers and which physiological systems to train. The Power Duration Curve shows whether a specific area of fatigue resistance needs improvement, helping make the "final 10 percent leap". (Ch 4)
Step 4 – Create workouts and begin training: build workouts aimed at specific power levels addressing your own needs and goals. To improve you must train hard; it will hurt. Hunter's saying: "Quitters never win, and winners never quit." (Ch 5)
Step 5 – Interpret your data: download every ride and race; correct interpretation guides next day's, month's and year's training. Spend time understanding what data say. (Ch 6)
Step 6 – Use the data analysis tools: Training Stress Score, Intensity Factor, Normalized Power, Functional Reserve Capacity, Pmax and Quadrant Analysis give further insight, define event demands and show additional success factors. The more unknowns eliminated, the better the chance of success. (Ch 7, 9, 10)
Step 7 – Race with your power meter: best data and efforts come from races, and some of the best data come from failures (learn why you failed and avoid repeating). Pacing is the most important component of success in the sport. (Ch 12, 13, 14)
Step 8 – Make changes to achieve your goals: power-meter training is about results and is worth doing only with a clear understanding of what needs doing; be ready and willing to change based on the steps above.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:ftp-retest-interval subtopic:workflow-summary
src: Epilogue | Steps 1-8
evidence: author-experience

## C09 Decision Rules & Heuristics

### [TRPM-C09-001] Race analysis with power data: lessons and rules

- The best data often come from races, where competition drives harder efforts than routine training.
- Review the file of a race where you were dropped, like a football coach reviewing game video, to find what to change.
- Case: an athlete dropped on a climb in the Gila Stage Race.
  - Hunter compared several races and found he was dropped whenever cadence fell below 70 rpm for more than 5 min while at threshold power.
  - The same athlete stayed with the same riders at or above threshold wattage when cadence was over 95 rpm.
  - Fix: the largest cog changed from the standard 23 teeth to 27 teeth, so he could spin over 100 rpm on the steepest climbs.
  - He stayed in the front group for the rest of the year.
  - Transferable lesson: look for cadence/gearing patterns that coincide with being dropped.
- Energy conservation: in thousands of files Hunter analyzed, consistent winners pedal less than the rest of the peloton. They sit in, watch, wait and hide from the wind, but pedal harder than the pack when they do pedal.
- Matches: a power meter shows when you "burned a match" (a very hard effort). You have only so many matches, so timing is everything.
- Check whether energy was spent in non-decisive parts of the race.
- Replay the race against the file to find tactical errors and what it would have taken to make the winning break or decisive split, then use that to focus training.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling metric:power,cadence subtopic:race-analysis subtopic:matches level:all
src: Ch 1 "Analyze Your Race"
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C09-002] Identifying strengths and weaknesses with power

- Before power meters, riders guessed at strengths and weaknesses and were often wrong, and guessing hurts improvement.
- Simple testing protocols plus experience in varied races and rides give a clearer picture. You cannot improve until you know your weaknesses.
- Example: a Category I racer in best 5-minute power but Category IV in best 20-minute power. This is fine for a track racer but disappointing for a road racer.
- Each racer differs and has different goals. Knowing strengths and weaknesses changes the focus of training.
- Questions the meter can answer: what happens at 105 percent of threshold power for more than 3 min, and do the lungs feel ready to explode?
tags: type:principle sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:strengths-weaknesses level:all
src: Ch 1 "Pinpoint Your Strengths and Weaknesses"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-003] Pacing with a power meter

- Use the meter on long rides, intervals, hill climbs and time trials to conserve or expend energy at the right times.
- Once you know FTP (see Chapter 3), you can hold it in a time trial or hill climb with confidence that you went as hard as possible.
- In time trials it provides a "ceiling" that prevents overexertion in the first 5 minutes, and a "carrot" when the going is hard.
- In mass-start races it helps conserve energy, judge whether the pace suits a breakaway attempt, and gauge what it takes to win.
- Course-simulation case (Randy Weintraub, Ironman Lake Placid, goal under 10 hours):
  - He rode the bike course near goal wattage and confirmed he averaged it and had energy left for the run.
  - He counted the hills longer than 2 minutes on the course.
  - He found home routes that mimic the course, riding into wind to simulate a longer climb.
  - He programmed the hill repeats and target wattages into his indoor trainer.
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling sport:triathlon metric:power subtopic:pacing level:all
src: Ch 1 "Pace Your Efforts"
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C09-004] Zero your power meter (calibration vs zeroing)

- Zeroing is like a bathroom scale: if it reads +5 lb before you step on, your weight is 5 lb heavier. The head unit must receive a signal from the meter with no load, taken as zero; any load above is attributed to the rider.
- Procedure: turn the meter on by turning the cranks, spinning pedals or rolling the wheel so it sends a signal to the head unit; once connected, zero it in head unit settings.
- Garmin calls it "calibration" (confusing): for a Garmin, to zero choose "calibration". True calibration means hanging a known weight (e.g., 50 kg) from the crank arm or pedal; complicated, best done at the factory, though many manufacturers publish the process.
- When: before every ride, and after any trauma (removing the rear wheel to load the bike in a car, bike falling so pedal/crank hits pavement, crash). Some units prompt before riding.
- Why: forgetting can skew all data (example: +23 watts).
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:zero-offset-calibration metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Zero Your Power Meter"

### [TRPM-C09-005] Don't trust a single sample: power spikes

- Erroneous samples appear as massive power spikes far beyond any wattage the rider can produce.
- Causes: cadence signal lost for a second; track stand at a stoplight inadvertently triggering the reed switch quickly and repeatedly; pedaling resumed on an uphill immediately after coasting downhill. Some meters are more susceptible by design.
- Software fix: post-ride software makes it easy to find and fix spikes by interpolation. Example: recorded sequence 213, 234, 242, 1,876, 254, 260, 267 → change 1,876 to 248 (splitting the difference between 242 and 254).
- Application: this vulnerability explains why the authors emphasize analyzing best 5-second power for neuromuscular capacity rather than an isolated 1-second peak, ensuring true maximum power is reflected.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:power-spikes metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Don't Trust a Single Sample"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-006] Average zeroes for power, but not for cadence

Set the head unit to include zero power in power averages (not pedaling = 0 W; important, see Chapter 4). Do NOT average zeros into cadence. Example: 5-min interval at 90 rpm, coasting 30 seconds: including the zero gives 81 rpm average, misleading; excluding it gives 90 rpm.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:averaging-zeros metric:power,cadence
src: Ch 2 "Average Zeroes for Power, but Not for Cadence"

### [TRPM-C09-007] Use the interval or lap button

Press lap just before starting an interval and again at its end so averages are recorded, viewable on the head unit and in software for comparison (needed for strategies in Chapter 5 that optimize number of intervals by average power). For micro-bursts, crisscross efforts or intervals with sub-goals, mark only the start and end of the larger interval (e.g., a 3-minute microburst interval: do not mark every 15 seconds), to capture the average of the whole effort.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:lap-button metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Use the Interval or Lap Button"

### [TRPM-C09-008] Heart rate guidelines relative to power levels

Relating power levels to HR zones is difficult because of the inherent variability of HR and individual differences in the power–HR relationship (even referenced to threshold power). Approximate HR guidelines in Table 3.1 are meant to be used alongside power, not instead of it.
Example (Joe): during Tempo (219–261 W) his HR may fluctuate from his Level 2 HR zone to his Level 4 HR zone while power stays at Tempo. Power vs HR comparison shows how fatigue, dehydration and low blood sugar may affect wattage dramatically even when HR is unaffected, or vice versa.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:power-vs-heart-rate sport:cycling metric:power,hr
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Heart Rate Guidelines; How to Determine Your Training Levels

### [TRPM-C09-009] Power distribution, variability and duration when applying levels

- Levels are based on average power, but the distribution of power matters. Mass-start race average power typically falls in Level 3, yet racing at Level 3 is more stressful than training at Level 3 because of greater variability and higher peaks.
- Due to soft-pedaling and coasting, average power on a hilly ride or group session is not equivalent to the same average on a flat or solo ride.
- Variability is partly built into the definitions of Levels 2 and 3; higher levels are more structured, limiting variation.
- Inverse power–duration relationship: shorter sessions/efforts fall toward the higher end of a level's range; longer ones toward the lower end.
- Classification example: 30 min Level 1 (warm-up) + 60 min Level 3 + 30 min Level 1 (cooldown) is best described as a Tempo session even though overall average power might fall in Level 2.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:variability-and-levels sport:cycling metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Other Issues

### [TRPM-C09-010] How to use your levels (Joe Athlete example)

- Train with a specific wattage range as the goal to improve specific areas. If VO2max power is the "hole," work in the Level 5 range (306–348 W for Joe).
- On recovery rides stay below the Level 1 ceiling (160 W for Joe); riding harder risks insufficient recovery.
- Knowing levels may reveal you were not training as hard as you could; make sure wattage does not drop below the targeted level.
- Compare HR zones with the new wattage levels to understand the relationship.
tags: type:decision-rule level:beginner subtopic:applying-levels sport:cycling metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | How to Determine Your Training Levels

### [TRPM-C09-011] How to read your Power Profile and define phenotype

- Find your peak/maximum average power for 5 s, 1 min, 5 min and FTP and locate the corresponding values in Table 4.1 (use the gender column). If between two values, assign the nearest ranking.
- Values must truly reflect your very best effort at that duration; otherwise the profile is distorted, leading to inappropriate training conclusions.
- The pattern of results defines your phenotype; it may shift slightly over time as you train weak areas.
- Performance is judged against the world's best, so road cyclists look weak in 5 s relative to match sprinters, and non-endurance track racers look low at 5 min/FTP.
- Revise the chart every four to six weeks and note changes from the previous training block.
- Race/ride to strengths: e.g., a world-class sprinter shouldn't expect to break the Mount Evans hill-climb record; genetics may limit some areas, but in most cases training can target specific goals.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:phenotype subtopic:power-profile
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "Defining Your Phenotype"; end of chapter | no page markers

### [TRPM-C09-012] Using the PDC: dips, crossings and fatigue resistance (Figs 4.2-4.4)

- Figure 4.2: blue line = actual Mean Maximal Curve (MMP); black line = PDC (best fit).
- Dips: look first where effort falls below the PDC. It may mean no all-out effort was done at that duration or a physiological weakness. Address with specific tests to improve the dips; refining them makes the model more accurate (Fig 4.3), improving prediction of FTP, Pmax, functional reserve capacity and stamina (Chapter 8).
- Caution: the PDC represents your very best effort, perhaps at peak fitness; you may not reach it at current fitness. If so, revisit when fitter.
- Fatigue resistance: where your PDC crosses different category curves (Power Profile categories plotted in the background, Fig 4.4) fatigue resistance needs improvement. Target those time frames and the 20-30-60 seconds that follow to keep the curve in line with the category or moving upward. Fig 4.4 callouts: about 0:10–0:40 and 0:40:00–2:00:00.
- Category lines in the chart are the lower border of each category (see category reference entry).
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:power-duration-curve subtopic:fatigue-resistance metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "The Power Duration Curve", Figs 4.2–4.4 | no page markers

### [TRPM-C09-013] Intervals to exhaustion: rule for when to stop intervals

- Answer to "how many intervals?": it depends on the workout goal, current fitness, how the session fits the big picture, and your ability to dig deep.
- Rule: the third effort is the "repeatable" interval. Ignore the first two (fresh) efforts because they use plentiful glycogen and anaerobic work capacity and are higher than can be repeated. Subtract the Table 5.1 percentage from the third-effort average watts. Stop when you cannot average at least that wattage, since intensity is then too low to stimulate adaptation.
- If long intervals allow only two total, the rule does not apply. With experience you learn the holdable wattage and need not discard the first two efforts, especially those over 3 minutes.
- Can be done fresh (maximizes interval count) or fatigued (fewer intervals, but still the optimal number for that workout).
- Authors hesitate: more research is needed; adjust to your situation, fitness and goals.
- Philosophy (Joe Friel): "An athlete should do the least amount of the most specific training that brings continual improvement" / "Train just enough for success." Power makes diminishing marginal returns quantifiable.
- Applies to every VO2max workout (Level 5).
tags: type:decision-rule type:principle sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:intervals-to-exhaustion subtopic:diminishing-returns
src: Ch 5
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-014] Worked example: eight 5-min VO2max intervals, FTP 300 W

- Goal: prepare for a race with eight 5-minute climbs. VO2max is stressed at 106–120% of FTP; FTP 300 W.
- VO2max adaptation needs a minimum effort of 3 minutes, maximum about 8 minutes; beyond 8 minutes 106–120% FTP is very hard to hold. 30 seconds at 120% is too short.
- Intervals: 1st 360 W, 2nd 350 W, 3rd 340 W (repeatable). Stop threshold = 340 − 5% (340 × 0.05 = 17) = 323 W.
- If the 6th is 320 W and a 7th cannot hold 310 W by the 2nd minute, he is below the intensity needed and should stop.
- Counter-example (Fig 5.1): 5-min VO2 intervals averaged 305, 314, 320, 309, 315 W. Third = 320, 5% drop = 304 W. The athlete stopped after 5 although the 4th and 5th were above 304 W; he could have done at least one more, probably several ("underachieved").
tags: type:methodology sport:cycling system:vo2max metric:power level:all subtopic:intervals-to-exhaustion
src: Ch 5 | Figure 5.1
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-015] Power meter setup and data cleaning before analysis

- Set the power meter to the smallest sampling interval available for the greatest recording accuracy. On some meters this limits total recording time, so use a higher (longer) recording interval for longer rides.
- Set the head unit power display to three-second-average power; this makes intervals easier to execute accurately.
- Zero the power meter before every ride. If the zero-offset is not zero at the start, the data are essentially worthless.
- Garmin head units call the zero-offset procedure "calibration", so on Garmin you must "calibrate" before each ride. Also make sure the meter is truly calibrated (correct usage of the term): many meters allow checking via a phone app, others have the process outlined on the manufacturer's website.
- Viewing ride graphs: use either no smoothing or five-second smoothing. Smoothing removes noise from inherent power-meter measurement issues (Ch 2) but can hide true peaks and valleys. Five-second smoothing loses little meaning and is easier on the eye.
- Remove erroneous data points. Example: max power listed as 2,000+ W when you have never exceeded 1,000 W in a sprint. Change the spike to 0 W or interpolate from the points before and after it.
- Record every ride and race and download every file; all data are significant.
tags: type:methodology level:all subtopic:data-hygiene subtopic:calibration subtopic:smoothing sport:cycling metric:power
src: Ch 6 "Adjusting the Calibrations and Settings"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-016] Power distribution chart: time not pedaling and the FTP drop-off

- Race power distribution: check time spent not pedaling (0 W). Most winning road racers do not pedal at least 15 percent of the time. If pedaling more than 85 percent of the time in a race, rethink where you sit in the peloton. The name of the game is saving energy.
- Winners' charts (Fig 6.1): huge time at 0 W, lots of time at Tempo to FTP, not much above it, plus some time well over FTP. In the example, lots of time between 250 and 340 W and lots of time over 360 W. Winners pedal the least, but when they pedal they pedal the hardest.
- On a normal training ride, time not pedaling is not important; time in power zones is.
- Estimating FTP from the distribution: look for the drop-off in time around FTP. In Fig 6.1 there is a huge drop from the 390-400 W bin to the 400-410 W bin, so FTP is a ballpark guess at the upper end of the 390-400 bar, unlikely over 400 W. Reason: you can ride right up to FTP but not much over, so the bin nearest the FTP edge has the largest share of time. Winning racers spend lots of time just at FTP but not much above, because they pace well. (See Ch 3 FTP determination.)
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:power-distribution subtopic:ftp-estimation sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power
src: Ch 6 "Power Distribution" | Fig 6.1
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-017] Checking time in power levels and updating levels as FTP changes

- Examine the power distribution chart by level to confirm you trained long enough in the level you targeted (especially a weakness).
- Levels must be set correctly from current FTP for the calculations to be valid.
- Levels are absolute watts calculated from FTP, so they shift as fitness changes. Example: FTP 200 W in January gives Tempo (Level 3, 76-90 percent of FTP) = 152-180 W. If FTP is 260 W by June, Level 3 = 197-234 W, and 152-180 W is now Level 2 (Endurance). Update settings and training as fitness changes.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:time-in-level subtopic:zone-update sport:cycling metric:power
src: Ch 6 "Time in Power Levels"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-018] Caveat on time-in-level for power; heart rate distribution

- Caveat: alternating 15 s at 400 W and 15 s at 100 W for an hour gives 30 min "in" the 400 W level, a power you could hold only about 4 minutes continuously. Time in level hides how long each foray lasts.
- This is less an issue with heart rate because (1) HR lags power, so short forays are smoothed/averaged by physiology, and (2) HR can be used only for levels below maximum HR or VO2max intensity. So "time in level" is much more meaningful for heart rate than for power.
- Heart rate distribution: with a large enough dataset including time at and above FTP, find the heart-rate threshold by setting the bin range to 3-5 bpm and locating the step-down. Fig 6.2: sharp decline from the 160-165 bpm bin to the 165-170 bpm bin.
tags: type:decision-rule type:caution level:all subtopic:heart-rate-distribution subtopic:time-in-level sport:cycling metric:hr metric:power
src: Ch 6 "Heart Rate Distribution", "One Caveat..." | Fig 6.2
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-019] Cadence distribution chart

- View in 5 rpm increments. It shows how much time is spent in each cadence range; useful when trying to raise or lower cadence for a workout or physiological change, and may hint at the percentage of fast-twitch vs slow-twitch fibers.
- Fig 6.3 example rider: large percentage of time over 90 rpm, even larger over 100 rpm (clue to muscle type); spike at 0-5 rpm = time not pedaling (about 13-14 percent).
- The cadence chart measures time not pedaling more accurately than the power distribution chart, because bins are smaller (1-5 rpm, 6-10 rpm...) than the 0-20 W power bin.
tags: type:monitoring level:all subtopic:cadence-distribution sport:cycling metric:cadence
src: Ch 6 "Cadence Distribution" | Fig 6.3
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-020] Mean Maximal Power (MMP) curve: reading and cautions

- MMP curve = plot of best average power for each duration in the ride (each second of the ride's best averages). Use it to confirm correct training level in workouts and, in intense race files, to pinpoint strengths and weaknesses.
- Over a large dataset (six months or more) the curve shape reflects personal abilities; slope changes show reliance on different physiological systems and help model the Power Duration Curve and decide rider phenotype.
- Example: slope consistent from 20 s to 2.5 min, then lower angle from 2.5 to 25 min suggests a shift at 2.5 min from anaerobic capacity to VO2max and lactate systems.
- A distinct hump at about 3-6 min = strength at VO2max power. A plateau = possible weakness in the duration where it occurs.
- Compare like durations: a workout of 5-minute efforts says little about 6-minute power; specific 6-minute efforts are needed. Without sprints, the max-power point is not your true max; without threshold intervals, the lactate-threshold point is not your true FTP. Power Duration model accuracy depends on enough data points across all durations.
- For the big picture, build the MMP curve over a long period, up to a full year.
- Fig 6.4: pure sprinter (153 lb woman, world-class sprinter on Power Profile): about 1330 W at 1 s, above 1190 W through about 12 s, steep decline after about 12-15 s (about 700 W at 28 s, below 420 W by 1:38, about 280 W from 5:49 to 23:56, below 210 W by 49:46). Note the long time at very high power and the break point of neuromuscular decline.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:mean-maximal-power subtopic:power-duration subtopic:phenotype sport:cycling metric:power
src: Ch 6 "Mean Maximal Power" | Fig 6.4
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-021] Stacked vs horizontal graph views and workflow for marking files

- Stacked view: raw data, not interpolated, smoothed or compressed on the X-axis; good for precise analysis and defining exact start/finish of each work period, but spans a long horizontal distance and hides long-term changes (e.g., small power decrease over 60+ minutes).
- Horizontal view: all data on one screen, compressed on time axis; the big-picture view, good for picking out intervals and spotting a decrease in power at the end of a long climb (Figs 6.5 vs 6.6).
- Recommended workflow: start in horizontal view with no smoothing or 5-second smoothing; mark segments of interest (create a range/"lap"), then switch to stacked view to define exact boundaries.
- Channels to display: road ride = watts, heart rate, speed (speed shows uphill vs flat). Cadence-focused workouts = replace speed with cadence to see the power-cadence relationship. Trainer ride = watts, HR, cadence (speed irrelevant).
- Finding unmarked efforts: e.g., a 25-minute mountain-bike climb shows high HR, low speed, fairly constant power (smooth power despite trail undulations). Marking a small power spike at the start of an effort while riding makes it easy to find later (Fig 6.7). Gridlines can be placed at threshold HR and threshold watts.
tags: type:methodology level:all subtopic:graph-views subtopic:file-markup sport:cycling metric:power metric:hr
src: Ch 6 "Stacked and Horizontal Views", "Areas of Interest" | Figs 6.5-6.7
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-022] Interval shape and meaning (twin peak, pacing, repeatability) - elite track racer example

- The shape of each interval's power line shows pacing, freshness/fatigue, and whether you gave your all. Comparing shapes helps set pacing strategy, a bigger wattage goal, or the number of intervals.
- Example (Figs 6.8-6.12): elite Canadian track racer, first time doing this workout: seven 3-minute maximal efforts, 3 minutes easy between, no wattage goal given (to avoid coloring results; also to see how quickly she adapted and expose weaknesses). Expected VO2max power 315-325 W. Done on an indoor trainer.
- Typical pattern, a "twin peak interval": hard start, power drops about 1 min in, then rises again at the end.
- Interval 1: peak at start and end with a valley in the middle = perfect pacing for a fresh first interval (conservative, then throttled at the end); the textbook way to create best average watts. Data: avg 324 W, max 355, avg HR 172, 58 kJ, NP 323.
- Interval 2: went too hard from the start (initial spike above 432 W; max 435), blew up, no power at the end. Avg 324 W, 59 kJ, avg HR 174.
- Intervals 3-6: dialed in, as hard as she could go. Interval 6: avg 312 W, max 363, NP 312, 56 kJ, avg HR 172. A power "hole" after the first minute of each (about 1:17:05-1:17:25 in Fig 6.11) = difficulty transitioning from purely anaerobic capacity (fast-twitch) to a larger share of slow-twitch; her anaerobic capacity depth is not deep enough. The drop-off should occur nearer the 2:30 mark (1:18:30).
- Interval 7: profile similar to Interval 1 (avg 318 W, max 417, NP 317, 57 kJ, avg HR 175). Since it is the last effort, this means she held back and could have done more repeats before diminishing returns (ties to intervals-to-exhaustion). Fatigue would instead show quicker drop-off and no ability to raise power at the end.
- Repeatability: always compare the 2nd and 3rd intervals with the last, because the first interval is done fresh and its wattage cannot be repeated. Here repeatability was good (low drop-off from Intervals 2 and 3 to 7); good average pursuit power and cruising power in pursuit.
- Recommendation: practice pacing, hold back slightly at the start, focus on minute 1 to minute 2 to remove the quick power drop, then dig deep in the last minute.

src: Ch 6 "Interval Shape and Meaning" | Figs 6.8-6.12

- Overlay/sort analysis also works for the same race in different years: select exactly the same range of data ("apples with apples").

| Year 1 - 2nd place | 1:49:41 | 1,651 | 184.8 (1.008) | 302 W (1.2) | 0 | 896 | 251 | 97 | 180 | 145 | 31 | 135 | 90 | 10.4 | 43.3 | 21.2 |
| Year 2 - WIN | 1:45:02 | 1,647 | 165.7 (0.973) | 292 W (1.12) | 0 | 878 | 261 | 94 | 182 | 147 | 33 | 127 | 82 | 7.8 | 42.1 | 21.7 |
- Year 2: HR higher, cadence lower, power smoother than Year 1. He won with a solo breakaway after an attack 45 minutes in; the HR climb marks the breakaway start.
- Interpretation: in Year 1 he was in a breakaway with three other riders, so cadence was likely higher (matching surges between pulls). Lower cadence solo may mean he prefers higher force at lower cadence when alone at FTP. (Force-cadence principle continues in Ch 7.)
