### [TRPM-C09-025] Scatterplots: power vs cadence, left/right balance, cadence vs speed

- A scatterplot relates two data channels (HR vs power, L/R power balance, power vs cadence).
- Power (Y) vs cadence (X) shows the cadence of highest power outputs and your upper cadence limit for high wattage. Fig 6.14 rider does not pedal faster than about 105 rpm in races (possible limiter); cadence decreases as power increases, indicating a shift from the cardiovascular system to the muscular system.
- Bilateral balance (Fig 6.15): reveals which leg a rider favors across wattage. Some riders "save" a leg at low watts for short high-power needs. Example: left-leg dominant below FTP (about 262 W marked), right leg more at VO2max and Anaerobic Capacity levels, back to left leg at Neuromuscular Power.
- Cadence vs speed (Fig 6.16, cyclocross): linear relationship up to about 100-105 rpm, then he shifts to a harder gear to keep increasing speed; faster speeds without faster cadence. May indicate a need to improve pedaling speed and/or muscular strength to use bigger gears at higher speeds.
- Only two variables to go faster besides aerodynamics: pedal harder or pedal faster. Scatterplots help decide which (or both) to set goals in.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:scatterplot subtopic:power-balance subtopic:cadence sport:cycling metric:power metric:cadence
src: Ch 6 "Scatterplots" | Figs 6.14-6.16
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-026] Finding and counting matches; Fast Find in WKO4

- Two ways to learn how many matches you have: do a super-hard training ride with planned matches, or do a tough race requiring many. Use RPE and rate of exhaustion to know when a match is burned or you are out; then find the matches in the downloaded data.
- TrainingPeaks WKO4 has charts that automatically find matches, shown in different colors with time at each power level. Fig 6.17 (Fast Find) colors iLevels for a race; at Pmax levels the color becomes black ("black matches are very hard, short spikes"). Example inset (FRC Level 645 W): FTP Level 222 kJ, 0:10:50, 9.8%; FRC/FTP Level 613 kJ, 0:24:17, 22.1%; FRC Level 115 kJ, 0:03:03, 2.8%; Pmax/FRC Level 24 kJ, 0:00:24, 0.4%; Pmax Level 0 kJ, 0:00:00, 0%.
- Benefits: knowing when and how many matches burned lets you enlarge the matchbook and the intensity of each match; review races postmortem for whether matches were spent too early or well; understand race demands and build a plan around weaknesses.
tags: type:methodology level:advanced subtopic:matches subtopic:wko4 sport:cycling metric:power
src: Ch 6 | Fig 6.17
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-027] MPA-based optimal intervals (constant strain rather than constant watts)

- Problem with fixed-wattage on/off workouts (e.g., microbursts at 150 percent FTP on, 50 percent FTP recovery): early efforts are easy when fresh, final ones too hard from accumulated fatigue, so the rider may not finish all efforts or sets.
- MPA-based workouts make every effort equal in strain/physiological demand, extending the Ch 5 optimal-intervals concept (rather than a static percentage drop-off from Interval 3).
- Fig 6.19 example (10 efforts per set): first interval starts at 439 W, drops to 363 W over 20 s; late in the set each starts at 340 W dropping to 304 W after 20 s, yet strain stays similar. Recovery power also gradually increases over the eight recovery efforts so MPA recovers and then fluctuates consistently. The strain of an interval remains constant, not the absolute power.
tags: type:workout type:methodology level:advanced subtopic:xert subtopic:optimal-intervals sport:cycling metric:power
src: Ch 6 | Fig 6.19
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-028] Other analysis checklist for training and race files

Training files:
- Kilojoules used: did you refuel enough?
- Quadrant Analysis per ride, compared with races to train specifically (Ch 7).
- When did wattage begin to decrease, and how many kJ had been expended by then?
- Overlay intervals: how many reps before a major drop-off? Too few or too many?
Race files:
- View matches burned to understand event demands, then create workouts mimicking them.
- Find peaks to locate the hardest parts of the race.
- If dropped, identify the efforts, their watts and the breaking point, and tailor the plan.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:file-review sport:cycling metric:power metric:load
src: Ch 6 "Other Ways to Analyze the Data"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-029] Using IF to detect FTP changes (Joe Athlete example)

- An IF above 1.05 for a race of about 1 hour often means true threshold power is higher than the FTP set in the software. Examining IF across a season's events can reveal FTP increases or decreases without formal testing.
- Example: FTP set at 290 W, 8 weeks of hard training, then about 1 h breakaway in a criterium: NP 310 W, TSS 114, IF 1.07. By definition 1 h at threshold should give 100 TSS and IF 1.0. Setting FTP to 310 W gives TSS 100, IF 1.0.
- Caution: a 20 W rise (290 to 310) in 8 weeks is relatively large, so raise the software FTP to only 300 W and confirm by formal testing as soon as possible.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:ftp-adjustment level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Using Intensity Factor to Recognize Changes in Fitness"

### [TRPM-C09-030] Quadrant Analysis: the four quadrants and their meaning

Reference lines: AEPF and CPV at threshold power and cadence (figures use CPV about 1.42 m/s and AEPF about 205-210 N, labeled as at 300 W and 80 rpm).
- Quadrant I (upper right, high force, high velocity): sprinting, extended supra-threshold efforts on flat ground (attack, bridging). Track mass-start racing (points race, fixed gear) has much of it.
- Quadrant II (upper left, high force, low velocity): climbing, accelerating from low speed, standing starts (the one situation where strength is truly limiting; AEPF maximal only when CPV is zero), off-road racing (cyclocross, MTB), steep climbs or over-gearing. Quadrants I and II both imply significant fast-twitch recruitment.
- Quadrant III (lower left, low force, low velocity): recovery or social rides (coffee shop); also soft-pedaling or recovering in a bunch during races.
- Quadrant IV (lower right, low force, high velocity): low fixed gear or rollers for pedaling smoothness; races with frequent rapid accelerations (criteriums).
Examples (Figs 7.7-7.12) all have average power near 250 W (the 40 km TT as reference) yet show different force-velocity distributions, e.g., constant-power ergometer vs micro-burst ergometer (15 s on, 15 s off).
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling system:neuromuscular subtopic:quadrant-analysis level:all
src: Ch 7 | Figs 7.6-7.12

### [TRPM-C09-031] Applying Quadrant Analysis to training specificity

- Learn how your events fall into the quadrants and check training is specific to them. Example: a triathlete doing only mass-start road races is probably not neuromuscularly ready for a triathlon. Do not expect to be a successful criterium racer if most time is in Quadrant III riding to the coffee shop; but do be in Quadrant III on recovery rides.
- Use Quadrant Analysis to ensure enough force is created to increase muscular strength, critical for sprint and short uphill attacks.
- Use it on selected rides/races to confirm training is on target.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling subtopic:quadrant-analysis subtopic:specificity level:all
src: Ch 7 closing

### [TRPM-C09-032] Strength endurance (big-gear, low-cadence) intervals: author's critique

- Format: 5-20 min at moderate to high intensity at 45-75 rpm; popular with triathletes; claims are anecdotal with no consensus on the benefit.
- Data: same athlete (68 kg), 2 × 20 min at 250 W or 5 × 5 min at 300 W, both at 45 rpm, no-gripping rule, Velodyne in ergometer mode, 53:12 gear; powers were 90% of what he could normally hold, nearly maximal at that cadence. AEPF was about twice that at the same power at 85-90 rpm, yet less than 50% of his strength (equivalent to weight training at 50% of 1RM) and below two-thirds of velocity-specific maximum. 1,125-1,800 reps were possible. Peak pedal force about twice AEPF, comparable to step-ups with body mass (68 kg × 9.81 = 667 N): more like climbing many flights of stairs than lifting.
- Conclusions: unlikely to cause meaningful hypertrophy or strength gain; a New Zealand lab study found no increase in leg extensor size (anthropometry) or maximal force (isokinetic dynamometry). The Type II recruitment claim is doubtful: AEPF was still well below the velocity-specific maximum, and the recruitment threshold is better a line sloping down left to right. Even if recruitment were higher, it is unclear it is needed: endurance athletes without such training still recruit/train Type II enough to convert IIx to IIa and reach Type II aerobic ability equal to Type I.
tags: type:caution sport:cycling system:neuromuscular subtopic:strength-endurance subtopic:low-cadence level:all
evidence: clinical-trial
src: Ch 7 "Strength Endurance Training" | Fig 7.14

### [TRPM-C09-033] When to change your pedaling print (bilateral rules)

- Evidence urging caution: elite cyclists pull up slightly less than non-elites (minimize power absorbed on upstroke); riders who deliberately pull up use more energy and are less efficient; deliberately altering force-application pattern to emphasize pulling up reduces metabolic efficiency (pedal stroke is like a fingerprint).
- Deliberately change it only if: (1) one leg differs by more than 10 percent in power output from the other: determine whether it is a muscular strength discrepancy or a movement pattern/bike-fit issue; (2) both legs have GPA greater than 35 watts while seated: may be a newer cyclist needing practice, but for a cyclist riding seriously for 3 years or longer, intentionally drive the knee toward the handlebar on the upstroke and lightly point the toe downward throughout the stroke.
- Kurtotic Index: use to smooth the stroke (Figure 7.22); a lower KI leg is smoother. Pedaling improvements may raise power in the 5 to 10 percent range, made during normal workouts.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling subtopic:bilateral-power subtopic:pedaling-technique subtopic:kurtotic-index level:all
evidence: study (three studies cited as notes 2-4, references not given in unit)
src: Ch 7 "Troubleshooting the Kurtotic Index"

### [TRPM-C09-034] Limitations of the impulse-response model

1. Purely descriptive (black box): model structure not linked to physiological events (glycogen resynthesis, mitochondrial biogenesis); linking τa and τf to mechanisms would give more confidence.
2. Assumes no upper limit on performance (more training always better once fatigue dissipates). In reality plateaus occur, even without illness, injury, overtraining or burnout.
3. Statistically valid fit needs multiple direct quantitative performance measurements over a duration comparable to the goal event: 5–50 measurements per parameter × 4 parameters = 20–200 measurements. Parameters change with training; Banister suggested refitting every 60–90 days, implying maximal performance measured at least every fourth day, if not several times a day: unrealistic outside a lab.
4. Fit may not be accurate enough to project future performance. "The best predictor of performance is performance itself." Best tapers improve performance by <5%, usually <2%, so model would need that accuracy on race day; usually not possible.
5. Time constants τa, τf are fairly consistent across studies, but gain factors k_a, k_f vary considerably (even their ratio, unrelated to overall training load; values depend on units, e.g., horsepower vs watts). Generic literature values can't be relied upon. The model is more sensitive to the gains than to the time constants, especially τa (changing τa by 10% changes output by <5%).
tags: type:caution sport:cycling level:all subtopic:impulse-response
src: Ch 9 "Limitations of the Impulse-Response Model"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-035] Customizing the ATL time constant (recovery speed and event type)

- Default ATL = 7 days. Recovering more quickly (e.g., building CTL while still below your CTL ceiling, early-season, fitness still improving): may set 5 days. Recovering more slowly (approaching highest sustainable CTL; later season after months of accumulated TSS; poor sleep, work/life and general stress): may set 10 days. Recovery is multifactorial.
- Short events dependent on neuromuscular power (power near Pmax strongly depends on freshness; near FTP much less): consider a longer ATL constant (10 or even 14 days) so TSB truly reflects dissipation of fatigue.
- Much longer races (e.g., mountain-bike marathon): consider shorter ATL constant (3–5 days), requiring training maintained until closer to the event so TSB does not go too positive.
- Younger individuals, relatively low training load, events favoring sustained power (longer TTs, 24-hour MTB, long-distance triathlon): often better with shorter constant, e.g., 4 or 5 days. Masters athletes, high training load, events favoring non-sustainable power (shorter TTs, criteriums): perhaps 10–12 days. Athletes preparing for longer events often carry higher load, which constrains the optimal constant.
- PMC is sensitive to the ATL time constant (and hence TSB) but little to the CTL constant. Part of the art is finding the ATL constant that best matches how you actually feel/perform vs. CTL/ATL/TSB.
- If you increase the time constant, the TSB at which you are most fresh will be lower, especially with ATL.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:atl-time-constant
src: Ch 9 "Managing Fitness and Fatigue"; "Build Precision with Experience" (note: text refers to Tables 9.2 and 9.3 for time constants; the age table is Table 9.1)
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-036] Applying the Performance Manager: accuracy, consistency, seeding, perspective

- Accuracy: the concept applies whether load is TRIMP, RPE or TSS. With TSS, FTP must be valid and current: TSS = duration[h] × IF² × 100, IF = NP ÷ FTP. Underestimating FTP by 4% (240 W instead of 250 W) raises TSS 8%, distorting CTL/ATL/TSB. Deviations of response from PMC expectation may reveal consistently over/underestimated FTP.
- Consistency: use the power meter every workout and race. Estimate missing TSS by (1) library of comparable workouts, (2) HR data to estimate NP then TSS formula, or (3) estimate IF (see Table 7.2) then compute TSS. Approach 2 is not better than others; experienced users may estimate as well without HR. Error from one or two missing workouts is minimal; if >10% of files missing in a block, interpret PMC output during and after with considerable caution. Hence race with a power meter.
- Seeding: CTL needs long data to be accurate. If new or unreliable data, seed CTL and ATL with the same value (TSB = 0). Most athletes train at 50–75 TSS/hour (average weekly IF ≈ 0.70–0.85): more training, mostly outdoors or unstructured = lower end; less training, indoors, structured = upper end. Revise estimates later; interpret cautiously until enough data.
- Perspective: the composition of training matters as much as dose; specificity always applies. Example: a pursuiter with identical CTL/TSB early (Levels 2–4 road-focused) vs. before nationals (Levels 5–7 track-focused) would pursue better later, but time trial better early. CTL/ATL/TSB remain good indicators of load and adaptation but do not replace choosing workouts for competition goals.
- Principles of periodization (Bompa 1968: build, taper, rest to create overload then adaptation; Banister 1970s) still hold; what changed is accurate quantification via power meter. The PMC predicts peaks, signals when to rest to prevent overtraining, and improves chances of being on form at the right time.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:pmc-practice metric:load
src: Ch 9 "Applying the Performance Manager Concept"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-037] Plan-building logic: from limiter to workout choice

Method used in all case studies: (1) record athlete data (weight, FTP, W/kg, peak 5 s, 1 min, 5 min); (2) read Power Profile, Power Duration Curve and Quadrant Analysis; (3) list strengths and weaknesses; (4) set goal and time available; (5) choose workouts that target the limiters and the demands of the event. The plan must be driven by the strengths and weaknesses identified in the Power Profile; if neuromuscular power is not a weakness, use that time on the bike elsewhere. Do not add techniques just because other cyclists use them.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:limiters
src: Ch 10 | "Developing your power-based training plan"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-038] Reading Power Duration Curve: category-line crossings as limiters

- Where the Power Duration Curve falls below a category line it generally indicates a weakness that later shifts into a strength (caption, Fig 10.5).
- A relatively flat PD curve with very good 1- and 5-minute power indicates a pursuiter phenotype, possibly time trialer with training (Bob).
- Jill: fall-off from 5 s to about 28 s (956 W to 509 W) = poor fatigue resistance in a long sprint; MMP "bump" above PD curve from 26 s to nearly 2 min shows good anaerobic capacity.
- Joe: Level 7 power below novice until 1 min, then flattens; triathlon is slow-twitch steady-state, so Level 7 is deliberately not trained.
- Bill: PD curve bumps up from 5 to 20 min = strength to train first.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:power-duration-curve
src: Ch 10 | Figs 10.2, 10.5, 10.8
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-039] Adjusting the week for racing: Plan A, B, C

- Race Saturday (Plan A): Thursday easy ride (rest two days before race); Friday tune-up workout to prep legs.
- Race Sunday (Plan B): Thursday Endurance ride, not overdone, with short bursts to keep legs sharp; Friday Active Recovery; Saturday tune-up day.
- No race (Plan C): Thursday Endurance with short bursts; Friday Active Recovery; Saturday solid Tempo ride with shorter efforts; Sunday the Level 3 Tempo ride (Jill: TEMP-W10).
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling phase:race level:all subtopic:race-week-adjustment
src: Ch 10 | "How to adjust your training program for racing"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-040] Plan traps and flexibility rules

- Always download power after every ride, including recovery rides. If no power meter/ it fails, use RPE and heart rate.
- Do not force the plan or "stack" workouts (term coined by Coach Gale Bernhardt) to make up missed ones; stacking Level 4, long group ride, hills and sprints in one day is a recipe for disaster; packing work into few days leads to illness or injury.
- If you miss a day, generally move on to the next workout. Exception: if the missed workout was highly specific and you will not do it again for at least two weeks, make it up as soon as possible.
- Tuesday and Wednesday workouts may be reversed. Thursday should be easier (follows two hard days, often two days before Saturday ride/race); if not racing Saturday or not needing sharpness, Thursday may be swapped with Tue/Wed workouts.
- If overly tired at end of hard week, move rest week up; if not tired, a scheduled rest week may be pushed back. Athletes often assume overtraining when only a little tired; push through to avoid limiting potential. A postponed rest week must still be taken the week after it was scheduled.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:plan-flexibility
src: Ch 10 | "Developing your power-based training plan"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-041] Climbing cadence and force management on multi-climb events

Quadrant Analysis reveals that climbing at lower cadences forces high Average Effective Pedal Force into Quadrants II and III.
- Low cadence / high force climbing (QII): acceptable for a single climb where subsequent recovery is possible, or for the final climb of a race.
- Multi-climb events (gran fondos, hilly road races): pedaling in QII causes excessive neuromuscular fatigue and accelerates glycogen depletion.
- Heuristic: for multi-climb events, deliberately train to increase climbing cadence into the upper 70s and 80s rpm. This shifts mechanical load away from the muscular/neuromuscular system onto the cardiovascular system, preserving leg stamina for subsequent climbs.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all metric:cadence metric:power subtopic:climbing-cadence
src: Ch 10 | Bill Masters's plan | Fig 10.11
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-042] Rules for reading fitness trends

- Test after a rest week to track fitness accurately.
- Use weekly (7-day) or 28-day windows, not per-ride points.
- Interpret altitude events cautiously: peaks are lower than at sea level.
- FRC jump with flat FTP suggests recent freshness; FRC gain with apparent FTP drop is likely a modeling artifact unless long-duration power is also tested.
- TTE near or above 1 h may predict an FTP increase.
- Pmax decline late in season: check for accumulated fatigue or lack of all-out sprint efforts.
- Compare MMP curve slopes for weaknesses, and use Normalized Power instead of average power for durations beyond about 1 h.
- Unchanged weekly average cadence can mislead because zeros are included; watch trends.
tags: type:decision-rule type:monitoring sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:fitness-tracking
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 (synthesis of rules)

### [TRPM-C09-043] Power calibration: why RPE alone is unreliable for pacing

- For races of Olympic distance and longer, perceived exertion at the correct pace is likely to be much lower than what you know you can do.
- Learn to associate RPE with power output by doing longer and longer intervals at different intensities, focusing on how each level feels.
- Quote (Charles Howe): "Power calibrates perceived exertion, and perceived exertion modulates power."
- Key to pacing: learn the wattage you must hold on the bike so you run well, then calibrate it to the correct perceived exertion.
- Problem: RPE changes daily, like heart rate, with sleep, stress and hydration. On race day RPE will likely be lower than in training for the same effort. The power meter is the objective anchor.
tags: type:principle sport:triathlon metric:rpe,power level:all subtopic:power-calibration
src: Ch 12 "Calibrating Your Power for Better Pacing"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-044] Anaerobic training in triathlon: when it matters

- Road racing uses many energy systems (Levels 5, 6, 7 matter as much as 1–4). Triathlon is more aerobic: the authors have never witnessed a field sprint in triathlon. A strong FTP is key to finishing position.
- Therefore it is not that important for most triathletes to train at Neuromuscular Power (Level 7), Anaerobic Capacity (Level 6) or even VO2max (Level 5).
- Draft-legal races: touch on these levels for well-rounded fitness (bridging short gaps to the next group).
- Exception: courses demanding short, high-wattage efforts, e.g. Ironman Wisconsin, with over 100 hills of 10 seconds to 5 minutes, most 30–90 seconds. Add the Anaerobic Capacity workout there.
- Related observation: triathletes' Power Profiles usually slope upward to the right (strong aerobic, less trained anaerobic/neuromuscular).
tags: type:decision-rule sport:triathlon system:anaerobic level:all subtopic:anaerobic-capacity
src: Ch 12 "Anaerobic Capacity"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-045] Racing triathlon: pacing rules and rehearsal

- Riding too fast on the bike is the number one cause of DNFs in triathlons. A well-paced versus poorly paced bike leg can differ by as little as 15 watts (normalized) average.
- It is not only average watts: how watts are produced, number of surges, and going harder early vs saving for the finish all affect run time.
- Pace with Normalized Power (NP) at any distance: it accounts for terrain and lets you focus on smooth, steady pedaling. If the head unit cannot show NP, pace on average power as a percentage of FTP.
- Rehearse: do rides holding target intensity for the whole event length. For events longer than Olympic, hold the pace for half the race distance followed by a half-distance run (brick) to see how much energy remains at the start and end of the run.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:triathlon metric:power level:all subtopic:race-pacing
src: Ch 12 "Racing Triathlons"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-046] Quadrant Analysis case studies: poor vs proper pacing (Figures 12.1, 12.2, 12.6)

- Figure 12.1 (poor): Ironman bike, 41.2% in Quadrant II, QI 2.1%, QIII 45.9%, QIV 10.8%. Duration 7:09:27, NP 198, VI 1.11, avg power 178 W, TSS 528.5. He pushed too big a gear, rode too hard and walked the run.
- Figure 12.2 (proper): Ironman-distance, QI 0.1%, QII 12.1%, QIII 70.8%, QIV 17.0%. Duration 5:24:26, NP 232, VI 1.08, avg power 216 W, TSS 293.4. Avoided power spikes, set a run PR and won his age group at Ironman Canada.
- Figure 12.6 (good, Ironman Wisconsin): QI 0.6%, QII 12.3%, QIII 51.1%, QIV 36.0%; NP 190, VI 1.05.
- Lesson: ride smoothly, avoid surges, do not push a big gear; the best triathletes know how to create watts to conserve energy for the run.
tags: type:evidence sport:triathlon metric:power level:all subtopic:quadrant-analysis
src: Ch 12 | Figures 12.1, 12.2, 12.6
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C09-047] Ironman/half-Ironman race execution rules (Endurance Nation)

- First 30–45 minutes: hold 95% of goal wattage; thereafter ride as close to goal wattage as possible.
- Hill longer than 3 minutes: ride at 105% of goal wattage.
- Hill of 30 seconds to 2 minutes: ride at 110% of goal wattage.
- Rationale: a 3-minute all-out effort is normally 115–120% FTP (Level 5) and 30 s–2 min is 120–150% FTP; holding 105/110% minimizes spikes.
- Do not attack or stand on hills; transition power smoothly from flats to hill and resist accelerating to keep up with faster riders. A low-VI race shows mostly Quadrants III and IV on the WKO chart.
- Postmortem metric: Variability Index of 1.04–1.07 on the bike leg means excellent smooth pedaling and limited surges.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:triathlon metric:power level:all subtopic:hill-pacing
src: Ch 12 "The Mathematical Approach to Pacing"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-048] Case study: poor pacing, Lake Placid Ironman (Figures 12.3, 12.4)

- Athlete: female age 40–44, FTP 215 W, went all out on the bike to qualify for Hawaii; run was 10–15 minutes off, but she placed fourth and qualified.
- Data: duration 5:30:31, NP 161, IF 0.751, TSS 306.5, VI 1.06. IF 0.75 is at the upper limit for Ironman (Table 12.1); TSS 306 is per Rich and Patrick for proven strong Ironman runners only.
- No single area was mispaced; she pedaled smoothly but too hard overall. First 30 minutes averaged 170 W (about 80% FTP), a little hot.
- Uphill rule: you can produce more watts uphill (more resistance). On longer climbs ride at FTP, on shorter ones above it (106%, 230 W for FTP 215). On downhills you can only produce about 120 W (about 55% FTP, Level 1 Active Recovery) and muscles recover without losing time to competitors (Fig 12.4: speed rises, cadence rises, watts drop).
- Action: tweak training for the number/length of hills, set finer wattage goals, practice pacing, and raise FTP.
tags: type:evidence sport:triathlon metric:power level:masters subtopic:hill-pacing
src: Ch 12 "Pacing in the Real Race" | Figures 12.3, 12.4
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C09-049] Case study: good pacing, Ironman Wisconsin (Figures 12.5, 12.7)

- Athlete: male 25–29, FTP 275 W. The prior year he went too hard, pushed a big gear, attacked hills and walked the run. He budgeted a 250 TSS day, targeting IF 0.70.
- Result: IF 0.692, TSS 246.4, NP 190 W, VI 1.05 (very low for a hilly course that usually causes VI over 1.10), duration 5:08:58. Held a wattage ceiling of 200 W, avoided a big gear, few and brief watts over FTP. Quadrants: over 51% QIII, 36% QIV. Set a run PR, passed over 20 competitors, won his age group and qualified for Hawaii. His FTP was 20 W higher than the previous year, yet he rode smoother rather than faster.
- Figure 12.7 power distribution: most time in Level 2 (about 34%) and Level 3 (about 33%), Level 1 about 15%, Level 4 about 13%, Level 5 under 4%, Level 6 under 2%, Level 7 about 0%.
- Note: Figure 12.6's printed TSS (352.2, 0.827) differs from Figure 12.5's (246.4, 0.692) for the same ride [Likely typo in source — recorded as printed].
tags: type:evidence sport:triathlon metric:power level:masters subtopic:race-pacing
src: Ch 12 "Pacing in the Real Race" | Figures 12.5, 12.6, 12.7
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C09-050] Case study: PMC peaking for an Ironman age-grouper (Figures 12.8–12.10)

- Athlete: near-full-time training, six seasons of data. Cycling bests: 368 W peak 1 min, 235 W peak 20 min, 217 W peak 120 min. Peaks: 1-min on Dec 14 after a huge rest, 20-min July 12, 120-min July 20; target races Ironman Arizona (early April), Wisconsin (early September), Hawaii (mid-October). Peaks did not match races; before Arizona she had her third-best 20-min the week before.
- Running: 4 of her 10 best runs of the year were within 15 days of Ironman Arizona; few 20/120-minute PBs until August. One can peak for one event and not another while training for both.
- Combined PMC: bests occurred mostly after normal rest weeks during hard weeks in build phases. This suggests a high year-round load needing little focused training to peak; she may have been on form too soon, which makes form hard to hold for the goal event.
- Coaching recommendation: plenty of endurance work for a long lead-up, then four hard weeks of intensity, some rest, then a week of endurance and intensity right before the Ironman. This avoids peaking early and builds aerobic fitness, giving both endurance and fitness on race day.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:triathlon metric:load phase:peak level:advanced subtopic:taper-peaking
src: Ch 12 "Timing Your Fitness Peak" | Figures 12.8–12.10
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C09-051] Criterium breakaway: sit on when over FTP (case example)

Case: Cat II racer, FTP 350 W, 175 lb, sprinter-shaped Power Profile.
- Attacked for a prime (1st prime max 1,065 W, avg 913 W for 13 s). He was then 20 s off the front solo and rode just below threshold power.
- Three riders bridged. In the rotation he was at more than 500 W on the pull and still 400 W in the recovery line. Knowing his FTP, he saw he would be dropped, so he sat on (did not pull). He suspected the others were also above FTP and about to blow up.
- Rule: if you are over your FTP just sitting in a break, your time in it is limited. Pulling in a break that is over your head only spits you out the back.
- At the 2nd prime he sprinted from the back and won it (max 1,007 W, avg 711 W for 11 s). The trio fell back. Now 45+ s ahead solo, he rode at about 350 W (his FTP) to the finish. Holding 360+ would only blow him up and get him caught. Normalized Power was 346 W; heart rate was steady.
- In the last 4-5 minutes he raised power to 360-370 W for the finish and held off the field by about 10 s.
- Lesson: use the power meter in nearly every lap to pace for the phase of the race you are in.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:intermediate subtopic:criterium subtopic:breakaway subtopic:ftp-pacing metric:power
src: Ch 13 "Pacing in a Criterium", Figs 13.1-13.2
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C09-052] Time trial pacing: do not start too hard

- Second rule of time trialing: "Don't start too hard, don't start too hard, don't start too hard." Rule number one: get to your start time on time.
- Mechanism: early in a race, RPE does not match actual exertion. Adrenaline, endorphins, caffeine and excitement mask the effort until about 4-5 minutes in, when it catches up. Then pacing cannot be fixed.
- Example: starting at 800 W for the first 300 m, then dropping from 29 mph to 22.3 mph, versus a 24 mph ability. Rule: if the goal is 250 W, use the first 15-30 s to get up to speed, then hold 250-260 W. This gives about 24 mph average rather than 22.3. Push a little harder in the last 5 minutes, then go all out to the line.
- Start restraint depends on length: 40 km, hold back for the first 5 minutes; 10-miler, hold back about the first 2 minutes; 4 km pursuit, almost no holding back. In general, the shorter the event, the less you hold back.
- Figure 13.3 (40 km TT): power spiked above 600 W, then settled at FTP (about 300 W); heart rate took nearly 5 minutes to reach threshold HR (about 150 bpm), which shows regulated early effort.
- Test pacing strategies in practice time trials on different courses.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:time-trial subtopic:pacing metric:power metric:rpe
src: Ch 13 "Pacing in a Time Trial", Fig 13.3
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-053] Flat time trial: isopower strategy and wind

- No-wind flat TT (control): ride at FTP until 3-5 minutes remain, then raise the pace to the finish. This isopower strategy is the default: be as smooth and consistent as possible.
- Headwind and tailwind: a tailwind normalizes fitness differences. Do not relax at high speed. It is hard to produce even FTP in a tailwind (gearing too small, or unaccustomed to high power at high speed).
- Example, 40 km with 20 km each way: FTP 340 W holds 320 W at 32.5 mph in the tailwind; FTP 320 W holds 300 W at 32.1 mph. The faster rider gains only 17 s.
- Headwind leg: 340 W rider averages 22 mph for 20 km; 320 W rider averages 20.5 mph. That is a 194 s advantage. All else equal, the TT can be won in the headwind.
- Variable pacing is a complex topic; the author gives only these considerations.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:advanced subtopic:time-trial subtopic:wind subtopic:isopower metric:power
src: Ch 13 "Flat Time Trials"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-054] Hilly time trial pacing

- Even hills (equal downhill): push harder than FTP uphill and recover on the downhill. How much depends on length and grade: a hill under 1 minute and steep allows much harder than a 5-minute gradual one. Longer uphill efforts take longer to recover from on the downhill.
- Do not overshoot: blowing up at the top ends the consistent effort.
- Starting point if unsure: use the power training levels. For a 3-minute hill, 115% of FTP is holdable when fresh; subtract 5-10% of that and try about 105% of FTP.
- With corresponding downhills, you can push a touch harder than on a flat TT.
- Hill that plateaus at the top (no recovery): hold FTP or just above on the climb. Return to speed immediately after the crest. Do not hammer up and then struggle on the flat. Time below FTP after the crest is time given to competitors.
- The RPE at the start is unreliable, so rely on the meter.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:advanced subtopic:time-trial subtopic:hills metric:power
src: Ch 13 "Hilly Time Trials"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-055] On-site post-event analysis

- Download power data between events for immediate adjustments to pacing, strategy or gearing. At a velodrome you may ride heats, qualifiers and finals with up to 30 min downtime between events.
- Compare cadence with your known best-performance cadence. If it was faster in a qualifier, the track may be quicker or air density different; adjust pace and gearing accordingly.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:advanced subtopic:track subtopic:gearing subtopic:race-analysis metric:cadence
src: Ch 13 "On-Site Post-Event Performance Analysis"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-056] Precision and conditions for aero field testing

- Under ideal conditions field-testing CdA gives a coefficient of variation (reproducibility) under 2%, comparable to wind tunnels and near the resolution limit of power meters.
- Conditions: no significant wind or car traffic (a single car can disturb the air for several minutes). Test very early in the morning, just after sunrise before wind and traffic. Optimizing something like aerobar height may take many days of testing.
- Accuracy check: compare outbound vs inbound within a session and across sessions.
- Limitation: tests are only valid near zero yaw (wind from straight ahead), unlike wind tunnels that can test multiple yaw angles. Aero equipment benefits are usually greatest at yaw angles.
tags: type:caution type:test sport:cycling level:advanced subtopic:cda subtopic:aero-testing subtopic:yaw
src: Ch 13 "Precision in Aerodynamic Testing"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C09-057] General recipe for using a power meter in any discipline

Four-step recipe (applies to any sport/discipline):
1. Determine the demands of the event by recording power data and the effort needed to complete the event successfully.
2. Understand your strengths and weaknesses relative to the demands of the event.
3. Train for both the demands of the event and your weaknesses (assuming the weaknesses could impact success in the event).
4. Review the results of those training sessions and compare them with data collected in races.
The chapter gives real-rider examples per discipline; ideas can be applied across most cycling disciplines.
tags: type:methodology level:all subtopic:event-demands subtopic:specificity sport:cycling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 intro | n/a

### [TRPM-C09-058] Track: determining race demands (match sprint cadence, gearing)

Dr. Jim Martin (University of Utah, with Australian Institute of Sport): world-class match sprinters initiate the jump in a flying 200 m TT essentially at the cadence giving maximal power; beyond this, power declines continuously from cadence above optimum plus fatigue. Implications: train not only peak power but fatigue resistance at very high cadences (e.g. 140–160 rpm); riders may benefit from larger gears in qualifying (vs match-sprint rounds) to stay closer to optimal cadence for more of the effort.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling system:neuromuscular metric:cadence level:elite subtopic:match-sprint
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Determining Race Demands" | n/a

### [TRPM-C09-059] Track: evaluating technical performance (starts, pursuit pacing, team pursuit drafting)

- Skill importance varies: team sprint (esp. lead riders) needs standing starts, no pacing; individual pursuit depends little on start, heavily on pacing.
- Record power, cadence and speed at high frequency during standing starts: shows impact of technique changes (e.g. hip position relative to crank) better than split times even over 25 m with timing tape.
- Actual pursuit power over time is more useful than splits for evaluating pacing, especially with variable conditions (wind outdoors).
- Project 96 (USA Cycling, Dr. Jeff Broker et al.): team pursuit riders in 2nd, 3rd or 4th position needed only 64–71 percent of lead rider's power; requirement varied with drafting skill, high at first and dropping dramatically as riders drafted closer. Dropping down too early (briefly overlapping the third rider then slotting on) needs no more power than a perfect exchange; coming down too late (losing draft, having to accelerate back) is much more costly.
tags: type:evidence sport:cycling level:elite subtopic:team-pursuit subtopic:drafting subtopic:pacing metric:power
evidence: author-experience (Project 96, Broker)
src: Ch 14 "Evaluating Technical Performance" | n/a

### [TRPM-C09-060] Track: evaluating training methods and gearing for standing starts

- Use power to check enough rest is taken between efforts (e.g. 2,000 m flying-start workout) so each effort stresses anaerobic capacity as intended; otherwise cumulative fatigue turns it into a suboptimal VO2max session. Use critical power approach (Chapter 3) to track anaerobic capacity changes.
- Myth: standing-start efforts in a larger gear do not give greater neuromuscular overload. Within fairly broad limits gear has no effect on force at any cadence; the force-velocity relationship is set by muscle contractile properties (and to a lesser extent technique). Fig 14.4: 54x14 (104.1") vs 48x14 (92.6") gears gave essentially identical AEPF-CPV lines (about 900 N at 0.5 m/s to about 400 N at 2.0 m/s). Gear only determines how quickly the rider moves down the force-velocity line stroke by stroke.
- Instead of swapping chainrings/cogs, manipulate motivation (pair riders, handicapped start chasing another) or increase dwell time at high force/low cadence (extremely large gear, or climbing up the banking so gravity and inertia slow acceleration).
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling system:neuromuscular level:elite subtopic:standing-start subtopic:gearing subtopic:force-velocity
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Evaluating Training Methods" | Fig 14.4

### [TRPM-C09-061] 24-hour MTB race pacing strategy (Dave Harris)

Rule: put out more wattage in the first four hours (up to six) to create a gap; in an ultra race (longer than six hours) most athletes fatigue at the same rate over the remaining 18 hours, so e.g. a 45-minute gap at six hours likely holds (barring hydration, nutrition, mechanicals). Dave Harris held power just above the level he thought sustainable for the whole race; lap-to-lap power was nearly identical over first four laps.
Table 14.2 Analysis of First 5 Laps
| Lap | Duration | Work kJ | TSS (IF) | NP (VI) | Min/Max/Avg Pwr | Min/Max/Avg HR | Min/Max/Avg Cad | Min/Max/Avg Spd |
| 1 | 1:05:26.16 | 814 | 51 (0.684) | 226 w (1.09) | 0/568/208 | 130/134/146 | 30/174/85 | 5.3/31.3/15.3 |
| 2 | 1:08:07.44 (1:09:26.08) | 839 | 50.8 (0.67) | 221 w (1.07) | 0/650/206 | 115/161/146 | 29/152/86 | 2.7/31.8/15.0 |
| 3 | 1:08:57.84 (1:09:10.36) | 852 | 53.7 (0.684) | 226 w (1.09) | 0/553/206 | 125/166/150 | 29/146/85 | 2.9/29.7/14.9 |
| 4 | 1:08:57.84 (1.13.10.96) | 855 | 53.1 (0.68) | 224 w (1.08) | 0/735/207 | 117/160/150 | 29/159/84 | 2.2/29.3/14.8 |
| 5 | 1:13:35.04 (1.19.53.00) | 827 | 47.5 (0.623) | 206 w (1.10) | 0/497/188 | 113/157/144 | 29/163/83 | 4.4/28.2/13.9 |
[Min HR 130 vs Max 134 vs Avg 146 in Lap 1 is inconsistent; duration parenthetical values inconsistent — recorded as printed]
Text: NP averaged 224 W over first four laps, dropping to 206 W in lap 5 — closer to the power sustainable for the remaining 19 hours.
Whole file (Fig 14.5): duration 19:30:27.36, work 11,539 kJ, TSS 657.1 (0.581), NP 192, VI 1.16, Pw:HR 5.93%, Pa:HR -6.15%, distance 253.76 mi; power min 0 max 735 avg 165 W; HR 80/166/128 bpm; cadence 29/174/80 rpm; speed 2.2/31.8/13 mph.
Quadrant Analysis (Fig 14.6): QI 3.9%, QII 8.6%, QIII 19.8%, QIV 67.8%; best riders modulate power to spend the majority (50% or more) of time in Quadrant IV (low force, high cadence), sparing muscle glycogen. Curves 240 W, threshold 320 W, 200 W; lines about CPV 1.60 m/s, AEPF 230 N.

### [TRPM-C09-062] 100-mile MTB race: Jeremiah Bishop, Shenandoah 100 (2009)

Data (Fig 14.7): FTP 390 W; gridline 340 W; duration 6:51:08; work 5,650 kJ; TSS 356.1 (IF 0.721); NP 274 W; VI 1.2; distance 98.174 mi; power min 0, max 775, avg 229 W; cadence 39/210/85 rpm; speed avg 14.3 mph. [HR and Pw:HR values printed as 12/41/27 bpm and -219.15% — Likely unit/typo error in source — recorded as printed; max speed 113 mph likewise]. Win judged as hard as a Tour de France stage.
Pacing: front group of five after two hours, paceline on gravel averaging over 30 mph. In the early fast 2–3 hours he limited hard efforts and sharp bursts to avoid early glycogen depletion, pedaled smoothly, spun higher cadence to minimize fatigue. Exceeded 500 W only 33 times (19 in first 2 h), each 10–60 s (Fast Find in WKO); a 1-hour criterium might have 100 power spikes.
Quote: what you have in the tank in the last 30 miles determines the winner; higher-than-average early pacing can decide long 24-hour races, but 6–8 hour races resemble long road races where winning attacks come near the end.
Quadrant Analysis (Fig 14.9): QI 3.1%, QII 21.5%, QIII 44.0%, QIV 31.4% (QIII+QIV about 75% = good pacing). Final 36 minutes (darker points; caption says last hour) mostly in QII (21%) — force to drop second place; Type II fibre fatigue resistance a determinant. Curves 420 W, threshold 390 W, 340 W.

### [TRPM-C09-063] Avoiding paralysis by analysis; keep the big picture

- Avoid the "paralysis by analysis" syndrome. Power meters can be frustrating, especially with unit trouble or initial technical difficulties, and may seem more hassle than worth; keep the big picture: each training day fits an overall set of objectives.
- It is fine occasionally to download a ride, store it in the database and not look at the workout file. Keep looking at long-term graphs to see how all systems are improving.
- It is acceptable to not spend hours on data files; the power meter still has value as the ultimate pacing tool (analogy: driving a Ferrari is fun even if you never test its limits on a racetrack).
- Be open to changing training based on new information; fitness improvement is rewarding, and quantifying it is doubly rewarding, confirming that hard work pays off.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:data-overload subtopic:pacing

## C10 Applied Guidance

### [TRPM-C10-001] Motivation, time efficiency and aerodynamics with a power meter

- Motivation: seeing average watts fall near the end of a 5-minute effort pushes you to reach the 5-minute goal. Goals must be challenging yet achievable. Marginal gains matter in a sport decided by a tire width.
- Time efficiency: sticking to the letter of the workout minimizes junk miles and maximizes limited time.
- Position and aerodynamics: body position is the single greatest factor in speed at a given power.
  - Simple power-meter tests show how position affects speed and how to change it for the most watts and least drag.
  - Wind-tunnel findings: improved position and equipment can allow about 30 watts less to hold a given speed.
  - This is a greater gain than most cyclists see in an entire year of training.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:aerodynamics subtopic:motivation level:all
src: Ch 1 "Gain Motivation to Work Harder"; "Improve Your Position and Aerodynamics"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C10-002] Quantifying sports nutrition with kilojoules

- Work in kilojoules lets you estimate kilocalorie use (nearly 1:1) to decide when to eat more or cut back.
- Depleted energy stores drastically reduce watts, so eating often enough and enough calories affects workout and race quality.
- Known on-bike expenditure lets you plan post-exercise meals to the kilocalorie, and balance intake with expenditure to maintain weight in heavy training.
- Replenishing expended glycogen, and possibly more, speeds recovery.
- Case (Sami Srour, metric century): he bonked late in practice rides, which hurt the next 2 days of training. Hunter measured total kJ for the ride, split the ride into segments with kJ per segment, and set calorie-intake goals per segment (when and how much to eat, plus electrolyte drink). A post-ride recovery protocol gave carbohydrate, protein and fat targets. [Specific amounts not given in source.]
tags: type:nutrition sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:kilojoules subtopic:fueling level:all
src: Ch 1 "Quantify Your Sports Nutrition"
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C10-003] ANT+ and Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE)

- ANT+: a chip in nearly every power meter and head unit giving a common language; ultra-low-power protocol owned by Garmin and universally adopted. It separates the power-measuring device from the head unit, so brands can be mixed (e.g., ANT+ SRM crank with Garmin head, PowerTap hub with Wahoo, Garmin pedal with Stages head). All meters on the market operate on ANT+. Choose head unit by features; Garmin dominates and allows independent apps on higher-end units.
- Downside: broadcast message definitions constrain data; head units receive four messages per second, three repeated, so truly one message per second. Protocols are updated (e.g., left/right pedaling metrics), but inability to capture higher-definition data hinders development.
- BLE: used by many manufacturers and in every smartphone, so a phone can replace the bike computer (record, view, upload via manufacturer app). Higher data rate than ANT+; for some meters it sends radial and tangential forces with left/right power at a useful rate. Makes firmware updates (bug fixes, new features) easy.
- Authors advise researching head units carefully for your needs; product details change quickly (e.g., DC Rainmaker site for up-to-date reviews).
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:ant-plus-bluetooth metric:power
src: Ch 2 "ANT+ Compatible Head Units"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C10-004] Best practice: Set a high recording rate

Record power data at the highest recording rate, typically every second; some head units allow more ("the more the better"). Garmin head units default to "smart recording", which records fewer points when riding straight and more when turning. That suits GPS but not power data; unless changed you will miss a tremendous amount of power data.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:recording-rate metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Best Practices"

### [TRPM-C10-005] Best practice: Display rate 3–5 seconds

Recording rate = rate data are stored; display rate = rolling average shown on the head unit (3, 5, 10, 20 s, etc.). Record fast, but smooth the display: power changes quickly, so a 3- or 5-second rolling average helps pacing and keeps you from chasing numbers. Use 3 or 5 s for 90 percent of training; a 10-second rolling average might be more beneficial in a time trial.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:display-rate metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Set Display Rate to 3–5 Seconds"

### [TRPM-C10-006] Power software: post-ride analysis

The power meter's true usefulness is post-ride analysis: download data and study graphs. A one-hour ride at high sampling rate yields overwhelming volume; software should present it digestibly so even a novice can make training decisions. Many meters have proprietary online or desktop software; authors do not review software (dates quickly) and urge finding the best for your needs to track fitness trends. Later chapters explain common charts.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:power-software metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Power Software"

### [TRPM-C10-007] Choosing terrain and venue for power workouts

- Match terrain to the effort. 1-minute anaerobic efforts at 475-500 W: use a slight uphill or a flat road with no downhills.
- 60-minute Tempo effort: keep power in the Tempo range 90 percent of the time; apply steady power uphill and stay on the gas on downhills.
- Only flat roads available: use wind to generate more resistance.
- Rolling hills are the hardest terrain for steady Tempo: keeping average watts in Tempo would require hammering every hill, which turns it into a hill-repeat workout (VO2max or anaerobic capacity work), not Tempo.
- Hilly terrain also makes recovery and endurance rides hard to keep in low levels. If you need a rest, ride an indoor trainer to follow recovery power guidelines.
- Venues:
  - Indoor trainer: easy to hold a specific wattage range (no wind or traffic).
  - Flat roads: steady power and controlled intervals.
  - Time trials: hold effort right at lactate threshold; cardinal rule: Don't start too hard.
  - Hill climbs: on a gradual grade stay in a small power range. Find preferred climbing cadence by repeating a climb at a different cadence each time and choosing the one giving the highest power.
  - Specific intervals: e.g., 5-minute intervals at 295-305 W. Heart rate as a guide for the same interval can lead to going too hard early and declining power.
  - Race-winning intervals and other wattage workouts: watch average watts as a carrot.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:terrain subtopic:indoor-trainer
src: Ch 5
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C10-008] Fueling notes in Ch 10 plans

- Bill Week 12: increase complex carbohydrate intake to fill muscle and liver glycogen.
- Jill long WATTS-W5 ride: with an hour to go, stop for sugar and caffeine (soda or energy drink).
- Taper/rest week lets athlete rebuild muscle glycogen (Bob Week 15).
- Bill's event: eat and drink during ride.
tags: type:nutrition sport:cycling level:all subtopic:glycogen
src: Ch 10
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C10-009] Conserving muscle glycogen for the run (training)

- Train the body to conserve muscle glycogen for the run. Consistent FTP and endurance workouts increase glycogen storage and efficiency of use.
- On non-maximal days, pedal efficiently. Use Quadrant Analysis (Ch 7) to check energy conservation: most of a triathlon should be in Quadrant III (low force, slow cadence) and Quadrant IV (low force, fast cadence).
tags: type:principle sport:triathlon system:fat-oxidation metric:cadence level:all subtopic:glycogen-sparing
src: Ch 12 "Conserving Energy for the Run"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C10-010] Using race kilojoules to plan race fueling

- Mark the point where you became exhausted (with a lap or interval; it can often be found in the file later). In the downloaded file, read the kJ and TSS accumulated before fatigue, and use them to decide calories for the next race.
- Example: a 2.5-hour race with 500 kcal consumed, but the file shows 2,000 kJ expended (roughly 2,200 kcal). Plan to eat more in the next race, or carbo-load in the days before.
tags: type:nutrition type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:all subtopic:kilojoules subtopic:race-fueling metric:load
src: Ch 13 "Comparing the Demands of Racing and Training"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C10-011] Track: aerodynamic testing on the velodrome

High track speeds make aero drag an even greater fraction of resistance, so racers use the most aero equipment (e.g. disc wheels) even in mass-start events. With a power meter and careful testing (see Chapter 12 "Aerodynamic Testing with a Power Meter") equipment and position choices can be data-based, saving tenths of a second in a flying 200 m or many seconds in a team pursuit. A velodrome is an excellent semicontrolled venue: same conditions as the race, no cars/dogs/bumps. Test only when the track is empty or nearly so (safety, and many riders disturb the air and affect data).
tags: type:protocol sport:cycling level:all subtopic:aerodynamics subtopic:track
evidence: author-experience

## C11 Recovery, Monitoring & Lifestyle

### [TRPM-C11-001] Using power to track fitness and avoid overtraining

- Download data right after the ride and compare it with the same ride last week, the week before, and so on.
- Fitness and strengths change from month to month, so track progress toward the bigger-picture season goals.
- Power data reveal the status of lactate threshold and aerobic capacity. If they are not improving, change the training.
- Past data show how long it took to reach a new fitness level, which allows realistic goal setting.
- Knowing when to rest matters. Power meters help avoid overtraining. Track overall training stress with a method such as Training Stress Score (TSS) to make better training-load decisions.
- Periodic testing (for example monthly) shows which physiological systems are improving and whether it is the right time to focus on a particular area. Testing regularly also helps avoid overtraining.
- "Training is testing; testing is training" (Andrew). Treat every session as an opportunity to build toward a peak performance.
tags: type:monitoring sport:cycling metric:power,load system:threshold level:all subtopic:tracking-fitness
src: Ch 1 "Track Fitness Changes"; "Create a Mobile Testing Lab"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C11-002] MMP Periodic Chart and week-by-week smoothing

- The Mean Maximal Power (MMP) Periodic Chart compiles data from every ride for a selected duration. Each data point represents the MMP (average best power) of a single ride for that duration (Fig 11.1 illustrates peak 5 s, 1 min, 5 min, and 20 min).
- Raw per-ride data contains too much scatter and density to discern fitness changes easily.
- Weekly smoothing: Smoothing the data over seven days produces a week-by-week view where each data point represents the peak wattage achieved for that duration over that entire week (Figs 11.2–11.4), highlighting seasonal peaks and troughs.
- 28-day window: Evaluating mean maximal power over the past 28 days provides a reliable operational method for observing improvements across different energy systems.
- Multi-year comparison: Plotting yearly peak wattages together (Fig 11.5) provides a concise long-term view of multi-season athletic progression.
tags: type:monitoring sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:mean-maximal-power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 "Changes in Mean Maximal Power" | Figs 11.1–11.5; closing

### [TRPM-C11-003] Case study: masters rider Jack over three seasons (Figs 11.2-11.5)

Transferable lessons from the example:
- Year 1: peak 5 s almost 1,080 W (early spring; Fig 11.5 lists 1,058 W). Peak 1 min 560 W (early May). Peak 5 min about 375 W (early Sept). Peak 20 min had two peaks, 327 W (May) and 323 W (Aug). Goal was FTP 350 W for masters nationals in August. A June crash (broken collarbone) caused a sharp drop in all power and stalled growth, so the goal was not reached.
- Year 2: peaks within about a month in April-May: 5 s 1,015 W, 1 min 575 W, 5 min 387 W, 20 min 333 W. Masters nationals at altitude (about 8,000 ft) gave lower wattage than at sea level, so peaks understate fitness.
- Year 3: training changed to peak for nationals (moved to late June), target FTP 375 W. Mid-June peaks: 1 min 631 W, 5 min 417 W, 20 min 375 W. Peak 5 s was highest in early April, almost exactly four weeks after the winter weight training program ended (as in previous years).
- Fig 11.5 yearly values: peak 5 s 1,058 / 1,001 / 1,117 W; peak 20 min 327 / 333 / 375 W (Year 1 / 2 / 3).
- Having several years of data is valuable for athlete and coach. Altitude can obscure true gains. A one-month complete break was taken in November to recharge before winter work.
tags: type:monitoring sport:cycling metric:power level:masters subtopic:case-study subtopic:altitude subtopic:peaking
evidence: case-study
src: Ch 11 | Figs 11.2-11.5

### [TRPM-C11-004] Mean Maximal Power (MMP) Curve: reading it

- The MMP Curve plots an athlete's best power outputs across every time duration over a selected time span (e.g., best 39 s, best 56 s, best 1:38, best 5:42, best 1:15:32). It reveals the rate of wattage decay as duration increases and identifies where decay is steepest.
- Curve shape and slope changes distinguish training levels, relative strengths and weaknesses, and rider phenotypes:
  - Fig 11.6: All-rounder Cat II female cyclist with high $\text{VO}_2\text{max}$ power shows minimal power decrease from ~1 min to 8 min (flattened curve between ~300 W and ~240 W), indicating competitive strength in events requiring sustained hard $\text{VO}_2\text{max}$ efforts.
  - Fig 11.7: Sprinter demonstrates high power output from 0 to 5 s (>1,360–1,520 W, reflecting high neuromuscular power), a stable/constant slope out to ~1 min, and over 700 W maintained at 30 s.
- Semi-logarithmic plot: The MMP curve is best displayed with a logarithmic time axis because most fitness changes occur between 1 s and 30 min; on a log plot, over half the chart covers the first 3 minutes (Fig 11.8).
- Physiological transitions and slope changes: The exact durations where the curve changes slope correspond to transitions between physiological systems. In Fig 11.9:
  - Level 7 (Neuromuscular Power): ~0:20 (~860 W)
  - Level 6 (Anaerobic Capacity): ~1:25 (~500 W)
  - Level 5 ($\text{VO}_2\text{max}$): ~7:25 (~350 W)
  - Level 4 (Lactate Threshold): ~15:00 (~320 W)
  - Level 2 (Endurance): ~2:25:34 (~240 W)
  A sharp change in slope (such as at 1:25 and 7:25 in Fig 11.9) marks the transition from anaerobic capacity to $\text{VO}_2\text{max}$ to lactate threshold. A steep drop-off indicates a specific weakness that can be targeted with duration-specific training.
- Dips in the curve: Dips or higher wattage at longer durations (e.g., 350 W for 1:45 but lower recorded wattage at 1:24) occur because the curve compiles maximal efforts actually performed in rides; an athlete may have performed a maximal 1:45 hill interval without performing a maximal 1:24 effort.
- Average Power vs. Normalized Power (NP): At longer durations, average power underestimates true physiological ability due to time spent coasting/not pedaling. Beyond approximately 1 hour (or beyond the longest generally flat time trial completed), Normalized Power is a more accurate measure of ability. In Fig 11.10, Average Power is higher at short durations, but at ~7:25 (~430–440 W) the curves cross, and Normalized Power remains higher for all longer durations.
tags: type:monitoring type:principle sport:cycling metric:power system:anaerobic system:vo2max system:threshold level:all subtopic:mmp-curve
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 "Changes in Mean Maximal Power" | Figs 11.6–11.10

### [TRPM-C11-005] Using the MMP curve to confirm a training change (Jack's sprint)

Jack lost Year 1 races in close final sprints, so in Year 2 he trained to lengthen his sprint. The first 16 s of the MMP curve changed slope drastically: overall peak watts were lower but he held much higher wattage for 16+ s. In Year 3 the slope returned to the Year 1 pattern but at higher watts: higher absolute peak and held longer, so sprint ability improved. From about 2 to 30 min the slope stayed about the same each year but the curve moved upward, indicating overall fitness gains (Fig 11.11). Upward shift with unchanged slope = general fitness gain; slope change = change in the type of ability.
tags: type:monitoring type:decision-rule sport:cycling metric:power system:neuromuscular level:masters subtopic:sprint subtopic:mmp-curve
evidence: case-study
src: Ch 11 | Fig 11.11

### [TRPM-C11-006] Distribution of training levels across the season (Figs 11.12-11.15)

Principle: the levels trained shift through the season. Off-season has more Level 2 (Endurance); toward spring, more Levels 3 (Tempo) and 4 (Lactate Threshold). This is natural and shows race-specific work being added piece by piece. Power distribution charts over different periods confirm the shift or reveal a need to change training. Level 7 omitted from the figures because its time is insignificant.
Sample cyclist, % time by level:
| Level | Jan-Feb (11.12) | Mar-May (11.13) | June (11.14) | July (11.15) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 Active Recovery | 24.4% | 23.8% | 40.2% | 40.0% |
| 2 Endurance | 30.4% | 29.4% | 28.2% | 32.2% |
| 3 Tempo | 18.9% | 20.0% | 11.8% | 12.1% |
| 4 Lactate Threshold | 14.2% | 13.0% | 7.4% | 9.1% |
| 5 VO2max | 6.4% | 7.0% | 4.3% | 3.5% |
| 6 Anaerobic Capacity | 5.8% | 7.0% | 8.1% | 3.2% |
Interpretation: Jan-Feb mostly Levels 1-3 (base; Level 2 about 135-225 W). Mar-May more time in higher wattage bins, more Level 3, 5, 6. June: Level 6 rising with more criteriums, big rise in Level 1. July: racing (mostly road races and time trials) raises time at/near FTP; a big "step-down" after Level 4 is visible in the histogram, an indicator of threshold. A 2% increase in Level 4 can be significant for chronic training stress (see the "time in levels" caveat, Ch 6).
tags: type:monitoring type:periodization sport:cycling metric:power metric:load phase:base phase:build phase:race level:all subtopic:power-distribution
evidence: case-study
src: Ch 11 "Changes in the Distribution of Training Levels" | Figs 11.12-11.15

### [TRPM-C11-007] Tracking cadence changes (Fig 11.16)

- Examine cadence as well as power: how you produce watts is nearly as important as how many (Quadrant Analysis). Chart cadence over time, not just per ride.
- Case: a pro triathlete pedaled at low cadence and struggled in the Ironman run. Quadrant Analysis versus other pros showed far more time in Quadrant II (high force, low velocity), contributing to muscle fatigue in the run. Hunter hypothesized that reducing force reliance would conserve muscle glycogen and help the 26.2-mile run. She was asked to raise cadence in all training and racing from her norm of 80 rpm to 90 rpm, with a concerted conscious effort.
- Progress was charted as weekly average cadence plus mean max cadence for 30 s, 20 min and 60 min; all rose (weekly average from about 70-75 to 80-85+).
- Caution: average cadence includes time at zero cadence (not pedaling), which artificially lowers it; look for trends.
tags: type:monitoring type:decision-rule sport:triathlon metric:cadence level:advanced subtopic:quadrant-analysis subtopic:cadence
evidence: case-study
src: Ch 11 "Changes in Cadence" | Fig 11.16

### [TRPM-C11-008] Overlaying workouts/races: same time trial in two years (Fig 11.17)

Compare the same ride (e.g. time trials a year apart) to see where you improved on a course. Near the genetic ceiling, aerodynamic position and efficiency matter more than raw watts.
Time trial comparison (last 12.4 miles):
| Range | Duration | Work (kJ) | TSS (IF) | Normalized | Min Pwr | Max Pwr | Avg Pwr | Min HR | Max HR | Avg HR | Min Cad | Max Cad | Avg Cad | Min Spd | Max Spd | Avg Spd | Sample |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Year 1 | 26:24.0 | 497 | 48.5 (1.05) | 315 W (1.00) | 0 | 555 | 314 | 136 | 176 | 155 | 58 | 98 | 89 | 20.3 | 32.1 | 28.2 | 1,604 |
| Year 2 | 26:36.0 | 499 | 48.8 (1.049) | 315 W (1.01) | 0 | 516 | 313 | 132 | 184 | 155 | 49 | 97 | 88 | 19.0 | 31.3 | 28.0 | 1,620 |
Watts and cadence were nearly identical (within a watt), but Year 2 was slower. The text says 10 seconds slower, then later 20 seconds slower; the table shows 12 s difference (26:24 vs 26:36) [inconsistency in source — recorded as printed]. Cause: Year 1 had higher peak speeds (exploiting a slight downhill) and higher speed in the final 3 minutes; the athlete was judged more focused in Year 1. Lesson: with equal power, differences come from focus/pacing; 10 s can decide a win.
tags: type:monitoring type:decision-rule sport:cycling metric:power metric:pace level:all subtopic:time-trial subtopic:overlay-comparison
evidence: case-study
src: Ch 11 | Fig 11.17

### [TRPM-C11-009] Power Duration history metrics (PD History chart, Fig 11.18)

- Fitness changes (Pmax to FTP) come irregularly, not linearly: FTP can stay flat for weeks, then after a rest week appear to jump 10, 15 or 20 W, as accumulated training stress adaptation is revealed once fatigue is shed. The same holds for all energy systems. A rest week is essential, and testing after a rest week allows accurate tracking.
- Example season: modeled FTP 176 to 256 W (smallest improvement; FTP improves slowest and in smaller absolute watts); Pmax 743 to 1,169 W. FRC often has an inverse relationship with FTP improvements (FRC up, FTP may drop), though here both rose at similar rates.
- Early April FRC jumped from 10 kJ to 15 kJ with FTP flat: typically indicates a recent rest week, since FRC (with a lot of anaerobic capacity) peaks when the athlete is freshest.
- Pmax peaked mid race season and fell late: either cumulative fatigue from insufficient rest reduced high-force ability, or no all-out sprint was done late in the season.
- TTE (time to exhaustion) can precede an FTP improvement: TTE approaching or exceeding 1 hour suggests FTP may increase soon; once FTP rises, TTE resets lower until the athlete holds that power for an hour again.
- "Teeter-totter": FRC, Pmax and mFTP interact. If you test 1 and 5 min power after a rest week and raise the FRC portion without also testing 20- or 60-min power, the right side of the curve (over 5 min) appears to drop by comparison, so modeled FTP may fall after a new FRC best. FTP likely did not really drop; it is an effect of how the PDC is fitted to the MMP curve.
- Good testing after a rest week shows improvement in FRC, TTE, Pmax and mFTP.
tags: type:monitoring type:decision-rule sport:cycling metric:power system:threshold system:anaerobic level:all subtopic:pd-model subtopic:ftp-tracking subtopic:rest-week
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 "Power Duration History Metrics" | Fig 11.18

### [TRPM-C11-010] Performance Manager Chart (PMC) use for triathlon peaking

- Build three PMCs in WKO: cycling only, running only, and combined. They show which workouts/sports give best peak fitness, where the highest training stress occurs in the year, and which sport contributes most stress.
- Add mean maximal power (1-, 20-, 120-minute NP) and pace bests to judge when fitness and endurance were highest. A best 1-minute value alongside a best 20- or 60-minute value indicates both aerobic and anaerobic systems at maximum.
- For an Ironman these bests do not determine race-day effort (an Ironman is more about endurance than 20-minute wattage); what matters is TSB on race day. A high positive TSB means too fresh and lost fitness; a negative TSB can mean too fatigued.
- Holding a peak six to eight weeks is possible but not advisable. Big numbers two weeks before the event would give more confidence of peaking on time.
- Swimming is not tracked here but also affects peaking and cumulative stress.
tags: type:monitoring sport:triathlon metric:load phase:peak level:all subtopic:performance-manager
src: Ch 12 "Timing Your Fitness Peak" | Figures 12.8–12.10
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C11-011] Track: managing training load (TSS/Performance Manager)

TSS concepts remain valid for track and the Performance Manager can monitor load and plan/execute a taper (its first use was quantifying the training of a national champion pursuiter). Caution: when analyzing track workouts, delete any significant portions of the file where the rider was not actively pedaling, otherwise TSS may be artificially inflated.

## C12 Adaptations by Population / Context

### [TRPM-C12-001] Power meters and team roles

- When all team members use power meters and test regularly, coach and riders know who is riding well enough to be the protected leader and who rides in support.
- A leader knows the watts needed to stay with the lead group over a climb.
- The data build confidence: for example, if 3 of 5 riders have the fitness to win, that confidence can propel the team.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling subtopic:team-roles level:all
src: Ch 1 "Improve Interaction with Teammates"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C12-002] Power Profile for masters and juniors: no separate chart; age limitations

- Requested separate charts for masters/juniors were declined. A masters chart would shift categories so Excellent becomes World Champion; but the authors have masters riders in late 50s/early 60s racing with top amateur 20-year-olds holding over 5.0 W/kg at FTP, and world champions in their 80s competing at Cat. III/Good level.
- Standards are based on young adults and do not account for aging or development. Age-specific standards were rejected (insufficient data, difficulty correcting for known physiological changes).
- From about age 30 VO2max declines yearly: men roughly 0.5 ml/kg/min per year; women about 0.35 ml/kg/min per year.
- Muscular strength and power can generally be maintained with training until around age 50, then decline somewhat more rapidly. Different age corrections might be needed per column, but differences are unlikely to alter a profile significantly.
- Recommendation: apply Table 4.1 as is, regardless of age.
tags: type:caution sport:cycling level:masters subtopic:age subtopic:power-profile metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "Limitations and Caveats"; "The Method Behind the Data" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C12-003] Phenotypes: All-Rounder (Fig 4.1A)

Generally horizontal plot: all four values at about the same point of the rider's range. Not necessarily excelling at one thing but likely competitive across a broad range of events. Example bars: 5 s 17.61, 1 min 8.68, 5 min 5.17, FTP 4.22 W/kg (about "Very Good"). Very few athletes show this pattern at the upper end because only specialists truly excel at extreme durations; most nonelite athletes and beginning racers show it because strengths are not yet developed; with more training strengths reveal themselves.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:phenotype subtopic:all-rounder
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "All-Rounder" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C12-004] Phenotypes: Sprinter (Fig 4.1B)

Distinctly downward-sloping plot, especially between 1 min and 5 min. Example bars: 5 s 21.85 (Exceptional), 1 min 9.15 (Very Good), 5 min 4.85 (Good), FTP 3.93 (Good) W/kg. Aerobic ability is quite trainable so such a rider may become more of an all-rounder with focused training; but a sprinter already training hard for many years may have natural short-duration, high-power ability. Then focus on events favoring this, such as track racing and criteriums.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:phenotype subtopic:sprinter
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "Sprinter" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C12-005] Phenotypes: Time Trialist, Climber, Steady-State Rider (Fig 4.1C)

Distinctly upward-sloping plot (especially 1 min to 5 min, also 5 min to FTP). Typical: weak neuromuscular power and anaerobic capacity, relatively high aerobic power and especially high lactate threshold. Example bars: 5 s 15.80 (Good), 1 min 8.45, 5 min 5.28, FTP 4.79 (Excellent) W/kg.
Training caution: lots of sprint practice may reduce strength (sustainable power); small sprint improvements may not win races, and time on sprints means less time improving FTP, so the time trialist could lose fitness and perform worse in time trials.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:phenotype subtopic:time-trialist subtopic:climber
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "Time Trialist, Climber, or Steady-State Rider" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C12-006] Phenotypes: Pursuiter (Fig 4.1D)

Sharply inverted V: relatively high anaerobic capacity and high aerobic ability, suited to events like the pursuit. Example bars: 5 s 14.89 (Moderate), 1 min 9.39 (Excellent), 5 min 4.85 (Good), FTP 3.84 (Good) W/kg. Alternatively a potential all-rounder who hasn't raised lactate threshold to its highest may show the same pattern.
Caution: the opposite sharp V pattern is relatively unlikely given the expected inverse relationship between neuromuscular power and LT and positive relationship between VO2max and LT; if your data show it, verify the values truly represent your abilities.
tags: type:principle type:caution sport:cycling level:all subtopic:phenotype subtopic:pursuiter
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "Pursuiter" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C12-007] Joe Athlete reference athlete for Chapter 5 workouts

- Category III racer; FTP 290 W; threshold HR 175 bpm; max HR 200; 160 lb; about 4 W/kg at threshold; good sprinter, good anaerobic capacity; Power Profile slopes down to the right (sprinter phenotype).
- Assumes best conditions; self-selected cadence unless stated. Own wattages = percentages of own FTP (Chapter 3). Joe's ranges are sometimes rounded to nearest 5 W. iLevels are automatic in TrainingPeaks WKO4.
- Safety note: always be safe and careful; do not focus only on numbers. Full workout guide in the Appendix.
