src: Ch 5 "Workouts for Power-Based Training"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C12-008] Case study profile: Bob Rider (42, masters)

Goal: 10 races this season, esp. two-day stage race late May: 10-mile TT + 25-mile criterium Saturday, 50-mile road race with 5-mile climb Sunday. Time: 8-12 h/week incl. weekends. Weight 155 lb (70.5 kg); FTP 268 W = 3.8 W/kg; peak 5 s 1,071 W (15.2 W/kg); 1 min 613 W (8.7 W/kg); 5 min 366 W (5.2 W/kg). Power Profile: 5 s Moderate, 1 min Very Good, 5 min Very Good, FTP Good (moderately inverted V). Ectomorph (thin, more slow-twitch); phenotype pursuiter, likely time trialer. Quadrant Analysis (race file): QI 21.5%, QII 9.5%, QIII 34.5%, QIV 34.4% (thresholds 1.63 m/s, 186 N); spends most hard pedaling in QIV (high cadence, low force), lacks muscular power for QII.
Strengths: above-average fatigue resistance (Level 5), good FTP, good climber/time trialist, very good 5-min VO2max, very good short-term anaerobic power, very good 60-90 min endurance. Weaknesses: below-average explosive power (Level 7), below-average fatigue resistance (Level 4 listed), below-average muscular strength, poor on-off intense efforts; struggles with 10-20 min intense efforts.
Plan of attack: (1) focused intervals right at limit for 10-20 min per iLevel guidelines; (2) big-gear efforts and steep hills in harder gear, TT bike big-gear practice, to build force; (3) plenty of 30-60 min efforts at FTP.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:masters subtopic:case-study-bob metric:power
src: Ch 10 | Bob's plan | Figs 10.1-10.3
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C12-009] Case study profile: Jill Racer (32)

Goal: win state championship criterium (first weekend of June); races 5 years, often in the final breakaway but edged in the sprint. Time 8-12 h/week. Weight 130 lb (59.1 kg); FTP 221 W = 3.74 W/kg; best 5 s 956 W (16.21 W/kg), 1 min 452 W (7.67), 5 min 260 W (4.42). Profile relatively flat; solid all-rounder with excellent bike handling.
Quadrants: criterium race mostly QIV, training Anaerobic Capacity ride mostly QII and III (race: QI 6.5%, QII 21.6%, QIII 21.1%, QIV 50.7%; thresholds about 1.67 m/s, 186.66 N) = training does not mimic race demands; probably why Level 7 power drops abruptly at 1:30. Conclusion: fast-twitch fibers fatigue quickly; above 1 min she must raise cadence to hold wattage and power still drops.
Strengths: moderate to very good Levels 2-5, excellent breakaways, very good FTP for category, good steady-state, excellent bike handling. Weaknesses: below-average Level 7 power, extreme fatigue in shorter efforts, poor fatigue resistance in sprint, possible sprint technique issues (also investigate gear selection).
Two principles: (1) train ability to pedal at 105 rpm for 1-2 min and/or improve neuromuscular power to avoid excessive fatigue after 30 s; (2) train pacing because make-or-break efforts are hard to pace; AC-W8 trains this (reduce overall output while holding high wattage as long as possible; stop when cannot maintain, recover, repeat).
Three-pronged approach: (1) neuromuscular power: sprint and big-gear intervals at least 2x/week; (2) anaerobic capacity: at least 1 workout/week raising Level 6 wattages and repeatability; (3) FTP: at least 2 workouts/week (one may be a weekend race) with Sweet Spot and Threshold intervals 10-20 min, aim to ride away solo.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:advanced subtopic:case-study-jill metric:power
src: Ch 10 | Jill's plan | Figs 10.4-10.6
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C12-010] Case study profile: Joe TriGuy (38, 70.3)

Goal: top 10 age group at hilly 70.3; 2 years of triathlon; good swimmer/runner, needs bike leg, wants better short-hill climbing. Weight 180 lb (81.8 kg); FTP 270 W (3.3 W/kg); best 5 s 780 W, 1 min 417 W, 5 min 320 W (labels 9.49, 5.10, 3.89, 3.31 W/kg). Profile upward-sloping to right: classic triathlete/steady-state/time trialist.
Reasoning: Level 7 is far below novice but not trained since he is focused solely on triathlon (only a triathlon bike). But anaerobic capacity (30 s-2 min) and VO2max must be raised to handle many small hills and recover to previous pace; the more trained these are, the less he must use them, saving energy for the run ("counterintuitive but correct"). Also keep working FTP and stamina (maintaining power after 1 hour; ability to produce and sustain power longer than 2 hours).
Quadrant analysis (Fig 10.9; thresholds 203 N, ~1.53-1.61 m/s): race QI 19.2%, QII 1.6%, QIII 1.3%, QIV 78.0%; training QI 5.8%, QII 3.6%, QIII 20.2%, QIV 67.3%. Low force/high cadence conserves leg glycogen; many triathletes pedal too slow in big gear; QII power on the bike depletes glycogen and ruins the run.
Strengths: good steady-state, very good endurance, good FTP for size, very good swimmer and runner. Weaknesses: low neuromuscular, anaerobic capacity, VO2max power.
Plan rule: some anaerobic or VO2max work every week; intervals aimed at improving the systems, not race-specific; weekend overdistance up to 4-5 h on a few weekends to run goal pace after hard bike leg.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:triathlon level:intermediate subtopic:case-study-joe metric:power
src: Ch 10 | Joe's plan | Figs 10.7-10.9
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C12-011] Case study profile: Bill Masters (58, gran fondo)

Goal: 80-mile (128 km) gran fondo, three climbs (est. 45, 30, 18 min), at least 5 h; ride climbs at solid Tempo pace with buddies; worried about endurance. Time 6-9 h/week, max ~3 h on a weekend, but a long ride twice a month with advance planning. Weight 150 lb (68.2 kg); FTP 235 W (3.45 W/kg); peak 5 s 803 W (11.8), 1 min 381 W (5.6), 5 min 261 W (3.8). Profile slopes modestly right: steady state/climber/time trialist; 5-min and 20-min power strengths; sprint/1-min not enough for all-rounder; similar to Joe but better sprint.
Quadrant (Gavia climb): QII 40.4%, QIII 53.0%, QI 0.0%, QIV 0.5% (entire workout QII 17.5%, QIII 34.3%, QI 0.1%, QIV 0.6%; thresholds 141 N, 1.63 m/s). Applies force at low cadence: fine for a single climb or climb at end of ride, but too taxing for multi-climb ride; work on climbing cadence in upper 70s and 80s to shift load from muscular to cardiovascular system.
WAC score: peak 74 (age-adjusted) at 22:48 = 74% of world champion best wattage for same duration; drops 43% at 5 hours = endurance concern. WAC (Williams, Allen, Coggan) score: scores each duration vs world's best, adjusted for age; a variation on Power Profile.
Strengths: good Level 5 and 4 for category/age; good climber with ability to use more force; solid handling. Weaknesses: poor Level 7 and 6; lackluster sprints due to poor Level 2 and 3 power. Also to become more all-rounder, address Level 6 and 7.
Endurance approach: two rides/month of 5+ h, ride hard and finish exhausted (deep fatigue gives adaptation); include substantial climbing to prepare low back, neck, legs; climbs at Sweet Spot or FTP for 10, 15, 20+ min boost FTP.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:cycling level:masters subtopic:case-study-bill metric:power
src: Ch 10 | Bill's plan | Figs 10.10-10.12
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C12-012] Training to the demands of the event (Quadrant Analysis, mountain-bike example)

- Train for event demands. Example: 20 criterium laps each with a 20-second hill: do 20 repeats of 20 s. Compare the race Quadrant Analysis chart with a typical training day. Ignoring neuromuscular demands (hills, accelerations) misses a critical component.
- Quadrants: I high force/high cadence; II high force/low cadence; III low force/low cadence; IV low force/high cadence.
- Mountain biker, training ride (Figs 13.4, 13.6): subthreshold work, almost all points just below the FTP line, mostly Quadrants III and IV. It stressed metabolic fitness but not Type II (fast-twitch) fibers. Rider notes: steep climb over 20%, 350 W for the first 2 min on the hill, 32:27 gear not enough.
- Race file (Figs 13.5, 13.7): much more stochastic. A "shotgun blast" distribution, mostly Quadrant III with much Quadrant II. Time over threshold was mostly spent in Quadrant II (pushing over obstacles, traction on steep slopes, momentum). Reference lines on the charts: CPV and AEPF at 300 W and 88 rpm.
- Prescription: (1) workouts focused on Quadrant II (large force at low cadence with high cardiovascular strain); (2) more variability in rides, e.g., race-like efforts off-road, time trials on a technical loop, micro-bursts in a big gear, or motorpacing.
tags: type:decision-rule type:methodology sport:cycling level:intermediate subtopic:quadrant-analysis subtopic:mountain-bike subtopic:specificity metric:power metric:cadence
src: Ch 13, Figs 13.4-13.7
evidence: case-study

### [TRPM-C12-013] Cyclocross: demands and how to read power files

- Demands: quick bursts over small obstacles; leaping off the bike and running while carrying it for sections up to 30 seconds; maintaining pace at FTP (and above) for 40 to 75 minutes.
- Cyclocross power files typically average 20 to 40 watts below the athlete's actual FTP. Reasons: much downtime (coasting down technical hills, running with the bike) and poor traction (mud, sandpits) which skews power down. Take this into account when reviewing files.
- Exact muscular demands are hard to determine because of running/coasting sections. Power is not recorded while dismounting, running and remounting, though this adds physiological and technical demand.
- Quadrant Analysis: largely Quadrant II (slow pedaling, higher force), but Quadrants III and IV also heavily involved. Figure 14.1 (typical race): QI 3.9%, QII 31.3%, QIII 29.2%, QIV 35.5% (about equal work in QII, III, IV); threshold lines about AEPF 121.64 N and CPV 1.65 m/s; power curves 203 W, threshold 185 W, 166 W.
- File resembles a criterium (stochastic spikes, easily discernible laps, race-winning efforts) but differs in power bursts, reduced power periods in each lap, and total training stress.
- Key to identify: number of watts above FTP and length of each effort, i.e. how many matches the racer burns. A cyclocross match differs: the racer is usually already at FTP when a hard effort is needed, so matches are like bursts of flames on an already raging fire. Identifying these flames and their intensity allows specific training. (Figure 14.2: a hard sustained effort with harder bursts on top; power spikes up to about 500 W.)
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:cyclocross subtopic:quadrant-analysis subtopic:matches metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Cyclocross" | Figs 14.1, 14.2

### [TRPM-C12-014] Track cycling: how a power meter is (and is not) used

Why useful: short distances/durations mean tiny margins; elite races often TT format (less tactics, more physical ability); even indoors, environmental conditions (e.g. air density) change TT time while power stays constant.
Limits: little use for pacing because (1) track efforts are often short, high-intensity, unpaced; (2) high speed and frequent turns make the display hard to read, even in longer events like individual pursuit. Except endurance events (points race, Madison), most track races are too short for Normalized Power to be interpretable.
Valuable for: determining precise event demands, optimizing training, position, equipment and race strategy.
tags: type:principle sport:cycling level:all subtopic:track metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Track Cycling" | n/a

## C13 Contraindications, Cautions & Safety Limits

### [TRPM-C13-001] Noncircular chainrings inflate reported power

- WHAT: Noncircular (oval) chainrings used with power meters measuring angular velocity at the crank or pedals.
- WHY: Most power meters report crank angular velocity once per rpm and assume it remains constant across one entire revolution. Noncircular chainrings speed the crank through the non-power portion of the pedal stroke (top and bottom dead centers) within a single rpm to return quickly to the power stroke (0–180 degrees). Because of this intra-revolution speed increase, calculated power output is inflated by up to 10–20 watts on average (depending on degree of ovality), resulting in false power reporting. The authors take no position on whether oval rings improve actual performance or save energy.
- LIMIT/EXCEPTION: The text states: "Using a power meter that measures power at the hub or in the pedals will eliminate this problem" [Inconsistency in source: text earlier states meters measuring angular velocity at crank or pedals show inflated power, but then concludes hub or pedal meters eliminate the problem].
tags: type:caution sport:cycling level:all subtopic:noncircular-chainrings metric:power
src: Ch 2 sidebar "Do Noncircular Chainrings Show a False Power Increase?"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C13-002] Cautions, contraindications and safety limits from Chapter 7

- **Deliberate pulling up on pedals:**
  - WHAT: Consciously pulling up on the pedals during the upstroke to equalize bilateral power or increase efficiency.
  - LIMIT: Avoid completely. Elite cyclists actually pull up slightly less than non-elites and focus on minimizing absorbed power.
  - WHO: All cyclists.
  - WHY: Deliberately pulling up increases metabolic energy cost, reduces pedaling efficiency, and alters an individual's natural pedaling pattern detrimentally.
  - EXCEPTION: Cyclists who have been riding seriously for 3+ years and have seated GPA > 35 W may intentionally drive the knee toward the handlebar on the upstroke while lightly pointing the toe downward.
- **Relying on standard ANT+ bilateral metrics:**
  - WHAT: Using ANT+ Pedaling Smoothness, Torque Effectiveness, and standard Power Balance as true indicators of asymmetry.
  - LIMIT: Avoid treating as ground truth; evaluate via Gross Power Released (GPR) and Gross Power Absorbed (GPA) instead.
  - WHO: Power-meter users analyzing bilateral data.
  - WHY: ANT+ metrics compare average power per leg rather than separating released power on the downstroke from absorbed power on the upstroke, which can falsely identify leg dominance.
  - EXCEPTION: None.
- **Sudden FTP increases based on single-event IF:**
  - WHAT: Automatically raising software FTP by large increments (e.g., 20 W) based on a high IF (> 1.05) in a 1-hour race.
  - LIMIT: Limit immediate upward software adjustment to half the observed jump (e.g., 10 W) until confirmed.
  - WHO: Cyclists tracking threshold changes via race files.
  - WHY: Avoid setting unrealistically high training zones that induce severe overreaching.
  - EXCEPTION: Must be confirmed by formal field or laboratory testing as soon as possible before full adjustment.
- **Heavy slow-speed resistance training for cycling power:**
  - WHAT: Lifting heavy weights at slow speeds to increase cycling power.
  - LIMIT: Do not rely on as a primary method to increase on-the-bike maximal power.
  - WHO: Trained endurance cyclists.
  - WHY: Specificity principle; strength per se rarely limits cycling power. Heavy slow lifting primarily builds force at slow velocities and when carried to extremes can reduce maximal muscle shortening velocity, impairing power at high contraction speeds.
  - EXCEPTION: Standing starts in BMX and track sprinting, where CPV starts at zero and strength is truly limiting.
- **Single-session training stress limits:**
  - WHAT: Workouts or races accumulating very high TSS (> 450).
  - LIMIT: Must be followed by several days of recovery / rest days.
  - WHO: All cyclists (especially amateur and developing riders).
  - WHY: Residual fatigue lasts for several days; inadequate recovery causes severe overtraining.
  - EXCEPTION: Highly adapted grand tour riders (e.g., Peter Sagan) who can tolerate 300–400 TSS daily across stage races.
tags: type:caution sport:cycling metric:power metric:load subtopic:cautions level:all
src: Ch 7

### [TRPM-C13-003] Cautions: overreaching, ramp rate, illness, data gaps

- Avoid CTL ramp >7 TSS/day per week for more than 4 weeks in a row (overreaching spiral); >7 acceptable 1 week, possibly 2.
- Newer cyclists: increase CTL more carefully to prevent injury and non-functional overreaching.
- ATL above CTL for too long = non-functional overreaching; prolonged = overtraining syndrome (months to a year off bike). Exception: short bursts (<2 weeks, e.g., camp) per Table 9.2 followed by a rest week.
- Sudden load increases or very hard races with a fast CTL ramp may compromise immunity (illness case).
- Long-term load >150 TSS/day not sustainable.
- >10% missing files in a block: interpret PMC cautiously. Inaccurate FTP magnifies errors.
tags: type:caution sport:cycling level:all subtopic:overtraining
src: Ch 9
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C13-004] Cautions in Ch 10 plans

- Stacking missed workouts into few days: avoid; risk illness or injury (see C09 rule).
- Rest weeks: do not overdo; Week 4/rest-week riding under 62% FTP for Jill; overdoing in pre-event week may compromise finishing position.
- Joe's kitchen-sink: pushing to the point of muscle twitch/threatened cramp is described as maximal; exhaust muscular endurance without damaging muscles.
- Skip Jill's AC-W3 workout if first 2-min interval cannot reach 120% of FTP (ride easy 30 more min).
- Do not make bike repairs two days before an event.
tags: type:caution sport:cycling level:all subtopic:overtraining-risk
src: Ch 10
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C13-005] Ironman bike leg TSS and Intensity Factor upper limits

- WHAT: Total Training Stress Score (TSS) and Intensity Factor (IF) during the bike leg of an Ironman-distance triathlon.
- LIMIT: Maximum budget of 300 TSS; realistic recommended target is 280 TSS (IF ~0.68 for a 6-hour split). Upper limit of IF is 0.76 (Table 12.1 range: 0.70–0.76, average power 68–78% of FTP).
- WHO: Age-group and amateur triathletes.
- WHY: Pushing past 300 TSS or riding above 0.75–0.76 IF places the athlete in the "danger zone," depleting muscle glycogen prematurely and causing failure on the marathon run leg (walking or DNF, which is the #1 cause of DNFs in triathlons).
- EXCEPTION: Elite and professional triathletes who train 30+ hours per week and possess genetic advantages can tolerate up to ~390 TSS at an IF of 0.83 while still running at personal-record pace.
tags: type:caution sport:triathlon metric:load,power level:all subtopic:ironman-pacing-limits
src: Ch 12 "The Mathematical Approach to Pacing" | "Racing Triathlons"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C13-006] Track data caution: remove non-pedaling segments; overlapping wheels

- Delete non-pedaling portions of track files before computing TSS (otherwise inflated).
- Test aero only on an empty/near-empty track (safety).
- Overlapping wheels during a team pursuit exchange can be dangerous; riders should aim for a perfect exchange regardless of power requirements.

## C14 Formulas & Calculations

### [TRPM-C14-001] Kilojoules to kilocalories (energy expenditure)

- Power meters report work in joules (J) and kilojoules (kJ), a measure of work performed (external work).
- 1 kilocalorie (large Calorie, capital C) = 1,000 small calories. By definition, 4.184 kJ per Calorie.
- Simply dividing kJ by 4.184 is not correct, because the meter measures external work, not the energy needed to do it. Most energy becomes waste heat.
- Thermodynamic (gross) efficiency in cycling for most trained cyclists is about 20–25 percent.
- Correct procedure: kJ ÷ 4.184, then multiply by 4 (25% efficiency) or 5 (20% efficiency).
- These factors cancel out, so total work in kJ can be taken as an estimate of energy expenditure in kcal (Calories).
- The relationship is not exactly one to one, but the error is not worth worrying about. Individual efficiency can only be determined in a lab and varies with intensity, duration, environment and other factors.
- Rule: kcal expended ≈ kJ of work.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:kilojoule-conversion level:all
src: Ch 1 sidebar "What Is a Kilojoule?"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C14-002] Power = Force × Speed (PowerPod basis)

Power = Force × Speed. The PowerPod flips the equation: it calculates the force and speed needed to create power from opposing forces. Later chapters expand on it.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling level:all subtopic:power-equation metric:power
src: Ch 2 "Opposing Forces Method"

### [TRPM-C14-003] FTP from the 20-minute test (subtract 5%)

Formula: FTP = 20-min average power − (20-min average power × 0.05).
Worked example: 305 W average; 305 × 0.05 = 15.25; 305 − 15.25 = 290 W. FTP estimated 290 W.
Reasoning: FTP is the highest average power maintainable in quasi-steady state, close to 60 minutes. A 60-minute test is hard to focus on and unpleasant, so 20 minutes gives better compliance and test quality. But 20 minutes incorporates more anaerobic capacity, skewing wattage ~5% over a 60-minute effort; subtracting 5% approximates 60-minute power.
Individual adjustment: riders with higher anaerobic capacity may need to subtract 7 or more percent; purely aerobic riders may need to subtract only 2–3 percent. This is only an estimate, another point of triangulation.
tags: type:formula level:all subtopic:ftp-calculation sport:cycling system:threshold metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Testing Protocol

### [TRPM-C14-004] Power-to-weight ratio (W/kg) calculation

Power-to-weight ratio = watts ÷ body mass in kg. The Power Profile chart is in W/kg (standard scientific measure).
- Pounds to kg: divide pounds by 2.2 (165 lb = 75 kg).
- Worked example: 423 W for 5 min ÷ 75 kg = 5.64 W/kg.
- Equivalence example: a 200 lb rider at 350 W and a 125 lb rider at 218 W both equal 3.85 W/kg and can ride side by side uphill.
Power-to-weight drives performance: goal is to be as light as possible while producing the highest possible power; the trick is finding the weight that allows the most watts.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling level:all subtopic:power-to-weight metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 "Power-to-Weight Ratio" | no page markers

### [TRPM-C14-005] Normalized Power calculation

1. Start at the beginning of the data and calculate a 30-second rolling average of power.
2. Raise the values from step 1 to the fourth power.
3. Average all values from step 2.
4. Take the fourth root of the result of step 3.
Rationale: (1) physiological responses to rapid intensity changes are not instantaneous but follow a predictable time course; (2) glycogen use, lactate production, stress hormones etc. are curvilinearly, not linearly, related to intensity.
Use: hard criterium or road race of about 1 h often has NP similar to what a rider averages in a flat 40 km TT, so mass-start NP can give an initial estimate of threshold power. Criteriums have lower average power than equally hard road races (soft-pedaling/coasting) but similar NP.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling metric:power subtopic:normalized-power level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Normalized Power"

### [TRPM-C14-006] VI, IF and TSS formulas with examples

- VI = NP / average power. Figure 7.1 mass-start road race segment: NP 357 W, average 319 W, VI 1.12, duration 15:02, work 288 kJ, TSS 29.3, HR avg 161 bpm (145-178), cadence avg 92 rpm, speed avg 26.2 mph, max power 1,009 W, distance 6.569 mi. Figure 7.2 steady climb: NP 304 W, average 300 W, VI 1.02, duration 21:14, work 382 kJ, TSS 26.7, HR avg 148, cadence avg 58 rpm, torque avg 442 lb-in, distance 3.818 mi.
- IF = NP / FTP. Example: NP 210 W with FTP 280 W gives IF 0.75; same ride later with FTP 300 W gives IF 0.70.
- TSS = [(s × W × IF) ÷ (FTP × 3,600)] × 100, where s = duration in seconds, W = Normalized Power in watts, IF = Intensity Factor, FTP = functional threshold power, 3,600 = seconds in 1 hour.
- Reference: 1 h at FTP = 100 TSS and IF 1.0. 200 TSS = two 40 km TTs worth. 100 TSS could also be a 2-h ride at IF 0.71.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling metric:load metric:power subtopic:tss subtopic:intensity-factor subtopic:variability-index level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 | Figs 7.1, 7.2

### [TRPM-C14-007] AEPF and CPV formulas

- AEPF = (P × 60) ÷ (C × 2 × π × CL), where AEPF = average effective pedal force (N, both legs, averaged over 360°, tangential to crank); P = power (W); C = cadence (rpm); CL = crank length (m).
- CPV = (C × CL × 2 × π) ÷ 60, where CPV = circumferential pedal velocity (m/s); C = cadence (rpm); CL = crank length (m).
tags: type:formula sport:cycling metric:power metric:cadence subtopic:aepf subtopic:cpv level:all
src: Ch 7

### [TRPM-C14-008] FRC work-above-FTP calculation

- 1 W = 1 J/s. FRC in joules is the absolute work above FTP regardless of time. Power above FTP = FRC / duration; sustainable power = FTP + FRC/duration.
- Worked example (FRC = 20 kJ = 20,000 J; FTP 300 W):
  - 30 s: 20,000/30 = 666.7 W above FTP.
  - 120 s: 20,000/120 = 166.7 W above FTP, so 300 + 167 = 467 W.
  - 180 s: 111 W above FTP, so 300 + 111 = 411 W.
  - 30 min: only 11 W added (311 W for 30 min versus 300 W for 60 min).
- Implication: for efforts longer than a couple of minutes, training to raise FTP gives better return than training FRC.
tags: type:formula metric:power system:anaerobic level:all subtopic:frc subtopic:work-above-ftp
src: Ch 8 "FRC"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C14-009] Banister impulse-response equation

$$p_t = p_0 + k_a \sum_{s=0}^{t-1} e^{-(t-s)/\tau_a} w_s - k_f \sum_{s=0}^{t-1} e^{-(t-s)/\tau_f} w_s$$

Variables:
- $p_t$ = performance at any time $t$
- $p_0$ = initial performance
- $k_a, k_f$ = gain or multiplier terms relating to the magnitudes of the positive adaptive and negative fatigue effects (and also help in converting the units used to quantify training to the units used to quantify performance)
- $\tau_a, \tau_f$ = time constants describing the rate of decay of the positive adaptive and negative fatigue effects
- $w_s$ = daily training dose

Model constraints and fitting:
- Four adjustable parameters ($k_a, k_f, \tau_a, \tau_f$), constrained such that $k_a < k_f$ and $\tau_a < \tau_f$ [Likely typo in source — recorded as printed: text states $\tau_a$ is less than $\tau_f$, whereas in the impulse-response model fatigue decays faster than fitness, so $\tau_f < \tau_a$].
- Best-fit solution determined iteratively by repeatedly measuring both daily training dose and resulting performance, then adjusting parameter values for closest correspondence between predicted and actual performance.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling level:all subtopic:impulse-response
src: Ch 9 sidebar "Banister's Impulse-Response Model"
evidence: study (Banister 1975)

### [TRPM-C14-010] Performance Manager formulas

- CTL = exponentially weighted moving average of daily TSS, time constant default 42 days.
- ATL = exponentially weighted moving average of daily TSS, time constant default 7 days.
- TSB = CTL – ATL.
- TSS = duration (hours) × IF² × 100; IF = NP ÷ FTP.
- Example: FTP 240 vs 250 W gives 8% higher TSS.
- Form = Fitness + Freshness.
tags: type:formula sport:cycling level:all subtopic:pmc-formulas
src: Ch 9
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C14-011] TSS-budget pacing method (Endurance Nation)

- Source: Rich Strauss and Patrick McCrann, Endurance Nation. Maximum TSS budget for an Ironman-distance event: 300 TSS. 300 is the danger zone for not being able to run the whole race; a more realistic budget is 280. Predict or set a goal finish time, then solve for IF and wattage.
- Steps:
  1. TSS per hour = total TSS ÷ race hours (280 ÷ 6 = 46.7).
  2. TSS per hour = IF² × 100, so IF² = 46.7 ÷ 100 = 0.47, IF = √0.47 = 0.68.
  3. IF = Average NP ÷ FTP, so Average NP = IF × FTP.
- Worked example: 6 hours, FTP 275 W. At 300 TSS, IF = 0.71. At 280 TSS, IF = 0.68, average NP = 0.68 × 275 = 187 W. Goal is 187 W for the Ironman-distance race.
- Guidelines are for amateurs. Pros can ride higher IF and TSS and still run PR pace (some create nearly 390 TSS at IF 0.83); they train 30+ hours a week and often have genetic advantages.
tags: type:formula sport:triathlon metric:power,load level:all subtopic:tss-budget
src: Ch 12 "The Mathematical Approach to Pacing"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C14-012] rTSS (Running Training Stress Score) and run vs bike equivalence

- Developed by Dr. Stephen McGregor, based on Coggan's TSS. It is scaled so that 100 TSS points on the bike equal the same training stress as 100 on the run.
- Running carries extra musculoskeletal risk from foot impact, so an hour at functional threshold pace (FTp, lowercase p for pace) is more stress than an hour at FTP on the bike and needs more recovery.
- Equivalence: roughly 45 minutes of running at FTp, or a flat-out effort for 15 kilometers, equals 1 hour at FTP on the bike.
- Power software can import GPS run data with bike power data.
tags: type:formula sport:triathlon metric:load,pace level:all subtopic:rtss
src: Ch 12 "Timing Your Fitness Peak"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C14-013] CdA and Crr determination by speed-power regression

Curvilinear relationship between power and speed on a flat or grade-corrected course:
$$Y = aX + bX^3$$

Linear transformation:
$$\frac{Y}{X} = a + bX^2$$

Variables:
- $Y$ = steady-state power output corrected for changes in kinetic energy (variations in start vs. finish speed) and potential energy (slope/grade), in watts ($\text{W}$).
- $X$ = ground speed, in meters per second ($\text{m/s}$), tested across 6 to 9 runs ranging from ~5 to 15 $\text{m/s}$.
- $a$ = constant representing rolling resistance force, in newtons ($\text{N}$).
- $b$ = constant proportional to one-half air density times effective frontal area:
  $$b \approx 0.5 \cdot \rho \cdot C_\text{d}A$$
- $\rho$ = air density, in grams per liter ($\text{g/L}$), calculated from ambient barometric pressure, temperature, and humidity at the time of testing.
- $C_\text{d}A$ = effective frontal area, in square meters ($\text{m}^2$).

Graphical derivation:
- Plotting $\frac{Y}{X}$ (watts per $\text{m/s}$, which equals total drag force in $\text{N}$) on the y-axis against $X^2$ ($\text{m}^2/\text{s}^2$) on the x-axis produces a linear regression line.
- Y-intercept = rolling resistance force ($a$, in $\text{N}$).
- Slope = $b = 0.5 \cdot \rho \cdot C_\text{d}A$.
- Aerodynamic drag is isolated as:
  $$C_\text{d}A = \frac{2 \cdot \text{slope}}{\rho}$$
tags: type:formula sport:cycling level:advanced subtopic:cda subtopic:aero-testing metric:power
src: Ch 13 "Regression Method"
evidence: author-experience

## C15 Evidence & Key References

### [TRPM-C15-001] Authors' credentials and origin of the power-training schema

- Andrew Coggan, PhD (exercise physiologist): began working with ergometers in early 1980s in his lab, creating testing protocols using specific workloads (wattage); learned how carbohydrates work in the body and how blood lactate affects performance; wrote numerous scientific papers. Competitive cyclist who used ergometers for own training. With a less-expensive mobile power meter in the mid-to-late 1990s he collected data outdoors, knowing the tool could quantify demands of racing, improve pacing and track fitness changes. Because the tool gave many cyclists more information than they could handle, he created a schema for training with a power meter and taught USA Cycling coaches. 2006 USA Cycling Sport Science Award; one of three finalists for the US Olympic Committee's Doc Councilman Award.
- Hunter Allen: former pro cyclist, elite coach, owner of Peaks Coaching Group; coaching endurance athletes since 1995; worked with early power-meter adopters in the late 1990s. In 2003 Allen, Coggan and Kevin Williams developed TrainingPeaks WKO Software (analyze workouts, compare race data, track progress); no longer an owner of TrainingPeaks. Analyzed thousands of power files, coached hundreds of athletes with power; taught over 5,000 coaches and athletes in over 20 countries. Technical coach to the USA BMX Olympic team in 2008; has coached world and national champions and elite riders in the Olympics, Tour de France and Ironman world championships.
- Stephen McGregor, PhD: early adopter; director of the Applied Physiology Laboratory and the Sport Performance Technology Laboratory at Eastern Michigan University. USA Cycling instructor since 2005 (physiology, general training, power training); co-lead instructor for USA Cycling Level 1 Elite coaching certification since 2013. Co-author of The Runner's Edge ebook, where he developed run Training Stress Score (rTSS) and normalized graded pace (NGP). Coaches for Peaks Coaching Group (national and world champions in road cycling, Olympians); 2016 Order of Ikkos medal as coach of an Olympic track cycling medalist. Former elite cyclist, now masters-level athlete.
tags: type:evidence level:all subtopic:author-credentials subtopic:authority
src: Introduction "How we got our start"
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C15-002] Evidence cited in Chapter 7

- Banister TRIMP studies: heart-rate-based training impulse models predicting training-induced performance changes; inspiration for TSS.
- Vollestad et al. 1985 (Acta Physiol Scand 125:395): fiber-type recruitment and glycogen depletion vs intensity.
- New Zealand laboratory study: low-cadence strength-endurance training did not increase leg extensor size or maximal force.
- Studies on pedaling: elite cyclists pull up less; deliberate pulling up is costlier; altering pedal force pattern reduces metabolic efficiency (notes 2-4).
- Note 1: typical left-right leg-strength discrepancy 5%.
tags: type:evidence sport:cycling level:all
src: Ch 7

### [TRPM-C15-003] Evidence relied on in Ch 9

- Banister 1975 impulse-response model: supports modeling fitness/fatigue as opposing exponential effects; explains >70% (often >90%) of day-to-day performance variation.
- Authors' survey of ~200 athletes: TSB at personal-best power across durations (Figs 9.6–9.8).
- Authors' experience with elite amateur track, masters MTB marathon and pro road racers: optimal CTL 100–150; ramp rates 3–7 TSS/day/week; Tour de France power data.
- Bompa 1968 periodization principles; Friel, The Cyclist's Training Bible.
tags: type:evidence sport:cycling level:all
src: Ch 9
evidence: author-experience

### [TRPM-C15-004] References cited for Chapter 7 (Beyond Average Power)

1. Carpes, Mota, Faria, "On the Bilateral Symmetry during Running and Cycling: A Review Considering Leg Preference," Physical Therapy in Sport 11 (2010): 136–142.
2. Coyle, E.F. et al., "Physiological and Biomechanical Factors Associated with Elite Endurance-Cycling Performance," Med Sci Sports Exerc 23(1) (1991): 93–107.
3. Edwards, L.M. et al., "Whole-Body Efficiency Is Negatively Correlated with Minimum Torque per Duty Cycle in Trained Cyclists," J Sports Sciences 27(4) (2009): 319–325.
4. Korff, T. et al., "Effect of Pedaling Technique on Mechanical Effectiveness and Efficiency in Cyclists," Med Sci Sports Exerc 39(6) (2007): 991–995.
(The book does not state in this unit what each supports; topics suggested by titles: left-right symmetry, elite cycling physiology, efficiency and torque, pedaling technique.)

4. Williams et al., "Genes to predict VO2max trainability: A systematic review," BMC Genomics 18, Suppl. 8 (2017): 83.

## C16 Notable Statements

### [TRPM-C16-001] Notable statements, Chapter 1

- "Wattage is the key to knowing when you truly need a rest day."
- "Training is testing; testing is training."
- "A power meter doesn't lie, and sometimes, the truth can be tough to face!"
- "If your training and cycling are to change (that is, improve), then you must be willing to change first."
tags: type:philosophy sport:cycling level:all subtopic:quotes
src: Ch 1

### [TRPM-C16-002] Notable Quotes on FTP, Pacing, and Phenotypes (Ch 8)

- "FTP represents an intensity that can be sustained for between perhaps 30 and 70 minutes, with more trained subjects tending to fall toward the upper end of this range."
- "FTP is the highest power a rider can maintain in a quasi-steady-state without fatiguing." [First edition definition cited]
- "There’s an old adage: If you want to win a race, get into a breakaway with a time trialist, because you know you’re going to win the sprint."
- "It becomes clear that there are no truly discrete efforts that rely on one singular physiological system."
tags: type:principle sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:notable-quotes
src: Ch 8 "Shifting to the Power Duration Model"

### [TRPM-C16-003] Notable statements Ch 9

- "Form equals fitness plus freshness."
- "The best predictor of performance is performance itself."
- "An athlete should do the least amount of the most specific training that brings continual improvement." (Joe Friel, quoted)
- "In conclusion, the more anaerobic the event, the more important it is to be fresh, and the more aerobic the event, the more important it is to be fit."
tags: type:philosophy sport:cycling level:all
src: Ch 9

### [TRPM-C16-004] A minimalist, zen approach to power data

"The simple collection of data is one of the most zen ideas about training with power. Ride, collect data, do nothing extra. Even though this motto may sound simplistic, it also brings out a concept some cyclists may find helpful: a minimalist approach to training with a power meter."
tags: type:philosophy sport:cycling metric:power level:all subtopic:minimalism
evidence: unsupported
src: Ch 11 closing

### [TRPM-C16-005] Notable Statements: Chapter 12 (Triathlon)

- "Pacing plays a leading role in triathlon; in fact, it’s the crux of the entire game."
- "Power calibrates perceived exertion, and perceived exertion modulates power." (Charles Howe)
- "Most triathletes do not understand how easy it is to ride too fast on the bike leg; it’s the number one cause of DNFs in triathlons. The difference between a well-paced bike leg and a poorly paced one can be as little as 15 watts (normalized) for an average in the event."
- "Pedaling as smoothly as possible is key in triathlon. By keeping the Normalized Power and average power as close to the same as you can, you save valuable energy for the run."
tags: type:philosophy sport:triathlon metric:power level:all subtopic:notable-statements
src: Ch 12 "A Powerful Triathlete"

## C17 Validity Notes

### [TRPM-C17-001] Validity Notes

[VALIDITY NOTE — not from source] Statement: "Power meters... ANT+ limits data to four messages per second" and product-landscape statements (Ch 2). What changed: device and protocol capabilities have evolved since 2019 (e.g. newer head units and BLE/ANT+ specs, many more power meters); consult current product reviews, as the authors themselves advise.

[VALIDITY NOTE — not from source] Statement: "VO2max declines about 0.5 ml/kg/min per year for men, 0.35 for women starting around age 30" (Ch 4). What changed: rates of decline vary across studies and with training; this should be treated as an approximation rather than a fixed figure. Confidence moderate.

No other validity notes. Training-method claims are practice-based and not guideline-governed.
tags: type:caution level:all subtopic:validity
src: synthesis
